# 371 Days of AI Research Papers — A Complete YouTube Curriculum

**One paper per day. 371 lessons. 53 weeks. Beginner → frontier.**

---

## How this document works

Every day is one episode. Each entry gives you:

| Field | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `Dxx · Title` | Day number and the paper |
| Authors, venue, year | Enough to find it in 5 seconds on arXiv / Semantic Scholar |
| Runtime | Target video length. Stick to it — drift is what kills 365-day series |
| Level | **L1** beginner · **L2** intermediate · **L3** advanced · **L4** research-frontier |
| Backtick timestamps | The actual chapter markers you paste into your YouTube description |
| ▸ Nail these | The specific technical points that *must* appear or the episode is shallow |

**On the two meanings of "timeline":** you get both. The backtick markers are the *in-video* timeline (YouTube auto-generates chapters from these). The month/week headers are the *calendar* timeline. Publish daily and you finish in 53 weeks.

**A note on arXiv IDs:** included where they're stable and I'm confident. Verify every ID before you put it on screen — a wrong number in a thumbnail is a correction video you don't want to make.

---

## Production system (read this before Day 1)

**The 6-block episode skeleton.** Every episode, no exceptions. Viewers learn the shape and stop bouncing.

1. **Cold open (0:00–2:00)** — the one result, plot, or failure that makes this paper matter. No "hey guys."
2. **Problem state (2:00–8:00)** — what the field could *not* do the day before this paper.
3. **The method (8:00–~60%)** — equations on screen, derived not displayed. This is the episode.
4. **Evidence (~60%–80%)** — the actual tables. Read the numbers out loud. Name the baseline.
5. **Limits & what broke (80%–92%)** — failed replications, the ablation they buried, what superseded it.
6. **Bridge (92%–100%)** — one sentence connecting to tomorrow. This is your retention mechanic.

**Weekly rhythm.** Mon–Fri: core papers. Sat: heavier/longer paper. Sun: lighter or historical paper — use it as your buffer day when you fall behind.

**Recording batches.** Batch 7 episodes per session. Record Sunday, edit Monday, schedule the week. Trying to record daily fails around Day 40; every 365-day channel that died, died there.

**Titles.** `Day 47 — BatchNorm: The Paper Whose Explanation Was Wrong`. Day number first for playlist ordering, paper name second, tension third.

**Prerequisite chain.** Days marked `⟵ Dxx` assume that episode. Pin it in the description.

**Equipment floor.** Screen recording + a tablet/whiteboard for derivations. Talking head is optional; the math is not.

---

# MONTH 1 — Foundations: The Papers Everything Rests On
### Days 1–30 · Level 1 · No deep learning yet

The temptation is to open with AlexNet. Don't. Half of what confuses people about modern papers is that they never learned what a loss surface, a decision boundary, or a generalization bound actually is. This month buys you the vocabulary for the next eleven.

---

**D1 · Computing Machinery and Intelligence** — Turing, *Mind* 1950 · 35 min · L1
`0:00` The question Turing refuses to answer ("can machines think?") and what he swaps it for · `6:00` The Imitation Game as actually described — three players, not two · `14:00` The nine objections, walked one by one · `26:00` The "child machine" passage: Turing invents machine learning in two paragraphs · `31:00` What the Loebner Prize got wrong about all of this
▸ **Nail these:** the original game has a man and a woman before it has a machine; the Theological and "Lady Lovelace" objections; Turing's 2000-era prediction (30% fool rate, 5 min) and why it's not "the Turing Test" as popularly stated; the learning-machine + education analogy.

**D2 · A Mathematical Theory of Communication** — Shannon, *Bell System Tech J.* 1948 · 55 min · L1
`0:00` Why every loss function you'll meet this year is a log · `5:00` The channel diagram; "meaning is irrelevant to the engineering problem" · `12:00` Deriving H = −Σ p log p from the three axioms · `26:00` Entropy of English, the redundancy estimate, and why that predicts tokenizers · `38:00` Channel capacity and the noisy-channel coding theorem · `50:00` Straight line from here to cross-entropy loss
▸ **Nail these:** the uniqueness derivation (continuity, monotonicity, decomposability); bits vs nats; conditional entropy and mutual information defined here; KL is *not* in this paper — say so; why perplexity = 2^H.

**D3 · A Logical Calculus of the Ideas Immanent in Nervous Activity** — McCulloch & Pitts, 1943 · 30 min · L1
`0:00` A neuron as a logic gate · `4:00` The five assumptions (all-or-none, fixed threshold, synaptic delay…) · `12:00` Building AND, OR, NOT from neurons; then any finite automaton · `21:00` What they got biologically wrong · `26:00` Why "neural network" is a 1943 word
▸ **Nail these:** inhibitory synapses are absolute here, not weighted; no learning in this paper at all; the equivalence to finite state machines; the missing piece is weight adjustment, which is Day 4.

**D4 · The Perceptron: A Probabilistic Model for Information Storage** — Rosenblatt, *Psych. Review* 1958 · 40 min · L1
`0:00` The New York Times headline and the machine underneath it · `5:00` S-units, A-units, R-units — the actual architecture · `13:00` The learning rule: w ← w + η(y − ŷ)x, derived from scratch · `22:00` The Perceptron Convergence Theorem, proved on screen (margin γ, radius R, bound R²/γ²) · `34:00` What "probabilistic" meant to Rosenblatt
▸ **Nail these:** do the convergence proof — it's 15 lines and viewers never see it; linear separability as the precondition; the perceptron does *not* minimize a differentiable loss; hardware Mark I was physical, with motor-driven potentiometers.

**D5 · Perceptrons** (Ch. 1–2, 13) — Minsky & Papert, MIT Press 1969 · 45 min · L1
`0:00` The book that allegedly caused an AI winter · `5:00` XOR: prove non-separability geometrically, then algebraically · `16:00` Order-limited predicates and the parity/connectedness results — the *real* content · `30:00` The multilayer footnote everyone misquotes · `38:00` Did this book kill funding? The historiography is contested — present both sides
▸ **Nail these:** their results are about *order-limited* perceptrons, a much sharper claim than "can't do XOR"; the connectedness theorem; the 1988 expanded edition's epilogue on backprop; the Rosenblatt-was-already-studying-multilayer counter-narrative.

**D6 · Adaptive Switching Circuits (ADALINE / LMS)** — Widrow & Hoff, 1960 · 30 min · L1
`0:00` The same year, a different rule · `4:00` LMS as stochastic gradient descent on squared error — before anyone called it that · `13:00` Why LMS converges where the perceptron rule oscillates · `21:00` MADALINE and the first real-world neural application (echo cancellation in modems) · `26:00` LMS → RLS → Kalman preview
▸ **Nail these:** LMS updates on the *pre-threshold* activation, perceptron on post-threshold — this is the whole difference; the stability condition on η relative to input power; this is Day 34's SGD nine years early.

**D7 · Nearest Neighbor Pattern Classification** — Cover & Hart, *IEEE IT* 1967 · 35 min · L1
`0:00` The dumbest algorithm that works · `4:00` 1-NN, decision boundaries, Voronoi cells · `12:00` The theorem: 1-NN error ≤ 2× Bayes error asymptotically — prove it · `24:00` Curse of dimensionality: distances concentrate, show it numerically · `30:00` Why this is the honest baseline for every embedding paper you'll see in Month 10
▸ **Nail these:** the R ≤ R* (2 − MR*/(M−1)) bound; the asymptotic assumption doing the work; metric choice matters more than k; k-NN as a non-parametric density estimator.

**D8 · Induction of Decision Trees (ID3)** — Quinlan, *Machine Learning* 1986 · 35 min · L1
`0:00` A model you can read out loud · `4:00` Information gain, computed by hand on a 14-row table · `14:00` The multi-valued-attribute bias and gain ratio as the patch · `22:00` Pruning, and why unpruned trees are pure overfitting machines · `29:00` ID3 → C4.5 → CART differences in one table
▸ **Nail these:** entropy from Day 2 reused directly; gain ratio's split-info denominator; Gini vs entropy is a near-non-difference — show it; trees are the base learner for Days 10–14.

**D9 · Bagging Predictors** — Breiman, *Machine Learning* 1996 · 30 min · L1
`0:00` Averaging as a variance reducer · `4:00` The bootstrap, and the 63.2% number derived (1 − 1/e) · `12:00` Why bagging helps unstable learners and does nothing for stable ones · `20:00` Out-of-bag error as free cross-validation · `26:00` The bias–variance ledger for bagging
▸ **Nail these:** derive 1 − (1 − 1/n)ⁿ → 1 − 1/e; bagging reduces variance, *not* bias; it can hurt on k-NN — Breiman says so; OOB estimate is nearly unbiased.

**D10 · Random Forests** — Breiman, *Machine Learning* 2001 · 40 min · L1
`0:00` Still the model to beat on tabular data in 2026 · `4:00` Bagging + random feature subsets = decorrelation · `13:00` The generalization bound: strength vs correlation, PE* ≤ ρ̄(1−s²)/s² · `24:00` Variable importance: permutation vs Gini, and the bias in the latter · `32:00` Why RF doesn't really overfit with more trees
▸ **Nail these:** mtry = √p classification, p/3 regression, and why; the strength/correlation tradeoff is the entire theory; permutation importance is biased toward high-cardinality features; proximity matrices as a forgotten feature.

**D11 · A Decision-Theoretic Generalization of On-Line Learning (AdaBoost)** — Freund & Schapire, *JCSS* 1997 · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Turning "slightly better than chance" into "arbitrarily good" · `5:00` The weak learning assumption, stated precisely · `12:00` AdaBoost's algorithm; derive α_t = ½ ln((1−ε)/ε) · `24:00` The training error bound Π 2√(ε(1−ε)) — full proof · `36:00` The margin explanation for why test error keeps dropping after train error hits zero
▸ **Nail these:** the exponential loss view (Friedman/Hastie/Tibshirani, 2000) as the modern reading; AdaBoost's noise sensitivity — it's a real failure mode; the boosting-margin vs VC-bound argument; weights are on *examples*, not features.

**D12 · Greedy Function Approximation: A Gradient Boosting Machine** — Friedman, *Annals of Statistics* 2001 · 50 min · L2
`0:00` Boosting reframed as gradient descent in function space · `6:00` Functional gradient: fit the base learner to the negative gradient · `18:00` Recovering AdaBoost as the exponential-loss special case · `28:00` Shrinkage, subsampling (stochastic GB), tree depth as interaction order · `40:00` Partial dependence plots — from this same paper
▸ **Nail these:** the line-search step per leaf; deviance/Huber/absolute-loss variants; ν (shrinkage) trades off against M (number of trees) — show the curve; depth d captures d-way interactions.

**D13 · XGBoost: A Scalable Tree Boosting System** — Chen & Guestrin, *KDD* 2016 (arXiv:1603.02754) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` The Kaggle-winningest paper ever written · `5:00` The regularized objective and its second-order (Newton) expansion · `16:00` Optimal leaf weight w* = −G/(H+λ) and the gain formula — derive both · `27:00` Sparsity-aware split finding + the weighted quantile sketch · `36:00` Systems: cache-aware access, block compression, out-of-core
▸ **Nail these:** it's a *systems* paper as much as an ML paper — cover both halves; the default direction for missing values; why second-order beats first-order here; LightGBM/CatBoost as the follow-ups (GOSS, EFB, ordered target statistics).

**D14 · Support-Vector Networks** — Cortes & Vapnik, *Machine Learning* 1995 · 55 min · L2
`0:00` The margin idea, geometrically, before any algebra · `6:00` Hard-margin primal; derive the 2/‖w‖ margin · `18:00` Lagrangian dual, KKT conditions, and why only support vectors survive · `34:00` Soft margin, slack variables, C as the regularization dial · `46:00` Hinge loss = the loss SVM was doing all along
▸ **Nail these:** actually derive the dual — most videos skip it and it's the whole point; complementary slackness explains sparsity; C → ∞ recovers hard margin; SVM is convex, so no local minima — contrast with everything after Day 31.

**D15 · A Training Algorithm for Optimal Margin Classifiers (the kernel trick)** — Boser, Guyon & Vapnik, *COLT* 1992 · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Infinite-dimensional features for the price of a dot product · `5:00` The dual only touches ⟨x_i, x_j⟩ — that's the whole insight · `14:00` Mercer's condition; polynomial and RBF kernels · `26:00` RBF as an infinite-dimensional map — show the Taylor expansion · `36:00` Kernels on strings, graphs, sets; and the O(n²) memory wall
▸ **Nail these:** positive semi-definite Gram matrix as the requirement; γ in RBF controls effective bandwidth, show under/overfit visually; the representer theorem; why kernels lost to deep nets (scaling, not expressiveness).

**D16 · A Theory of the Learnable (PAC learning)** — Valiant, *CACM* 1984 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` What does it even mean to say a model "learned" · `5:00` The (ε, δ) formulation, stated carefully · `14:00` Sample complexity for finite hypothesis classes: m ≥ (1/ε)(ln|H| + ln(1/δ)) — derive it · `26:00` Realizable vs agnostic PAC · `33:00` Why nobody computes these bounds in practice, and why you should still know them
▸ **Nail these:** the union bound doing all the work; polynomial in 1/ε, 1/δ, and size; the connection to Occam's razor; this is the setup for Day 17.

**D17 · On the Uniform Convergence of Relative Frequencies (VC dimension)** — Vapnik & Chervonenkis, 1971 · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Infinite hypothesis classes that still generalize · `6:00` Shattering, growth function, VC dimension — with the 3-points-in-2D example done live · `18:00` Sauer–Shelah lemma · `28:00` The VC generalization bound and its shape (√(d/n)) · `38:00` Why VC bounds are vacuous for neural nets — set up the Day 316 double-descent puzzle now
▸ **Nail these:** VC-dim of linear classifiers in ℝ^d is d+1 — prove it; VC-dim ≠ parameter count (the sin(θx) example has infinite VC-dim, one parameter); bounds are distribution-free, hence loose; this is *the* classical theory deep learning broke.

**D18 · Neural Networks and the Bias/Variance Dilemma** — Geman, Bienenstock & Doursat, *Neural Computation* 1992 · 35 min · L1
`0:00` The decomposition, derived line by line · `8:00` Irreducible noise, bias², variance — and the estimator-vs-model distinction · `18:00` The classic U-curve, drawn · `25:00` Where this framing quietly breaks (flag Day 316) · `31:00` Practical read: which knob moves which term
▸ **Nail these:** derive E[(y − f̂)²] = σ² + bias² + var; the decomposition is for squared loss and doesn't cleanly extend to 0-1 loss; "variance" is over training sets, an ensemble concept people forget; modern nets sit past the U-curve's right edge.

**D19 · Maximum Likelihood from Incomplete Data via the EM Algorithm** — Dempster, Laird & Rubin, *JRSS-B* 1977 · 50 min · L2
`0:00` Chicken-and-egg estimation · `5:00` Latent variables; the complete-data likelihood · `13:00` E-step and M-step on a 2-component Gaussian mixture, by hand · `26:00` The ELBO and the proof that likelihood is monotonically non-decreasing · `40:00` Local optima, initialization, degenerate solutions
▸ **Nail these:** derive the ELBO via Jensen — you will reuse this *exactly* on Day 241 (VAE); E-step = tighten the bound, M-step = maximize it; EM never decreases likelihood but converges to a stationary point, not a global max; k-means as hard-assignment EM.

**D20 · Least Squares Quantization in PCM (k-means)** — Lloyd, 1957/*IEEE IT* 1982 · 30 min · L1
`0:00` A 1957 telephone-compression paper is your clustering algorithm · `4:00` Lloyd's iteration and its guaranteed convergence · `11:00` NP-hardness of the actual objective; local minima demoed · `18:00` k-means++ seeding and the O(log k) guarantee · `25:00` Choosing k: elbow, silhouette, gap — and the honest answer that it's ill-posed
▸ **Nail these:** k-means implicitly assumes isotropic equal-variance clusters — show it failing on elongated blobs; Voronoi partition connection to Day 7; k-means++ is Arthur & Vassilvitskii 2007; vector quantization here reappears on Day 243 (VQ-VAE).

**D21 · Eigenfaces for Recognition** — Turk & Pentland, *J. Cognitive Neuro.* 1991 · 40 min · L1
`0:00` PCA, but you can see the components · `5:00` Covariance, eigendecomposition, and the snapshot trick for d ≫ n · `17:00` Projection, reconstruction error, and the variance-explained curve · `27:00` Face space, distance-from-face-space for detection · `34:00` Why it fails on lighting and pose — and what that taught the field
▸ **Nail these:** the n×n vs d×d covariance trick is the practical crux; PCA = SVD of centered data, show the equivalence; mean face subtraction is not optional; first 3 components are mostly illumination — that's the famous failure.

**D22 · The Use of Multiple Measurements in Taxonomic Problems (LDA)** — Fisher, *Annals of Eugenics* 1936 · 35 min · L1
`0:00` The iris dataset's origin, and an honest word about the journal it appeared in · `6:00` Fisher's criterion: maximize between-class over within-class scatter · `15:00` The generalized eigenproblem S_W⁻¹S_B, solved on screen · `25:00` LDA vs PCA — supervised vs unsupervised, demonstrated on the same data · `31:00` LDA as a generative classifier with shared covariance
▸ **Nail these:** at most C−1 discriminant directions and why; the singular-S_W problem when d > n and the shrinkage fix; QDA as the relaxed-covariance sibling; do address the eugenics context directly and briefly — viewers will ask.

**D23 · A Comparison of Event Models for Naive Bayes Text Classification** — McCallum & Nigam, 1998 · 30 min · L1
`0:00` The baseline that refuses to die · `4:00` Bayes rule, the conditional independence assumption, and why it's obviously false · `12:00` Multinomial vs Bernoulli event models — the paper's actual contribution · `20:00` Laplace/Lidstone smoothing; log-space computation to avoid underflow · `26:00` Why a wrong model still classifies well (argmax is robust to bad calibration)
▸ **Nail these:** the two event models give different answers at different vocabulary sizes — show their crossover figure; NB is a linear classifier in log space; probability estimates are badly calibrated even when arg max is right — links to Day 326.

**D24 · A Maximum Entropy Approach to Natural Language Processing** — Berger, Della Pietra & Della Pietra, *Comp. Ling.* 1996 · 45 min · L2
`0:00` "Assume nothing beyond what you know" as a hard constraint · `6:00` Feature constraints; the maxent variational problem · `16:00` Deriving the exponential family form via Lagrange multipliers · `28:00` Equivalence to maximum likelihood for log-linear models — the duality result · `38:00` GIS/IIS training; why this became "logistic regression" and then "the softmax"
▸ **Nail these:** softmax is the maxent distribution — say it explicitly, it reframes every classifier head in this series; the duality proof; feature functions are hand-designed here, which is exactly what deep learning eliminates; regularization as constraint relaxation.

**D25 · A Tutorial on Hidden Markov Models and Selected Applications in Speech Recognition** — Rabiner, *Proc. IEEE* 1989 · 55 min · L2
`0:00` The paper that ran speech recognition for 25 years · `6:00` The three problems: evaluation, decoding, learning · `14:00` Forward algorithm (and the scaling problem nobody mentions) · `26:00` Viterbi decoding, traced on a lattice by hand · `38:00` Baum–Welch as EM for HMMs — connect straight back to Day 19 · `50:00` Left-right topologies, continuous emissions, why CTC replaced this
▸ **Nail these:** forward-backward vs Viterbi is sum-product vs max-product — say it; underflow and the scaling coefficients; Baum–Welch is EM, not a separate thing; the Markov assumption is the failure mode, and it's what RNNs fix on Day 58.

**D26 · A New Approach to Linear Filtering and Prediction Problems** — Kalman, *J. Basic Eng.* 1960 · 50 min · L2
`0:00` The algorithm that landed Apollo · `6:00` State-space model: transition + observation, with noise covariances · `15:00` Predict and update steps, derived as recursive Bayesian estimation · `28:00` The Kalman gain as a precision-weighted average — the intuition that makes it click · `38:00` EKF, UKF, and the linear-Gaussian assumption's cost · `46:00` Kalman filter = HMM with continuous Gaussian state
▸ **Nail these:** derive the gain K = PHᵀ(HPHᵀ+R)⁻¹ and read it as "trust the measurement in proportion to its relative precision"; optimality holds only under linear-Gaussian; covariance propagates independently of the data — surprising and worth a beat; the RLS connection to Day 6.

**D27 · Probabilistic Reasoning in Intelligent Systems** (Ch. 3–4, belief propagation) — Pearl, 1988 · 50 min · L2
`0:00` Structure as the thing that makes probability tractable · `6:00` DAGs, conditional independence, the chain rule factorization · `16:00` d-separation, all three cases, drawn · `28:00` Explaining away — the intuition pump that sells the whole framework · `36:00` Belief propagation on trees; loopy BP as a heuristic · `45:00` Where this leads: PGMs, and Pearl's later causal work
▸ **Nail these:** d-separation with the collider case done slowly — it's the one everyone gets wrong; explaining away = conditioning on a common effect induces dependence; exact inference is #P-hard in general; Markov blanket definition, since it returns in Gibbs sampling tomorrow-ish.

**D28 · Equation of State Calculations by Fast Computing Machines (Metropolis)** — Metropolis et al., *J. Chem. Phys.* 1953 · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Sampling when you can't normalize · `5:00` Markov chains, stationary distributions, detailed balance · `15:00` The Metropolis acceptance ratio — note the target's normalizer cancels · `25:00` Hastings' 1970 generalization to asymmetric proposals · `33:00` Burn-in, mixing time, autocorrelation, and how to tell it's broken · `40:00` HMC in one minute as the modern answer
▸ **Nail these:** detailed balance ⇒ stationarity (and it's sufficient, not necessary); the cancellation of Z is the entire practical value; proposal variance tuning and the ~23.4% acceptance folklore; trace plots and R̂ as diagnostics.

**D29 · Stochastic Relaxation, Gibbs Distributions, and the Bayesian Restoration of Images** — Geman & Geman, *IEEE PAMI* 1984 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` MCMC arrives in vision · `5:00` Gibbs sampling as Metropolis with acceptance always 1 · `14:00` MRFs, cliques, the Hammersley–Clifford theorem · `24:00` Simulated annealing and the log-cooling convergence result · `32:00` Image denoising demo — run it, show the chain converging
▸ **Nail these:** Gibbs needs tractable conditionals, that's the whole catch; Markov blanket from Day 27 is what you condition on; the annealing schedule that's provably optimal is also uselessly slow; contrastive divergence (Day 55) is a truncated version of this.

**D30 · Gaussian Processes for Machine Learning** (Ch. 2) — Rasmussen & Williams, MIT Press 2006 · 50 min · L3
`0:00` A distribution over functions, drawn · `6:00` Multivariate Gaussian conditioning — the one formula that generates everything · `16:00` The GP posterior mean and variance, derived · `28:00` Kernel choice as prior belief: RBF, Matérn, periodic — sample from each · `38:00` Marginal likelihood and hyperparameter learning without cross-validation · `45:00` O(n³) and the sparse-GP escape hatches
▸ **Nail these:** conditioning formula for joint Gaussians, done fully; the posterior variance doesn't depend on observed y values — a genuinely surprising fact; Matérn smoothness parameter ν; infinite-width nets are GPs (Neal 1996) — plant this seed for Day 318.

---

# MONTH 2 — Neural Network Fundamentals & Optimization
### Days 31–60 · Level 1–2 · Everything that makes training actually work

Month 3 onward is architectures. Architectures are the fun part and they are *not* where models are won or lost. This month is. If a viewer only watches one month, make it this one.

---

**D31 · Learning Representations by Back-Propagating Errors** — Rumelhart, Hinton & Williams, *Nature* 1986 · 55 min · L1
`0:00` The two-page paper that restarted the field · `5:00` Chain rule on a 2-layer net, every partial derivative written out by hand · `20:00` The general backward pass; δ recursion derived · `32:00` Code it live in NumPy, 40 lines, no framework · `45:00` What Nature's page limit hid: the internal-representation figure and the symmetry-breaking requirement
▸ **Nail these:** backprop is reverse-mode autodiff, not a learning algorithm — separate the two ideas; why zero init is fatal (symmetry); computational cost is ~2× forward; Linnainmaa 1970 and Werbos 1974 got there first — say so.

**D32 · Automatic Differentiation in Machine Learning: A Survey** — Baydin et al., *JMLR* 2018 (arXiv:1502.05767) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` What your framework is actually doing · `5:00` Four things people confuse: manual, numerical, symbolic, automatic differentiation · `14:00` Forward mode and dual numbers · `24:00` Reverse mode and the tape; cost analysis n inputs vs m outputs · `34:00` Checkpointing, and the memory–compute tradeoff you'll re-meet on Day 341 · `40:00` Static vs dynamic graphs → TensorFlow vs PyTorch
▸ **Nail these:** forward mode costs O(n) sweeps, reverse costs O(m) — this is why ML uses reverse; expression swell in symbolic diff; numerical diff's O(√ε) error floor; higher-order derivatives via nesting.

**D33 · Gradient-Based Learning Applied to Document Recognition (LeNet-5)** — LeCun, Bottou, Bengio & Haffner, *Proc. IEEE* 1998 · 55 min · L1
`0:00` A 1998 net that read a third of US bank cheques · `6:00` Convolution: local receptive fields, weight sharing, translation equivariance — derive the parameter savings numerically · `20:00` Subsampling, and how it differs from modern max-pooling · `28:00` The full LeNet-5 layer table, C1 through F6 · `38:00` Graph Transformer Networks — the half of the paper nobody reads · `48:00` Why it took 14 more years (Day 61)
▸ **Nail these:** equivariance ≠ invariance, and pooling is what converts one to the other; the sparse C3 connection table and its rationale; RBF output layer, not softmax; the "why 2012 not 1998" answer is data + GPUs + ReLU, all three.

**D34 · A Stochastic Approximation Method** — Robbins & Monro, *Ann. Math. Stat.* 1951 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Optimization before there was anything to optimize · `5:00` Root-finding with noisy evaluations · `13:00` The Robbins–Monro conditions: Σηₜ = ∞, Σηₜ² < ∞ — explain what each one prevents · `24:00` Why SGD is this algorithm applied to ∇L = 0 · `32:00` Minibatches: variance reduction as √B, and the linear-scaling heuristic
▸ **Nail these:** the two conditions map to "can reach anywhere" and "noise dies out" — say it that way; 1/t satisfies both, constant η satisfies neither; constant-η SGD converges to a noise ball, not a point; gradient noise as implicit regularization.

**D35 · Some Methods of Speeding Up the Convergence of Iteration Methods** — Polyak, 1964 (+ Nesterov 1983) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Why gradient descent zigzags in a ravine · `5:00` Heavy-ball momentum; the physical analogy and its limits · `14:00` Condition number κ and the √κ vs κ convergence-rate improvement · `26:00` Nesterov's accelerated gradient: the lookahead difference, drawn · `36:00` The PyTorch/TF formulation vs the paper's — they differ, and it matters
▸ **Nail these:** β = 0.9 ⇒ effective step ×10 at steady state (1/(1−β)); NAG evaluates the gradient *after* the momentum step; Nesterov's O(1/k²) is optimal for smooth convex; momentum can diverge if η is set as though there were none.

**D36 · Adaptive Subgradient Methods (AdaGrad)** — Duchi, Hazan & Singer, *JMLR* 2011 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Per-parameter learning rates, and why sparse features need them · `5:00` The accumulator G_t = Σ g², and the η/√G update · `14:00` The regret bound, sketched · `24:00` The monotonic-decay failure: learning rate → 0 and training stalls · `32:00` Straight line to RMSProp's exponential window
▸ **Nail these:** AdaGrad shines on sparse gradients (NLP embeddings), stalls on dense deep nets; full-matrix vs diagonal versions; the ε in the denominator is a numerical guard, not a hyperparameter to tune much; this failure *is* the motivation for tomorrow.

**D37 · Adam: A Method for Stochastic Optimization** — Kingma & Ba, *ICLR* 2015 (arXiv:1412.6980) · 50 min · L2
`0:00` The default that ate the field · `4:00` RMSProp (Hinton's unpublished slide) as the missing link ⟵ D36 · `12:00` Algorithm 1 line by line · `20:00` Bias correction derived, plus the t=1 blow-up shown numerically · `30:00` What the convergence proof claims, and the Reddi et al. counterexample (AMSGrad) · `40:00` ε placement, β₂'s ~1/(1−β₂) memory, and the L2-vs-decoupled-decay bug
▸ **Nail these:** derive m̂ = m/(1−β₁ᵗ); β₂ = 0.999 ⇒ ~1000-step effective window; the ICLR proof has an error — that's real and you should say it; ImageNet ResNet cases where SGD+momentum still wins the final point of accuracy.

**D38 · Decoupled Weight Decay Regularization (AdamW)** — Loshchilov & Hutter, *ICLR* 2019 (arXiv:1711.05101) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` A one-line fix worth points of accuracy on every LLM since · `4:00` L2 penalty vs weight decay: identical for SGD, *not* for adaptive methods — prove it algebraically · `14:00` Why Adam's denominator undoes L2 on large-gradient weights · `22:00` The AdamW update; decoupling λ from η · `28:00` Why every LLM recipe from Day 167 onward says "AdamW, λ=0.1"
▸ **Nail these:** show the two updates side by side and cancel terms — the proof is four lines and it's the episode; PyTorch's `weight_decay` in Adam is the *broken* one; typical λ values; interaction with LR schedules.

**D39 · SGDR: Stochastic Gradient Descent with Warm Restarts** — Loshchilov & Hutter, *ICLR* 2017 (arXiv:1608.03983) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` The learning-rate curve you've seen in every training log · `4:00` Cosine annealing, written out · `11:00` Warm restarts, T_mult, and the escape-a-basin story · `20:00` Snapshot ensembles as a free byproduct · `27:00` Why plain cosine (no restarts) won for LLMs, plus linear warmup
▸ **Nail these:** the cosine formula η_t = η_min + ½(η_max−η_min)(1+cos(πT_cur/T_i)); warmup exists because Adam's second-moment estimate is garbage for the first ~100 steps; LR schedule interacts with batch size; the "restarts help" claim is weaker than the cosine-shape claim.

**D40 · Cyclical Learning Rates + Super-Convergence** — Leslie Smith, *WACV* 2017 / arXiv:1708.07120 · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Training 10× faster by making the LR *bigger* · `4:00` The LR range test — run it live, read the plot · `13:00` Triangular policies; one-cycle · `22:00` The large-LR-as-regularizer argument and its evidence · `29:00` Where super-convergence reproduces and where it doesn't
▸ **Nail these:** the LR range test is the single most useful practical tool in this month — demo it properly; one-cycle also cycles momentum, inversely; results are architecture- and dataset-sensitive; this is fast.ai's core practice.

**D41 · Approximation by Superpositions of a Sigmoidal Function** — Cybenko, *MCSS* 1989 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` One hidden layer can approximate anything — and why that tells you almost nothing · `6:00` The theorem stated precisely: continuous functions, compact domain, uniform approximation · `16:00` Proof sketch via Hahn–Banach and the Riesz representation theorem · `28:00` Hornik's generalization; what "discriminatory" means · `34:00` The three things the theorem does *not* give you: width, learnability, generalization
▸ **Nail these:** width may need to be exponential in dimension; existence is non-constructive; depth separation results (Telgarsky 2016, Eldan & Shamir 2016) show exponential gaps — this is the real answer to "why deep"; ReLU version is Leshno et al. 1993.

**D42 · Learning Long-Term Dependencies with Gradient Descent Is Difficult** — Bengio, Simard & Frasconi, *IEEE TNN* 1994 · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Why RNNs forgot · `5:00` The product-of-Jacobians in BPTT, written out · `15:00` Spectral radius < 1 ⇒ vanish, > 1 ⇒ explode; the dilemma is *provable* · `26:00` Hochreiter's 1991 thesis result, and the flat-error-surface analysis · `34:00` Every fix that follows: gating (D58), gradient clipping, skip connections (D67), attention (D129)
▸ **Nail these:** the tension — storing information robustly *requires* contraction, which kills gradients; exponential decay in the number of timesteps, so 100 steps is hopeless at ρ=0.9; clipping fixes explosion only, never vanishing; this paper is the reason ResNets and LSTMs both exist.

**D43 · Understanding the Difficulty of Training Deep Feedforward Neural Networks (Xavier init)** — Glorot & Bengio, *AISTATS* 2010 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Deep nets that wouldn't train, and the histogram that explained it · `5:00` Variance propagation forward and backward, derived · `16:00` The compromise Var(W) = 2/(fan_in + fan_out) · `25:00` The activation-saturation figures — the empirical heart of the paper · `33:00` Why this fails for ReLU (tomorrow)
▸ **Nail these:** do the variance derivation properly; the forward and backward conditions conflict, hence the harmonic-mean compromise; assumes linear/tanh regime around zero; the saturation plots for sigmoid's top layer are the most persuasive figure — put it on screen.

**D44 · Delving Deep into Rectifiers (He init + PReLU)** — He, Zhang, Ren & Sun, *ICCV* 2015 (arXiv:1502.01852) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` The factor of 2 that lets 30-layer nets train · `4:00` Redo yesterday's variance derivation, but with ReLU killing half the mass · `14:00` Var(W) = 2/fan_in, and the 22-layer vs 30-layer convergence figure · `23:00` PReLU: learn the negative slope, ~free parameters · `29:00` The first superhuman-on-ImageNet claim, and how to read that responsibly
▸ **Nail these:** the ½ from ReLU's zeroing is the entire delta from Xavier; fan_in vs fan_out mode and when each matters; PReLU's learned slopes differ by depth — show their table; superhuman = beats one human annotator on one benchmark, and say that plainly.

**D45 · Deep Sparse Rectifier Neural Networks** — Glorot, Bordes & Bengio, *AISTATS* 2011 (+ Nair & Hinton 2010) · 35 min · L1
`0:00` The activation function that made depth cheap · `4:00` Sigmoid/tanh gradients: max 0.25, and the compounding · `12:00` ReLU: constant gradient, sparsity, no saturation on the positive side · `21:00` Dying ReLU — demonstrate it, then LeakyReLU/ELU/GELU/SiLU as answers · `28:00` Why GELU won in transformers
▸ **Nail these:** ReLU is not differentiable at 0 and nobody cares — explain why (measure zero, subgradient); 50% sparsity as a claimed feature; GELU = x·Φ(x), and its smoothness argument; the honest verdict that activation choice is worth <1% once you have residuals + norm.

**D46 · Dropout: A Simple Way to Prevent Neural Networks from Overfitting** — Srivastava et al., *JMLR* 2014 · 40 min · L1
`0:00` Deliberately breaking your network · `4:00` The procedure; the exponential-ensemble interpretation · `13:00` Train/test asymmetry and inverted dropout — get the scaling right, it's where people bug it · `22:00` Co-adaptation, and the sexual-reproduction analogy from the paper · `30:00` Why dropout mostly vanished from modern nets (BN, then scale, then residual regularization)
▸ **Nail these:** weight scaling as an approximate geometric-mean ensemble; interaction with BatchNorm is genuinely bad (Li et al. 2019 variance shift) — cover it; p=0.5 hidden, 0.8 input as the paper's defaults; DropConnect, spatial dropout, and stochastic depth as relatives.

**D47 · Batch Normalization** — Ioffe & Szegedy, *ICML* 2015 (arXiv:1502.03167) · 50 min · L2
`0:00` The technique that worked, with an explanation that didn't · `5:00` Internal covariate shift as claimed; the BN algorithm; γ and β · `16:00` Backprop through BN — derive it, including the mean/variance paths · `28:00` Train vs inference: running statistics, and every bug that comes from getting this wrong · `36:00` Santurkar et al. 2018: it's loss-surface smoothing, not ICS — the refutation · `44:00` Batch-size dependence, and why LLMs use LayerNorm instead
▸ **Nail these:** the backward pass in full — most channels hand-wave it; BN makes examples in a batch non-independent, which breaks RL and small batches; BN before or after activation (paper says before, practice often says after); BN + weight decay interaction is weird because BN makes the net scale-invariant.

**D48 · Layer Normalization** — Ba, Kiros & Hinton, 2016 (arXiv:1607.06450) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Normalizing the other way · `4:00` Per-example, across features — draw the tensor and shade the axis · `11:00` Why this is batch-size independent and works for variable-length sequences · `18:00` Pre-LN vs Post-LN placement, and the gradient magnitudes at init · `25:00` RMSNorm as the cheaper version everyone now uses ⟶ D162
▸ **Nail these:** draw the (batch, sequence, feature) cube and shade what each norm averages over — this single visual is why people watch normalization videos; GroupNorm and InstanceNorm on the same cube; LN's learnable gain/bias; LN is applied per-token in transformers.

**D49 · Understanding Deep Learning Requires Rethinking Generalization** — Zhang, Bengio, Hardt, Recht & Vinyals, *ICLR* 2017 (arXiv:1611.03530) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` They trained a net to memorize random labels. It worked. · `6:00` The random-label experiment and the random-pixel one · `16:00` Why this detonates VC bounds and Rademacher complexity ⟵ D17 · `26:00` Explicit regularization is neither necessary nor sufficient — their evidence · `35:00` Implicit regularization, and the open question this paper left · `41:00` The 2021 CACM revisit
▸ **Nail these:** effective capacity ≥ memorizing the whole training set, so uniform-convergence bounds are vacuous; time-to-fit is *longer* for random labels, a hint people miss; SGD's implicit bias toward min-norm solutions; this is the honest "we don't know why deep learning generalizes" episode — don't paper over it.

**D50 · On the Importance of Initialization and Momentum in Deep Learning** — Sutskever, Martens, Dahl & Hinton, *ICML* 2013 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Hessian-free was beaten by SGD tuned properly · `5:00` Momentum schedules; μ ramped 0.5 → 0.99 · `15:00` NAG vs classical momentum in the deep, non-convex setting · `25:00` Sparse init and the deep-autoencoder benchmarks · `32:00` The meta-lesson: tuning beats novelty more often than papers admit
▸ **Nail these:** momentum matters most early; the deep autoencoder and echo-state-network benchmarks; NAG's stability advantage at high μ; this paper is why "did you tune the baseline?" is the right first question about any result.

**D51 · A Fast Learning Algorithm for Deep Belief Nets** — Hinton, Osindero & Teh, *Neural Computation* 2006 · 50 min · L3
`0:00` The paper that started the deep learning revival · `6:00` RBMs: energy, the bipartite structure, tractable conditionals · `18:00` Greedy layer-wise stacking and the variational bound it improves · `30:00` Up-down fine-tuning · `40:00` The MNIST result, and the historical role: pretraining as the crutch we later threw away
▸ **Nail these:** the proof that adding a layer improves a variational bound on the log-likelihood; complementary priors; DBNs are directed-except-the-top-pair — a detail almost everyone gets wrong; by 2012 ReLU+dropout+data made this unnecessary, and that's a real lesson about "essential" techniques.

**D52 · Reducing the Dimensionality of Data with Neural Networks** — Hinton & Salakhutdinov, *Science* 2006 · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Autoencoders beat PCA, in Science, on one page · `4:00` The 784-1000-500-250-2 architecture and its mirror · `12:00` Pretrain-then-unroll-then-finetune · `20:00` The 2-D MNIST and Reuters embeddings vs PCA — put both on screen · `27:00` Undercomplete vs overcomplete; the identity-function failure mode
▸ **Nail these:** a linear autoencoder with squared loss spans the PCA subspace — prove it, then show the nonlinear gain; the bottleneck is the regularizer; reconstruction loss ≠ useful representation, which is the whole tension of Month 4's self-supervision.

**D53 · Extracting and Composing Robust Features with Denoising Autoencoders** — Vincent, Larochelle, Bengio & Manzagol, *ICML* 2008 · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Corrupt the input, demand the clean output · `4:00` The corruption process; masking vs Gaussian noise · `12:00` The manifold interpretation: learning to project back onto the data manifold · `21:00` Score matching equivalence (Vincent 2011) — DAEs estimate ∇log p · `28:00` Direct line: DAE → BERT masking (D137) → MAE (D118) → diffusion (D260)
▸ **Nail these:** noise is what prevents identity-copying in overcomplete codes; the score-matching result is the deep one and it's why diffusion feels familiar later; contractive autoencoders as the explicit-penalty sibling; make the BERT/MAE/diffusion lineage explicit — it's one of the best connective moments in the whole series.

**D54 · Emergence of Simple-Cell Receptive Field Properties by Learning a Sparse Code** — Olshausen & Field, *Nature* 1996 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Sparsity derives V1 from natural images alone · `5:00` The objective: reconstruction + sparsity penalty · `14:00` Learned Gabor-like filters — show them next to macaque recordings · `24:00` Dictionary learning, overcompleteness, L1 vs L0 · `32:00` ISTA/LISTA and the sparse-coding-as-unrolled-network idea
▸ **Nail these:** the filters emerge without labels or supervision — that's the claim; efficient-coding hypothesis (Barlow); L1 relaxation and why it induces sparsity geometrically; this is the ancestor of Day 310's sparse autoencoders for interpretability, and viewers should feel that echo.

**D55 · Training Products of Experts by Minimizing Contrastive Divergence** — Hinton, *Neural Computation* 2002 · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Learning when the normalizer is intractable · `5:00` Products of experts vs mixtures — the sharpening argument · `14:00` The RBM log-likelihood gradient: data term minus model term · `24:00` CD-k: truncate the chain, accept the bias · `33:00` Why CD is biased and works anyway; PCD/persistent chains
▸ **Nail these:** ⟨v h⟩_data − ⟨v h⟩_model is the whole gradient — say it in one line; CD-1 is not a gradient of anything (Sutskever & Tieleman 2010); free energy; connection to Day 29's Gibbs sampling; energy-based models return in Day 262's score-based framing.

**D56 · Greedy Layer-Wise Training of Deep Networks** — Bengio, Lamblin, Popovici & Larochelle, *NIPS* 2007 · 30 min · L2
`0:00` The systematic study behind the DBN hype · `4:00` Unsupervised vs supervised pretraining, ablated · `12:00` Optimization effect vs regularization effect — the paper's central question · `20:00` Continuation-method framing · `25:00` The 2012 obsolescence, and why it still matters conceptually
▸ **Nail these:** pretraining acts mostly as a data-dependent regularizer (Erhan et al. 2010 confirms); it helped most in the small-data regime — which is exactly today's transfer-learning story; the honest arc from "essential" to "unnecessary" in 5 years.

**D57 · A Neural Probabilistic Language Model** — Bengio, Ducharme, Vincent & Jauvin, *JMLR* 2003 · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Word embeddings, ten years early · `5:00` The curse of dimensionality for n-grams: |V|ⁿ · `13:00` The architecture: lookup table → concat → tanh → softmax · `24:00` Why the |V|-way softmax was the bottleneck; hierarchical softmax as the fix · `33:00` What Mikolov changes in 2013 to make this cheap ⟶ D121 · `40:00` The distributional hypothesis (Firth 1957) stated properly
▸ **Nail these:** the shared embedding matrix is *the* idea; generalization to unseen n-grams via embedding similarity; softmax cost is O(|V|) per token; direct input-to-output connections in their architecture; this is the true origin of word2vec.

**D58 · Long Short-Term Memory** — Hochreiter & Schmidhuber, *Neural Computation* 1997 · 55 min · L2
`0:00` The cell that held a memory for 1000 steps · `6:00` Constant error carousel — derive why the cell state's gradient is 1 ⟵ D42 · `18:00` Input and output gates as written in 1997 · `28:00` The forget gate: Gers et al. 2000, *not* in the original paper · `36:00` Full forward and backward pass, drawn on one diagram · `48:00` Peepholes, bidirectional LSTM, and why LSTM ruled 1997–2017
▸ **Nail these:** the CEC is the entire innovation — additive state update, gradient 1; the original had no forget gate and that's a great historical beat; gate saturation and forget-gate bias init to 1; parameter count 4(nm + n² + n).

**D59 · Learning Phrase Representations using RNN Encoder–Decoder (GRU)** — Cho et al., *EMNLP* 2014 (arXiv:1406.1078) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` LSTM with a third of the moving parts · `4:00` Reset and update gates; the equations side by side with LSTM · `13:00` No separate cell state — the consequence of merging · `20:00` Greff et al. 2017 and Jozefowicz et al. 2015: the empirical verdict is "basically a tie" · `27:00` The encoder–decoder framing this paper also introduced, which matters more than the cell
▸ **Nail these:** the update gate does what LSTM's input *and* forget gates do together; ~25% fewer parameters; the fixed-length context vector bottleneck introduced here is precisely what attention fixes on Day 126; the phrase-scoring-for-SMT original application.

**D60 · Generating Sequences With Recurrent Neural Networks** — Graves, 2013 (arXiv:1308.0850) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` The handwriting samples that felt like magic in 2013 · `5:00` Character-level prediction; sampling temperature, demoed at 0.3 / 1.0 / 1.5 · `15:00` Stacked LSTMs with skip connections to the output · `24:00` Mixture Density Network outputs for real-valued pen strokes · `34:00` The attention-like window mechanism for text-conditioned synthesis — attention, one year early · `41:00` Teacher forcing and exposure bias
▸ **Nail these:** the soft-window Gaussian attention is a genuine precursor to Day 126 — show the equations next to each other; MDN loss for continuous outputs; temperature's effect on entropy; exposure bias sets up scheduled sampling and later RLHF.

---

# MONTH 3 — Computer Vision I: Backbones and Detection
### Days 61–90 · Level 2 · The architecture arms race, 2012–2020

Vision is where deep learning proved itself, and where the architectural vocabulary — residuals, bottlenecks, normalization placement, feature pyramids — was invented before being exported to everything else. Treat this month as architecture school, not as a vision course.

---

**D61 · ImageNet Classification with Deep Convolutional Neural Networks (AlexNet)** — Krizhevsky, Sutskever & Hinton, *NIPS* 2012 · 55 min · L2
`0:00` The 10.8-point gap that ended an era · `6:00` The 8-layer architecture, layer by layer, with the two-GPU split explained · `20:00` The four ingredients: ReLU, GPUs, dropout, augmentation — attribute each properly · `32:00` Local Response Normalization, and why it died · `40:00` Top-5 error 15.3% vs 26.2%, and what the runner-up actually was · `48:00` What was new vs what was 1998 ⟵ D33
▸ **Nail these:** the GPU split was a memory constraint, not a design idea, and it accidentally produced the color/frequency filter specialization — show the famous first-layer filters; 60M parameters, 5 conv + 3 FC; overlapping pooling; PCA color augmentation; the honest answer that nothing here was conceptually new except scale.

**D62 · Visualizing and Understanding Convolutional Networks (ZFNet)** — Zeiler & Fergus, *ECCV* 2014 (arXiv:1311.2901) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Looking inside AlexNet, and fixing it by looking · `5:00` Deconvnet: unpooling with switches, rectify, transposed filtering · `16:00` Layer-by-layer feature visualizations — the hierarchy made visible · `27:00` The diagnosis: AlexNet's 11×11 stride-4 first layer was aliasing; the 7×7 stride-2 fix · `36:00` Occlusion sensitivity maps · `41:00` The first real interpretability paper — plant the flag for Month 11
▸ **Nail these:** switches make the deconvnet input-specific, so it's not a true inverse; the "fix the architecture by looking at it" methodology is the paper's real contribution; feature evolution during training figures; this is the ancestor of Days 301–306.

**D63 · Network In Network** — Lin, Chen & Yan, *ICLR* 2014 (arXiv:1312.4400) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Two ideas that quietly ate the field · `4:00` MLPconv: a 1×1 convolution is a per-pixel fully-connected layer — make this click properly · `14:00` Global average pooling replaces the FC head; parameter count collapse · `22:00` GAP as a structural regularizer, and its role in Grad-CAM later ⟶ D302 · `27:00` Where you'll see 1×1 next: Inception, ResNet bottlenecks, SE blocks, MobileNet
▸ **Nail these:** 1×1 conv = channel mixing with no spatial mixing, and it's the cheapest expressivity you can buy; GAP removes ~90% of AlexNet's parameters (the FC layers); GAP forces channel↔class correspondence; this 30-minute paper underpins the next ten episodes.

**D64 · Very Deep Convolutional Networks (VGG)** — Simonyan & Zisserman, *ICLR* 2015 (arXiv:1409.1556) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` One idea, executed with discipline: 3×3, everywhere · `5:00` Two stacked 3×3 = one 5×5 receptive field with fewer parameters and two nonlinearities — do the arithmetic on screen · `15:00` The A–E configuration table, read properly · `24:00` 138M parameters, ~90% in the first FC layer · `31:00` Multi-scale training/testing; why VGG features became the default perceptual loss backbone
▸ **Nail these:** 2×(3²C²) = 18C² vs 25C² — show it; three 3×3 = 7×7 with 27C² vs 49C²; VGG is the standard for perceptual/style losses to this day (Days 253, 264); training required staged initialization from the shallow model, a detail that motivates Day 43/44.

**D65 · Going Deeper with Convolutions (GoogLeNet / Inception v1)** — Szegedy et al., *CVPR* 2015 (arXiv:1409.4842) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` 22 layers, 12× fewer parameters than AlexNet · `6:00` The naive Inception module and its cost explosion · `14:00` 1×1 bottlenecks as dimensionality reduction ⟵ D63 · `24:00` The Hebbian/sparse-structure motivation from Arora et al. — the theory they cite · `32:00` Auxiliary classifiers: the intent vs what they actually did · `39:00` Multi-branch design as a recurring idea
▸ **Nail these:** compute the naive module's FLOPs vs the bottlenecked version — that number is the episode; auxiliary heads were later shown to act as regularizers, not gradient highways (Inception-v3 says so); 5M parameters; the "we tried architectures until one worked" honesty.

**D66 · Rethinking the Inception Architecture (Inception-v2/v3)** — Szegedy et al., *CVPR* 2016 (arXiv:1512.00567) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Four design principles, stated explicitly — rare and valuable · `5:00` Factorizing 5×5 into two 3×3, then n×n into 1×n + n×1 · `16:00` Avoiding representational bottlenecks; the efficient grid-size reduction block · `26:00` Label smoothing: the formula, the entropy argument, and its afterlife in every modern classifier · `34:00` BN-auxiliary; the 3.5% top-5 result
▸ **Nail these:** asymmetric factorization works best at medium grid sizes (12–20) — they say so, most summaries don't; label smoothing ε=0.1 and its effect on logit gaps and calibration ⟶ D326; the four principles are worth reading verbatim on screen.

**D67 · Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition (ResNet)** — He, Zhang, Ren & Sun, *CVPR* 2016 (arXiv:1512.03385) · 60 min · L2
`0:00` The degradation problem: 56 layers worse than 20, on *training* error · `8:00` Why that can't be overfitting, and what it implies about optimization · `16:00` The residual block; y = F(x) + x; the identity-is-easy argument · `28:00` Gradient flow through the skip: ∂L/∂x = ∂L/∂y·(1 + ∂F/∂x) — derive it ⟵ D42 · `38:00` Bottleneck blocks, projection vs identity shortcuts (options A/B/C) · `48:00` ResNet-152 and the 3.57% ensemble; the 1202-layer overfitting note
▸ **Nail these:** degradation is a *training* problem — the whole paper hinges on this and it's constantly misreported as overfitting; the +1 in the gradient is the vanishing-gradient fix; ResNet-50's bottleneck (1×1 → 3×3 → 1×1) is the most-copied block in ML; the ensemble-of-shallow-nets interpretation (Veit et al. 2016) is worth a minute.

**D68 · Identity Mappings in Deep Residual Networks (pre-activation ResNet)** — He et al., *ECCV* 2016 (arXiv:1603.05027) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Moving BN and ReLU, and gaining a clean gradient path · `4:00` The ablation grid of five shortcut variants — read the table properly · `14:00` Deriving the clean backward path when the shortcut is a true identity · `24:00` Pre-activation: BN → ReLU → conv; why post-activation blocked the path · `30:00` ResNet-1001 trained successfully
▸ **Nail these:** any scaling, gating, or 1×1 on the shortcut degrades performance — the paper proves it empirically across five variants; the analysis showing x_L = x_l + Σ F is what makes the "unraveled ensemble" view precise; pre-activation ordering is what modern transformers copied as Pre-LN ⟶ D163.

**D69 · Wide Residual Networks** — Zagoruyko & Komodakis, *BMVC* 2016 (arXiv:1605.07146) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Depth isn't the only axis · `4:00` Widening factor k; WRN-28-10 vs ResNet-1001 · `12:00` Same accuracy, several times faster on GPU — the parallelism argument · `19:00` Dropout inside residual blocks, which works here and not elsewhere · `25:00` The diminishing-feature-reuse critique of very deep thin nets
▸ **Nail these:** GPU efficiency favors wide over deep at equal FLOPs — this is a hardware argument, and it's why it's underrated; WRN-28-10 has 36M parameters vs 10M for ResNet-1001 but trains ~8× faster; the depth-vs-width tradeoff resurfaces in EfficientNet ⟶ D76.

**D70 · Densely Connected Convolutional Networks (DenseNet)** — Huang, Liu, van der Maaten & Weinberger, *CVPR* 2017 (arXiv:1608.06993) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Concatenate instead of add · `4:00` L(L+1)/2 connections; growth rate k · `13:00` Why concatenation preserves information that addition destroys · `21:00` Transition layers, compression, bottlenecks (DenseNet-BC) · `27:00` The memory problem, and the shared-storage implementation fix
▸ **Nail these:** add vs concat is the single conceptual axis of this episode — make it vivid; parameter efficiency is real (fewer params, same accuracy) but memory cost is worse than ResNet due to stored activations; feature reuse heatmaps; k=12 to 32 typical.

**D71 · Aggregated Residual Transformations (ResNeXt)** — Xie, Girshick, Dollár, Tu & He, *CVPR* 2017 (arXiv:1611.05431) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` A new dial: cardinality · `4:00` Split-transform-merge, and its three equivalent formulations — show all three · `14:00` Grouped convolutions, from AlexNet's accident to a design choice ⟵ D61 · `21:00` Cardinality 32 beating depth and width at equal FLOPs · `26:00` Where grouped conv leads: MobileNet's depthwise, ShuffleNet
▸ **Nail these:** the three-equivalent-diagrams figure is the paper — put it on screen and walk each; cardinality vs width tradeoff table; grouped conv with groups = channels is depthwise conv ⟶ D73; VGG-style repetition + Inception-style branching, unified.

**D72 · Squeeze-and-Excitation Networks** — Hu, Shen & Sun, *CVPR* 2018 (arXiv:1709.01507) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Attention arrives in CNNs, for almost no compute · `4:00` Squeeze: global average pool to a per-channel scalar ⟵ D63 · `11:00` Excitation: two FC layers with reduction ratio r, sigmoid, rescale · `19:00` Channel attention as feature recalibration; <1% FLOP overhead · `25:00` ILSVRC 2017 win; CBAM and spatial attention as the follow-up
▸ **Nail these:** this is attention with a global receptive field inserted into a local architecture — say it that way and the transformer chapters later land harder; r=16 default; sigmoid not softmax, so channels aren't competing; SE blocks drop into any backbone, which is why they're everywhere.

**D73 · MobileNets: Efficient CNNs for Mobile Vision** — Howard et al., 2017 (arXiv:1704.04861) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Deep learning on a phone · `4:00` Depthwise separable convolution: split spatial from channel mixing · `13:00` The cost ratio 1/N + 1/D_k² — derive it, then compute it for 3×3 (≈ 8–9× cheaper) · `23:00` Width multiplier α and resolution multiplier ρ · `30:00` Why FLOPs ≠ latency, and what actually runs fast on hardware
▸ **Nail these:** do the FLOP derivation explicitly; depthwise = grouped conv with groups=channels ⟵ D71; accuracy/latency tradeoff curves; depthwise convs are memory-bandwidth-bound, so they underperform their FLOP count — the most useful practical caveat in this episode.

**D74 · MobileNetV2: Inverted Residuals and Linear Bottlenecks** — Sandler et al., *CVPR* 2018 (arXiv:1801.04381) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Two counterintuitive changes · `4:00` Inverted residual: skip connects the *narrow* layers, expand in the middle · `13:00` Linear bottleneck: no ReLU on the low-dimensional output, and the manifold-collapse argument · `22:00` The memory-efficient inference argument · `27:00` MobileNetV3 and hardware-aware NAS in one minute
▸ **Nail these:** the ReLU-destroys-information-in-low-dimensions experiment (their Figure 1) is the persuasive bit — show it; expansion factor 6; inverted residuals reduce peak memory, which is the actual deployment constraint; this block reappears in EfficientNet.

**D75 · ShuffleNet: An Extremely Efficient CNN for Mobile Devices** — Zhang, Zhou, Lin & Sun, *CVPR* 2018 (arXiv:1707.01083) · 25 min · L2
`0:00` The problem with stacking grouped convolutions · `4:00` Information isolation between groups, drawn · `10:00` Channel shuffle: a reshape-transpose-reshape, and it's differentiable and free · `17:00` ShuffleNetV2's four practical guidelines — memory access cost, not FLOPs · `22:00` The lesson: measure latency on the target device
▸ **Nail these:** channel shuffle is three tensor ops and zero parameters — implement it live in five lines; the V2 guidelines (equal channel width minimizes MAC, avoid excessive group conv, avoid fragmentation, avoid element-wise ops) are more useful than V1 itself.

**D76 · EfficientNet: Rethinking Model Scaling** — Tan & Le, *ICML* 2019 (arXiv:1905.11946) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Scaling depth, width, and resolution together, with one coefficient · `5:00` The compound scaling rule: d=α^φ, w=β^φ, r=γ^φ with αβ²γ² ≈ 2 · `16:00` Why that constraint keeps FLOPs ~2^φ — derive it · `24:00` The NAS-found EfficientNet-B0 baseline (MBConv + SE) ⟵ D72, D74 · `32:00` B7: 84.3% top-1 at 8.4× fewer parameters than the previous SOTA · `36:00` EfficientNetV2 and the training-speed correction
▸ **Nail these:** the grid search for α, β, γ is done once at φ=1 and then extrapolated — that's the actual method; resolution scaling drives most of the gain and also most of the memory cost; the "fewer FLOPs but slower in practice" critique is legitimate and V2 concedes it.

**D77 · Designing Network Design Spaces (RegNet)** — Radosavovic, Kosaraju, Girshick, He & Dollár, *CVPR* 2020 (arXiv:2003.13678) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Search for design *principles*, not a single architecture · `5:00` Design spaces and error-EDF curves as the analysis tool · `15:00` Progressive simplification: AnyNet → RegNet, one constraint at a time · `26:00` The quantized linear width rule u_j = w_0 + w_a·j · `31:00` Why this beats NAS for interpretability, and the "NAS finds points, this finds spaces" framing
▸ **Nail these:** the EDF plot is an unfamiliar but excellent tool — teach it properly; the discovered rules (shared bottleneck ratio, shared group width, increasing width and depth) are human-readable, which is the point; good depth saturates around 20 blocks; contrast honestly with Day 76's NAS approach.

**D78 · A ConvNet for the 2020s (ConvNeXt)** — Liu, Mao, Wu, Feichtenhofer, Darrell & Xie, *CVPR* 2022 (arXiv:2201.03545) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Modernizing ResNet until it matches Swin · `5:00` The full roadmap, change by change, with the accuracy delta for each — this table *is* the paper · `18:00` Stage ratios, patchify stem, depthwise 7×7, inverted bottleneck · `28:00` Fewer normalizations, LN instead of BN, GELU instead of ReLU, separate downsampling layers · `38:00` The conclusion: training recipe and macro design, not self-attention, explained most of the ViT gap
▸ **Nail these:** put the step-by-step accuracy ladder on screen and narrate each rung — 78.8% → 82.0%; the training recipe alone (AdamW, 300 epochs, Mixup/CutMix, stochastic depth) is worth ~2.7 points before any architecture change, which is the most important number in this month; large-kernel depthwise conv as a local approximation of attention.

**D79 · Deformable Convolutional Networks** — Dai et al., *ICCV* 2017 (arXiv:1703.06211) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Sampling grids that bend to the object · `4:00` Learned 2D offsets per sampling location · `12:00` Bilinear interpolation makes it differentiable — this is the mechanism, spend time here · `20:00` Deformable RoI pooling · `25:00` v2's modulation scalars; effective receptive field visualizations
▸ **Nail these:** the offsets are predicted by a parallel conv branch and are fractional, hence bilinear sampling; the receptive-field visualizations adapting to object scale are the money figure; this is spatial adaptivity without attention's quadratic cost; relates to Spatial Transformers ⟶ D81.

**D80 · Multi-Scale Context Aggregation by Dilated Convolutions** — Yu & Koltun, *ICLR* 2016 (arXiv:1511.07122) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Growing the receptive field without losing resolution · `4:00` Dilation rate; the exponential receptive-field growth, computed · `13:00` Why pooling is wrong for dense prediction · `20:00` The gridding artifact and the hybrid-dilation-rate fix · `25:00` Where this goes: DeepLab's ASPP ⟶ D93, WaveNet's causal dilations ⟶ D256
▸ **Nail these:** with rates 1,2,4,8 the receptive field is (2^{n+2}−1)² — derive it; dilated conv adds zero parameters and zero FLOPs relative to the same kernel size; the gridding/checkerboard artifact is real and under-discussed; identity initialization for the context module.

**D81 · Spatial Transformer Networks** — Jaderberg, Simonyan, Zisserman & Kavukcuoglu, *NIPS* 2015 (arXiv:1506.02025) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` A module that learns to crop, rotate, and straighten · `5:00` Three parts: localisation net, grid generator, sampler · `15:00` The affine parameter θ and the inverse-warping convention (grid on the *output*) · `24:00` Differentiable bilinear sampling — derive ∂/∂θ · `30:00` Distorted-MNIST results; why it's now mostly a component, not a headline
▸ **Nail these:** the sampling grid maps output coordinates back to input coordinates — getting this direction backwards is the classic implementation bug; the gradient through bilinear interpolation is the crux; thin-plate-spline variant; `grid_sample` in PyTorch is literally this paper.

**D82 · Rich Feature Hierarchies for Accurate Object Detection (R-CNN)** — Girshick, Donahue, Darrell & Malik, *CVPR* 2014 (arXiv:1311.2524) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Detection jumps 30% in one paper · `5:00` The pipeline: selective search → warp → CNN features → SVM per class → bbox regression · `18:00` Supervised pretraining then domain-specific fine-tuning — transfer learning's founding demonstration · `28:00` IoU, positives/negatives, hard negative mining, NMS · `36:00` 47 seconds per image, and why the next three papers exist
▸ **Nail these:** 2000 forward passes per image is the bottleneck the whole lineage attacks; why SVMs instead of the softmax head (they explain it and it's a good methodological story); bbox regression on pool5 features; mAP and IoU definitions done properly here, since you'll reuse them for ten episodes.

**D83 · Fast R-CNN** — Girshick, *ICCV* 2015 (arXiv:1504.08083) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` One forward pass instead of two thousand · `4:00` Sharing the conv feature map across proposals · `12:00` RoI pooling: the quantization, done on a grid, on screen · `21:00` Multi-task loss: classification + smooth-L1 localization, jointly trained · `29:00` 213× faster inference; selective search is now 90% of the runtime
▸ **Nail these:** RoI pooling's two quantization steps are what RoIAlign later fixes ⟶ D89; smooth L1 vs L2 and why outlier robustness matters for boxes; end-to-end single-stage training replaces R-CNN's three-stage pipeline; the remaining bottleneck sets up tomorrow perfectly.

**D84 · Faster R-CNN: Towards Real-Time Object Detection with RPNs** — Ren, He, Girshick & Sun, *NIPS* 2015 (arXiv:1506.01497) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Making the proposals themselves a neural network · `5:00` The Region Proposal Network as a sliding 3×3 over the feature map · `14:00` Anchors: 3 scales × 3 aspect ratios, translation-invariant references · `26:00` Anchor assignment rules, the RPN loss, and the 4-step alternating training · `36:00` 5 fps, 0.2s/image; the two-stage template that lasted five years
▸ **Nail these:** anchors are the key abstraction — regression is relative to a fixed reference box, parameterized as (t_x, t_y, t_w, t_h); the alternating training scheme and its later simplification to joint training; positive/negative IoU thresholds 0.7/0.3; nearly every detector until DETR uses anchors.

**D85 · You Only Look Once: Unified, Real-Time Object Detection (YOLO)** — Redmon, Divvala, Girshick & Farhadi, *CVPR* 2016 (arXiv:1506.02640) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Detection as a single regression problem · `5:00` The S×S×(B·5 + C) output tensor, decoded on screen · `16:00` The multi-part loss with λ_coord and λ_noobj, and the √w, √h trick · `27:00` 45 fps; fewer background false positives than Fast R-CNN, worse localization · `34:00` v2/v3 in brief: anchors return, multi-scale, Darknet-53
▸ **Nail these:** each grid cell predicts one class — the paper's main structural limitation, bad for small clustered objects; the square-root on width/height is there to equalize error weighting across scales; λ_noobj = 0.5 handles class imbalance crudely, which is exactly what Day 87 solves properly.

**D86 · SSD: Single Shot MultiBox Detector** — Liu et al., *ECCV* 2016 (arXiv:1512.02325) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` YOLO's speed with anchor-based accuracy · `4:00` Multi-scale feature maps: default boxes on conv4_3 through conv11_2 · `13:00` The scale formula across layers, computed · `20:00` Hard negative mining at 3:1, and matching strategy · `26:00` Why small objects still fail — insufficient semantic depth in early layers ⟶ D88
▸ **Nail these:** predicting from multiple feature maps is the core idea and the direct ancestor of FPN; aspect ratios {1, 2, 3, 1/2, 1/3} plus an extra scale-1 box; the small-object weakness is a feature-semantics problem, not a resolution problem — that distinction is what makes FPN's answer non-obvious.

**D87 · Focal Loss for Dense Object Detection (RetinaNet)** — Lin, Goyal, Girshick, He & Dollár, *ICCV* 2017 (arXiv:1708.02002) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Why one-stage detectors were stuck below two-stage · `5:00` The class imbalance diagnosis: ~100k anchors, a handful positive · `14:00` Focal loss FL = −(1−p_t)^γ log(p_t), plotted for γ = 0, 0.5, 1, 2, 5 · `24:00` Why easy negatives dominate the gradient even when individually small · `31:00` The prior-probability initialization trick (π = 0.01) and why training diverges without it · `36:00` RetinaNet = ResNet + FPN + focal loss beats two-stage
▸ **Nail these:** plot the modulating factor and read off the down-weighting at p_t=0.9 (γ=2 gives 100×); α-balancing and focal loss are complementary, not alternatives; the bias-init detail is a great "the paper's footnote is the whole trick" moment; class imbalance is the unifying theme.

**D88 · Feature Pyramid Networks for Object Detection** — Lin, Dollár, Girshick, He, Hariharan & Belongie, *CVPR* 2017 (arXiv:1612.03144) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Four pyramid designs, compared in one figure · `5:00` The problem: high-resolution features are semantically weak ⟵ D86 · `13:00` Top-down pathway + lateral 1×1 connections, drawn · `22:00` Assigning RoIs to pyramid levels by size (the k = k₀ + log₂(√wh/224) rule) · `29:00` Near-free: same backbone, marginal cost, large gains on small objects
▸ **Nail these:** the four-panel figure (a: image pyramid, b: single map, c: pyramidal features, d: FPN) is the fastest way to teach this — walk all four; nearest-neighbor upsample + element-wise add + 3×3 smoothing; FPN is in essentially every detector afterwards, including Day 97's SAM decoder lineage.

**D89 · Mask R-CNN** — He, Gkioxari, Dollár & Girshick, *ICCV* 2017 (arXiv:1703.06870) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Instance segmentation by adding one branch · `5:00` RoIAlign: removing both quantizations from RoI pooling, with bilinear sampling ⟵ D83 · `16:00` The +10% relative gain from RoIAlign alone under strict IoU — the ablation that matters · `24:00` Decoupled mask prediction: per-class binary masks with sigmoid, not softmax · `32:00` Extending to keypoints; the one-hot mask trick
▸ **Nail these:** draw the RoI grid with and without quantization side by side — this visual is the episode; decoupling mask from class prediction avoids inter-class competition, and the ablation proves it; the mask branch is an FCN ⟵ D91 preview; Mask R-CNN generalizes to pose with a trivial change, which is the strongest evidence for the design.

**D90 · End-to-End Object Detection with Transformers (DETR)** — Carion, Massa, Synnaeve, Usunier, Kirillov & Zagoruyko, *ECCV* 2020 (arXiv:2005.12872) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Deleting anchors and NMS entirely · `6:00` CNN backbone → transformer encoder-decoder → N object queries · `16:00` Bipartite matching with the Hungarian algorithm — this is the real contribution, work through a small example · `28:00` The set prediction loss; ∅ (no-object) class · `35:00` The 500-epoch training problem and slow convergence; Deformable DETR's fix · `41:00` Why "no hand-designed components" was the headline claim
▸ **Nail these:** Hungarian matching makes the loss permutation-invariant, which is what removes the need for NMS — say exactly why; object queries are learned positional embeddings, and their learned spatial specialization is a great figure; poor small-object performance in v1; auxiliary decoding losses at every decoder layer are load-bearing.

---

# MONTH 4 — Computer Vision II: Dense Prediction, 3D, and the Transformer Takeover
### Days 91–120 · Level 2–3 · Segmentation, video, 3D, and the arrival of ViT

Two arcs this month. First, dense prediction — where every pixel needs an answer and the encoder–decoder shape gets invented. Second, the moment attention walks into vision and the field reorganizes around it.

---

**D91 · Fully Convolutional Networks for Semantic Segmentation** — Long, Shelhamer & Darrell, *CVPR* 2015 (arXiv:1411.4038) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Classification networks, converted to pixel labelers · `5:00` Turning FC layers into convolutions — do the reshape explicitly, it's the key move · `15:00` Transposed convolution ("deconvolution") and the checkerboard artifact · `25:00` Skip connections: FCN-32s → 16s → 8s, with the output quality at each · `33:00` Per-pixel cross-entropy; mIoU defined properly
▸ **Nail these:** convolutionalization means any input size works and you get a heatmap for free — demonstrate on a big image; transposed conv is not a mathematical inverse, and the checkerboard artifact comes from uneven overlap (Odena et al. 2016); FCN-8s vs 32s comparison is the argument for skips; mIoU vs pixel accuracy and why the latter lies on imbalanced classes.

**D92 · U-Net: Convolutional Networks for Biomedical Image Segmentation** — Ronneberger, Fischer & Brox, *MICCAI* 2015 (arXiv:1505.04597) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` The most-cited architecture diagram in machine learning · `5:00` Symmetric contracting/expanding paths; concatenation, not addition ⟵ D70 · `15:00` Overlap-tile strategy and mirror padding for arbitrarily large images · `23:00` The weighted loss for touching cells — the borders trick nobody remembers · `30:00` Trained on 30 images: elastic deformation as the enabler · `36:00` Why U-Net is now the backbone of every diffusion model ⟶ D260
▸ **Nail these:** skip connections carry high-frequency spatial detail the bottleneck destroys; the pixel-weight map w(x) with the exponential border term is genuinely clever and always skipped; valid-padding means output is smaller than input in the original; the diffusion connection is worth stating loudly.

**D93 · DeepLab: Atrous Convolution and Fully Connected CRFs** — Chen, Papandreou, Kokkinos, Murphy & Yuille, *TPAMI* 2018 (arXiv:1606.00915) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Three problems with FCN, three fixes · `5:00` Atrous convolution to keep resolution without losing receptive field ⟵ D80 · `15:00` ASPP: parallel dilation rates for multi-scale context · `24:00` The fully connected CRF as post-processing; mean-field inference in brief · `33:00` Output stride 8 vs 16, and the accuracy/memory tradeoff
▸ **Nail these:** atrous conv lets you reuse ImageNet weights at higher output resolution — that reuse is the practical argument; ASPP rates {6,12,18,24}; the CRF sharpens boundaries and was dropped by v3+ because the network learned to do it; output stride is the single most important knob in segmentation deployment.

**D94 · Encoder-Decoder with Atrous Separable Convolution (DeepLabv3+)** — Chen, Zhu, Papandreou, Schroff & Adam, *ECCV* 2018 (arXiv:1802.02611) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Merging the two segmentation families · `4:00` DeepLabv3's ASPP encoder plus a simple decoder module · `12:00` Depthwise separable atrous conv ⟵ D73 — the cost saving computed · `20:00` Xception backbone modifications · `25:00` The low-level-feature 1×1 projection detail (48 channels) that makes the decoder work
▸ **Nail these:** the decoder is deliberately minimal — two 3×3 convs — and that's a finding, not laziness; 4× upsample, concat, 4× upsample; projecting low-level features down to 48 channels prevents them from swamping the ASPP output, and the ablation shows it; this is the practical SOTA baseline for years.

**D95 · Pyramid Scene Parsing Network (PSPNet)** — Zhao, Shi, Qi, Wang & Jia, *CVPR* 2017 (arXiv:1612.01105) · 25 min · L2
`0:00` Failure cases as a design method — start with their error taxonomy · `4:00` Mismatched relationships, confusion categories, inconspicuous classes · `11:00` The pyramid pooling module: 1×1, 2×2, 3×3, 6×6 adaptive pooling, concatenated · `18:00` Auxiliary loss at an intermediate layer, weight 0.4 · `22:00` Global context vs local: the boat-on-the-river example
▸ **Nail these:** the error analysis opening is a model for how to write a paper — teach the methodology, not just the module; PPM is global context injection, conceptually adjacent to ASPP but pooling-based rather than dilation-based; the deep supervision auxiliary loss is load-bearing here, unlike Inception's ⟵ D65.

**D96 · Panoptic Segmentation** — Kirillov, He, Girshick, Rother & Dollár, *CVPR* 2019 (arXiv:1801.00868) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Things vs stuff, and why the field split in two · `4:00` The unified task definition: every pixel gets a label and an instance id, non-overlapping · `12:00` Panoptic Quality = Segmentation Quality × Recognition Quality — derive the decomposition · `22:00` Human consistency study; the baseline heuristic merge · `27:00` Panoptic FPN and Mask2Former in one minute
▸ **Nail these:** PQ = (Σ IoU over TP) / (TP + ½FP + ½FN) and its clean factorization into SQ×RQ; the IoU > 0.5 matching is provably unique — a nice small proof; the non-overlapping constraint is what makes this hard for instance-segmentation methods; this episode is really about task design and metric design, which is rarer content than architecture.

**D97 · Segment Anything** — Kirillov et al., *ICCV* 2023 (arXiv:2304.02643) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` A foundation model for a task nobody had defined · `6:00` The promptable segmentation task; points, boxes, masks, text · `15:00` Architecture: MAE-pretrained ViT encoder ⟵ D118, prompt encoder, lightweight mask decoder (~50ms) · `26:00` Ambiguity handling: predict 3 masks, backprop only the lowest-loss one · `33:00` The data engine — assisted-manual → semi-automatic → fully automatic, 1.1B masks · `40:00` Zero-shot transfer results, and what SAM cannot do
▸ **Nail these:** the data engine is the paper's real contribution and it's a *process* contribution, not an architecture one; the min-over-3-masks loss is how they make an ill-posed task trainable; the decoder is deliberately tiny so the expensive encoder runs once per image; SAM has no semantics — it segments, it doesn't name, and that limitation is instructive.

**D98 · Two-Stream Convolutional Networks for Action Recognition** — Simonyan & Zisserman, *NIPS* 2014 (arXiv:1406.2199) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Video is not just images · `4:00` Spatial stream (appearance) and temporal stream (optical flow) · `12:00` Stacking L flow frames as a 2L-channel input; the two flow-stacking variants · `20:00` Late fusion; multi-task training across UCF-101 and HMDB-51 · `25:00` Why hand-computed optical flow was both the strength and the bottleneck ⟶ D107
▸ **Nail these:** the biological two-pathway motivation; optical flow as explicit motion supervision the network doesn't have to learn; precomputing flow is expensive and non-differentiable, which is exactly why the field moved to 3D convs; multi-task training as a small-data fix.

**D99 · Quo Vadis, Action Recognition? (I3D)** — Carreira & Zisserman, *CVPR* 2017 (arXiv:1705.07750) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Kinetics, and the ImageNet moment for video · `4:00` Inflating 2D kernels to 3D: N×N → N×N×N by repeating and rescaling · `13:00` Why inflation lets you bootstrap from ImageNet weights — the key trick · `20:00` Two-stream I3D: 3D convs *and* flow, since both still helped · `25:00` C3D and R(2+1)D as the neighbours
▸ **Nail these:** the bootstrapping trick (repeat weights along time, divide by N to preserve activation scale) is elegant and takes two minutes to explain; Kinetics pretraining is worth more than the architecture — the recurring lesson of this series; R(2+1)D factorizes 3D into 2D spatial + 1D temporal for the same reason Inception factorizes ⟵ D66.

**D100 · SlowFast Networks for Video Recognition** — Feichtenhofer, Fan, Malik & He, *ICCV* 2019 (arXiv:1812.03982) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Two frame rates, one network · `4:00` Slow pathway: low frame rate, high capacity, semantics · `11:00` Fast pathway: high frame rate, low channel capacity (β=1/8), motion · `19:00` Lateral connections and the three fusion variants · `24:00` The retinal M/P-cell analogy, and how much weight to put on it
▸ **Nail these:** the Fast pathway is ~20% of total compute despite 8× the frames, because channels were cut — that asymmetry is the design; no optical flow anywhere, and it still beats two-stream; α=8, β=1/8 as defaults; be measured about the neuroscience analogy — it's post-hoc motivation.

**D101 · Is Space-Time Attention All You Need for Video Understanding? (TimeSformer)** — Bertasius, Wang & Torresani, *ICML* 2021 (arXiv:2102.05095) · 30 min · L3
`0:00` Attention over spacetime, and the combinatorial problem · `4:00` Five attention schemes compared: space-only, joint space-time, divided, sparse local-global, axial · `14:00` Divided space-time attention: temporal then spatial, and the cost saving computed · `22:00` Long-range video and the linear-in-frames scaling claim · `27:00` Video Swin as the windowed alternative
▸ **Nail these:** joint space-time attention is O((HW·T)²) and infeasible — put the number on screen for a real clip; divided attention decomposes it into O(T²) + O((HW)²); the empirical ablation across five schemes is the paper; ImageNet-pretrained ViT weights are required, again the pretraining lesson.

**D102 · PointNet: Deep Learning on Point Sets** — Qi, Su, Mo & Guibas, *CVPR* 2017 (arXiv:1612.00593) · 40 min · L3
