`0:00` Neural networks on unordered sets · `5:00` The three properties: permutation invariance, transformation invariance, point interactions · `14:00` Shared MLP + max pooling as a symmetric function · `22:00` The universal approximation theorem for set functions — sketch the proof · `31:00` T-Net alignment modules and the orthogonality regularizer · `36:00` Critical point sets and the upper-bound shapes — the interpretability result
▸ **Nail these:** max pooling is the whole permutation-invariance mechanism, and the theorem says it's enough; the critical-points visualization (only ~100 points determine the output) is a genuinely surprising robustness result; T-Net on features needs regularization or it destabilizes; Deep Sets (Zaheer et al. 2017) proves the general version — mention it.

**D103 · PointNet++: Deep Hierarchical Feature Learning on Point Sets** — Qi, Yi, Su & Guibas, *NIPS* 2017 (arXiv:1706.02413) · 30 min · L3
`0:00` PointNet has no local structure — that's the whole motivation · `4:00` Set abstraction: farthest point sampling → ball query grouping → PointNet · `14:00` Multi-scale and multi-resolution grouping for non-uniform density · `22:00` Feature propagation for segmentation (inverse distance interpolation) · `27:00` Where 3D went next: sparse convs, transformers, splatting
▸ **Nail these:** FPS gives better coverage than random sampling and is the standard 3D primitive; ball query vs kNN — ball query gives fixed-scale regions, which generalizes better; random input dropout during training is what makes MSG robust; this is a CNN hierarchy rebuilt for irregular data, and framing it that way makes it easy.

**D104 · NeRF: Representing Scenes as Neural Radiance Fields** — Mildenhall, Srinivasan, Tancik, Barron, Ramamoorthi & Ng, *ECCV* 2020 (arXiv:2003.08934) · 55 min · L3
`0:00` Photorealistic novel views from an MLP · `6:00` The 5D function (x, y, z, θ, φ) → (RGB, σ) · `14:00` Volume rendering: the transmittance integral, then its discrete quadrature form — derive both, this is the episode · `28:00` Positional encoding and the spectral bias fix ⟵ (link forward to D318) · `38:00` Hierarchical coarse/fine sampling · `45:00` View-dependence: why direction enters late in the MLP · `50:00` One scene per network, 1–2 days of training — the limitation that spawned a field
▸ **Nail these:** the discrete rendering equation with T_i = exp(−Σ σδ) must appear on screen and be walked term by term; positional encoding is what lets an MLP represent high-frequency detail — connect to Fourier features (Tancik et al. 2020); density σ is view-independent by construction and that's a deliberate physical constraint; the whole thing is differentiable, which is why it works at all.

**D105 · Instant Neural Graphics Primitives (Instant-NGP)** — Müller, Evans, Schied & Keller, *SIGGRAPH* 2022 (arXiv:2201.05989) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Days to seconds · `5:00` Multiresolution hash encoding — the data structure, drawn · `16:00` Why hash collisions are *fine*: gradient averaging resolves them implicitly · `26:00` Tiny MLP (2 layers, 64 wide) because the encoding does the work · `33:00` Fused CUDA kernels; the systems half of the speedup
▸ **Nail these:** collisions are not resolved — important points dominate the gradient and unimportant ones lose, which is a beautiful piece of engineering reasoning; L=16 levels, T=2^19 table size, F=2 features; the split between "the encoding carries capacity" and "the MLP carries smoothness"; this is as much a GPU paper as an ML paper, so cover the kernel fusion honestly.

**D106 · 3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering** — Kerbl, Kopanas, Leimkühler & Drettakis, *SIGGRAPH* 2023 (arXiv:2308.04079) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` NeRF quality at 100+ fps, with no neural network in the representation · `6:00` 3D Gaussians: position, anisotropic covariance, opacity, SH coefficients · `16:00` The covariance parameterization as R S Sᵀ Rᵀ, and why you must parameterize it that way to stay PSD · `26:00` Differentiable tile-based rasterization and the sorted alpha blending · `35:00` Adaptive densification: cloning vs splitting under- and over-reconstruction · `41:00` Explicit vs implicit representations — the pendulum swing
▸ **Nail these:** the PSD parameterization argument is the one piece of real math and it's often skipped; spherical harmonics give view-dependent color without a network; approximate global sorting per tile is the trick that makes it fast and it's an approximation — say so; storage cost (hundreds of MB) is the real drawback.

**D107 · RAFT: Recurrent All-Pairs Field Transforms for Optical Flow** — Teed & Deng, *ECCV* 2020 (arXiv:2003.12039) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Optical flow, solved cleanly ⟵ D98 · `5:00` The all-pairs correlation volume: every pixel against every pixel · `14:00` The 4-level correlation pyramid and lookup operator · `22:00` A GRU-based iterative update operator with shared weights — an unrolled optimizer · `29:00` Why iterative refinement generalizes better than coarse-to-fine warping
▸ **Nail these:** the correlation volume is H×W×H×W and they compute it *once* — memory analysis worth doing on screen; the recurrent update with tied weights is a learned optimizer, which links to the general "unrolled optimization" idea from Day 54; FlowNet 2015 as the predecessor; small-and-fast motion is the classic failure of pyramid methods that this avoids.

**D108 · Distinctive Image Features from Scale-Invariant Keypoints (SIFT)** — Lowe, *IJCV* 2004 · 45 min · L1
`0:00` What computer vision was before learning · `6:00` Scale space, difference-of-Gaussians, extrema detection · `18:00` Keypoint localization, edge rejection via the Hessian ratio · `26:00` Orientation assignment for rotation invariance · `33:00` The 128-D descriptor: 4×4 cells × 8 orientation bins, built on screen · `40:00` The ratio test for matching, and why 0.8 is the magic number
▸ **Nail these:** the descriptor construction is worth drawing carefully — it's the design ancestor of every patch embedding; DoG as an efficient LoG approximation; the ratio test is a great lesson in relative vs absolute thresholds; SIFT still wins on some geometric matching tasks in 2026, which is worth an honest minute.

**D109 · Histograms of Oriented Gradients for Human Detection** — Dalal & Triggs, *CVPR* 2005 · 30 min · L1
`0:00` Feature engineering at its best · `4:00` Gradient computation, orientation binning, cells and blocks · `13:00` Block normalization and the overlap — the ablation that mattered most · `20:00` Linear SVM on top ⟵ D14; sliding window + NMS · `25:00` INRIA dataset; hard negative mining
▸ **Nail these:** their ablation study is a masterclass — every design choice is justified by a number; L2-Hys normalization; the pipeline (dense features → linear classifier → sliding window) is exactly what R-CNN replaced ⟵ D82; deformable part models as the follow-up worth naming.

**D110 · Rapid Object Detection using a Boosted Cascade of Simple Features (Viola–Jones)** — Viola & Jones, *CVPR* 2001 · 35 min · L1
`0:00` Real-time face detection in 2001, on a 700MHz CPU · `5:00` Haar-like features and the integral image — O(1) rectangle sums, derived · `15:00` AdaBoost as feature selection, not just classification ⟵ D11 · `24:00` The attentional cascade: reject 50% of windows in 2 features · `31:00` The math of cascade FP/detection rates, and why this is the ancestor of early-exit networks
▸ **Nail these:** the integral image trick is four array lookups per rectangle — show it; each weak classifier is a single-feature decision stump, so boosting *is* the feature selector; cascade stage rates multiply (0.99^10 detection, 0.3^10 false positive) — do the arithmetic; this is still what your camera's face box uses in some embedded devices.

**D111 · ImageNet: A Large-Scale Hierarchical Image Database** — Deng, Dong, Socher, Li, Li & Fei-Fei, *CVPR* 2009 · 35 min · L1
`0:00` The dataset that mattered more than any architecture in this month · `5:00` WordNet synsets as the label hierarchy · `13:00` Amazon Mechanical Turk, the multi-vote consensus protocol, quality control · `22:00` ILSVRC-2012: 1000 classes, 1.28M images — a *subset*, and people forget that · `28:00` Known label noise, the 2020 "Are we done with ImageNet?" re-annotation · `32:00` Dataset work as first-class research
▸ **Nail these:** the full ImageNet is ~14M images and 21k+ classes; top-5 error exists because of intra-class ambiguity in the label set; ImageNetV2 (Recht et al. 2019) showed accuracy drops on a fresh test set — a crucial generalization lesson; Beyer et al. 2020 on multi-label reality. Also note the 2019 face-blurring and category-removal work, briefly and factually.

**D112 · mixup: Beyond Empirical Risk Minimization** — Zhang, Cisse, Dauphin & Lopez-Paz, *ICLR* 2018 (arXiv:1710.09412) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Training on images that don't exist · `4:00` x̃ = λx_i + (1−λ)x_j, ỹ = λy_i + (1−λ)y_j, λ ~ Beta(α, α) · `12:00` Vicinal risk minimization — the theoretical framing · `19:00` Effects: better calibration, adversarial robustness, memorization resistance · `25:00` Cutout as the complementary occlusion-based augmentation
▸ **Nail these:** mixing the *labels* is the part people drop and it's essential; α ∈ [0.1, 0.4] typical, and α→∞ degrades; mixup reduces overconfidence — a real calibration result ⟶ D326; it works between random classes, which is the counterintuitive bit; the linear-interpolation-of-behavior inductive bias.

**D113 · CutMix and RandAugment** — Yun et al. *ICCV* 2019 (arXiv:1905.04899); Cubuk et al. *NeurIPS* 2020 (arXiv:1909.13719) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Two papers that are worth ~2 points on ImageNet, free · `4:00` CutMix: paste a patch, mix labels by area ratio · `13:00` Why CutMix beats both Cutout and mixup on localization tasks · `20:00` AutoAugment's RL search, and its 15,000 GPU-hour cost · `26:00` RandAugment: two hyperparameters (N, M), grid-searchable, matches AutoAugment · `31:00` The meta-lesson about expensive search vs a good simple baseline
▸ **Nail these:** CutMix's label mixing is by pixel area, which makes it locally honest in a way mixup isn't; RandAugment removes the search space entirely and *still* matches — one of the best "was the search necessary?" results in ML; augmentation strength must scale with model size (RandAugment shows this); these are in every recipe from ConvNeXt ⟵ D78 onward.

**D114 · How Transferable Are Features in Deep Neural Networks?** — Yosinski, Clune, Bengio & Lipson, *NIPS* 2014 (arXiv:1411.1792) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` The experiment everyone should have run and one team did · `5:00` The AnB / AnB+ / BnB / BnB+ notation, explained carefully · `14:00` Two distinct causes of transfer loss: co-adaptation splitting vs specialization · `24:00` The transferability curve across layers, and the dip in the middle · `30:00` Random-features baseline; distant vs nearby task pairs
▸ **Nail these:** fine-tuning (the "+" conditions) recovers and often *exceeds* baseline, and that's the headline for practice; the mid-layer dip is caused by fragile co-adaptation, which is a distinct phenomenon from specialization — the paper separates them cleanly and that's the intellectual contribution; first layers are Gabor filters regardless of task ⟵ D54.

**D115 · An Image is Worth 16x16 Words (Vision Transformer)** — Dosovitskiy et al., *ICLR* 2021 (arXiv:2010.11929) · 55 min · L2
`0:00` A transformer with almost no vision-specific design, winning · `6:00` Patchify: 16×16 patches → linear projection → sequence of tokens · `16:00` Class token, learnable 1D position embeddings, standard encoder ⟵ D129 · `26:00` The data-scaling result: worse than ResNet on ImageNet-1k, better on JFT-300M — the central figure · `36:00` Inductive bias as a data-efficiency tradeoff, stated precisely · `44:00` Attention distance analysis: ViT learns local attention in early layers · `50:00` Hybrid variants and what didn't matter
▸ **Nail these:** the crossover plot is the paper — put it on screen and spend real time on it; ViT has no translation equivariance and no locality prior, and it learns both from data given enough of it; 2D-aware position embeddings gave no gain, which is a striking negative result; the mean attention distance figure is the evidence for "it learns to be a CNN early on."

**D116 · Training Data-Efficient Image Transformers (DeiT)** — Touvron, Cord, Douze, Massa, Sablayrolles & Jégou, *ICML* 2021 (arXiv:2012.12877) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` ViT on ImageNet-1k alone, in three days, on eight GPUs · `5:00` The recipe: RandAugment, mixup, CutMix, repeated augmentation, stochastic depth ⟵ D112, D113 · `15:00` The distillation token — a second learnable token supervised by a teacher ⟵ D331 · `24:00` Hard vs soft distillation, and why hard won here · `29:00` A CNN teacher beats a transformer teacher — the inductive-bias-by-proxy result
▸ **Nail these:** DeiT's real contribution is a training recipe, and admitting that is the honest framing; the distillation token learns something different from the class token (their agreement analysis shows it); a RegNet teacher outperforms a ViT teacher, which is a lovely piece of evidence that distillation transfers inductive bias; repeated augmentation is an underrated trick.

**D117 · Swin Transformer: Hierarchical Vision Transformer using Shifted Windows** — Liu et al., *ICCV* 2021 (arXiv:2103.14030) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Making ViT work for detection and segmentation · `6:00` Window attention: O(n) instead of O(n²) in image size — do the arithmetic · `16:00` Shifted windows for cross-window connection, drawn over two consecutive blocks · `26:00` The cyclic-shift + masked-attention implementation trick · `34:00` Patch merging for a hierarchical feature pyramid ⟵ D88 · `40:00` Relative position bias, and why it beats absolute here
▸ **Nail these:** the shifted-window figure needs to be drawn slowly — it's the single hardest visual in the month and most explanations rush it; the cyclic shift avoids padding and keeps batch shapes fixed, which is why it's fast; hierarchy is what makes Swin a drop-in backbone for dense tasks, which ViT was not; W=7 default.

**D118 · Masked Autoencoders Are Scalable Vision Learners (MAE)** — He, Chen, Xie, Li, Dollár & Girshick, *CVPR* 2022 (arXiv:2111.06377) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Mask 75% of an image and reconstruct it ⟵ D53 · `6:00` The asymmetric encoder-decoder: encoder sees only visible patches · `16:00` Why 75% and not 15% — the information-density argument versus language · `26:00` Reconstruction in pixel space, with per-patch normalization · `33:00` 3× faster pretraining; the fine-tuning vs linear-probe gap · `39:00` MAE vs contrastive learning: what each representation is good for
▸ **Nail these:** the encoder processing only 25% of tokens is where the speedup comes from, and it's only possible because ViT accepts arbitrary-length sequences; the masking-ratio ablation curve is the paper's most quoted figure; per-patch pixel normalization as the target improves results, a small detail with real effect; linear probing underrates MAE — say why that's a measurement artifact.

**D119 · BEiT: BERT Pre-Training of Image Transformers** — Bao, Dong, Piao & Wei, *ICLR* 2022 (arXiv:2106.08254) · 30 min · L3
`0:00` Predicting discrete tokens instead of pixels · `4:00` The dVAE tokenizer (from DALL·E ⟵ D265) turning patches into a visual vocabulary · `13:00` Masked image modeling on token ids; the BERT analogy made literal ⟵ D137 · `21:00` Why discrete targets avoid wasting capacity on high-frequency pixel noise · `26:00` BEiT vs MAE: the target-space debate, and how it resolved
▸ **Nail these:** the two-stage dependency on a pretrained tokenizer is BEiT's cost and MAE's absence of it is MAE's elegance — frame the comparison this way; blockwise masking rather than random; the argument that pixel-level regression is "too easy/too noisy" is exactly what MAE disputes, so present it as a live disagreement.

**D120 · Emerging Properties in Self-Supervised ViTs (DINO) + DINOv2** — Caron et al. *ICCV* 2021 (arXiv:2104.14294); Oquab et al. 2023 (arXiv:2304.07193) · 50 min · L3
`0:00` The attention maps that segment objects with zero labels · `6:00` Self-distillation with no labels: student, EMA teacher, same architecture · `16:00` Collapse and the two fixes — centering and sharpening, and why you need both · `27:00` Multi-crop: 2 global + several local views, and the local-to-global correspondence loss · `35:00` k-NN classification at 78.3% with a frozen backbone — the headline result · `41:00` DINOv2: curated 142M-image data pipeline, scaling, and general-purpose frozen features
▸ **Nail these:** centering prevents one dimension dominating, sharpening prevents uniform output — they push against each other and that balance *is* the anti-collapse mechanism; there's no negative pairs and no contrastive loss, which is the surprise; the segmentation-in-attention-maps figure is the most shared image of 2021 self-supervision — but be precise that it's the [CLS] token's attention in the final layer; DINOv2's data curation (retrieval-based deduplication) is the underrated half.

---

# MONTH 5 — NLP: From Word Vectors to the Transformer
### Days 121–150 · Level 2–3 · The single most important month in this curriculum

Everything from here to Day 371 assumes this month. Do not rush it. If you fall behind, borrow days from Month 3, not from this one.

---

**D121 · Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space (word2vec)** — Mikolov, Chen, Corrado & Dean, 2013 (arXiv:1301.3781) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` king − man + woman = queen, and why that was shocking ⟵ D57 · `6:00` CBOW and Skip-gram architectures, drawn side by side · `16:00` Removing the hidden layer: what made it 1000× cheaper than Bengio 2003 · `25:00` The analogy task construction and results table · `33:00` What the vector arithmetic actually means, and the critiques of the analogy evaluation
▸ **Nail these:** the model is a shallow linear model, and its cheapness is the contribution — say this clearly; CBOW is faster, skip-gram better on rare words; the analogy result depends on excluding the input words from the answer set, which is a real methodological caveat (Linzen 2016); training details are in tomorrow's paper, not this one.

**D122 · Distributed Representations of Words and Phrases and their Compositionality** — Mikolov, Sutskever, Chen, Corrado & Dean, *NIPS* 2013 (arXiv:1310.4546) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` The paper with the actual algorithm · `5:00` Hierarchical softmax with a Huffman tree: O(log|V|) · `15:00` Negative sampling, derived from NCE — the objective on screen · `26:00` The 3/4-power unigram distribution, and the empirical justification · `32:00` Subsampling frequent words: the √(t/f) formula · `38:00` Phrase detection by pointwise mutual information
▸ **Nail these:** negative sampling is *not* a proper likelihood — it's NCE-inspired and simplified, and Goldberg & Levy 2014 spells this out; k=5–20 negatives; the 3/4 exponent is pure empiricism and admitting that is good teaching; Levy & Goldberg 2014 proved SGNS implicitly factorizes a shifted PMI matrix — this is the bridge to tomorrow.

**D123 · GloVe: Global Vectors for Word Representation** — Pennington, Socher & Manning, *EMNLP* 2014 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Count-based vs predict-based, reconciled · `5:00` The co-occurrence ratio insight: P(k|ice)/P(k|steam) for solid/gas/water/fashion — walk the table · `16:00` Deriving the objective from the ratio requirement, step by step · `28:00` The weighting function f(x) with x_max=100, α=3/4 · `34:00` The GloVe vs word2vec bake-off, and the later finding that hyperparameters dominated
▸ **Nail these:** the derivation from "differences of vectors should encode ratios of probabilities" to a log-bilinear objective is genuinely elegant — do it fully; the weighting function prevents rare co-occurrences from dominating and frequent ones from saturating; Levy, Goldberg & Dagan 2015 showed most of the GloVe/word2vec gap was hyperparameters, and that's an important methodological lesson.

**D124 · Enriching Word Vectors with Subword Information (fastText)** — Bojanowski, Grave, Joulin & Mikolov, *TACL* 2017 (arXiv:1607.04606) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` What do you do with a word you've never seen? · `4:00` Character n-grams (3–6) plus the whole word, summed · `12:00` The hashing trick for bucketing n-grams into a fixed table · `19:00` Morphologically rich languages: Finnish, Turkish, German results · `25:00` OOV handling as the practical win; and why BPE ⟶ D130 supersedes it
▸ **Nail these:** angle-bracket boundary markers `<where>` distinguish the word from its substrings; 2M hash buckets typical; the gain is largest for morphologically rich languages and small for English — a good honest caveat; this makes embeddings compositional, which is the conceptual link to subword tokenization.

**D125 · Sequence to Sequence Learning with Neural Networks** — Sutskever, Vinyals & Le, *NIPS* 2014 (arXiv:1409.3215) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Neural machine translation, end to end · `5:00` Encoder LSTM → fixed vector → decoder LSTM ⟵ D58 · `14:00` Reversing the source sentence: +4.7 BLEU from one line of preprocessing · `22:00` Why reversal helps — minimal time lag between corresponding words · `29:00` Beam search decoding, beam width effects · `36:00` The fixed-length bottleneck, quantified against sentence length
▸ **Nail these:** the reversal trick is the best "surprising empirical hack" story in NLP — spend time on it and explain the optimization argument; 4 layers, 1000 cells, 380M parameters, 10 days on 8 GPUs; deep LSTMs beat shallow ones significantly here; the BLEU-vs-length degradation curve is exactly the motivation for tomorrow.

**D126 · Neural Machine Translation by Jointly Learning to Align and Translate (Bahdanau attention)** — Bahdanau, Cho & Bengio, *ICLR* 2015 (arXiv:1409.0473) · 55 min · L3
`0:00` The most important idea in modern machine learning, in its first form · `6:00` The problem restated: one vector cannot hold a sentence ⟵ D125 · `14:00` Additive attention: e_ij = vᵀ tanh(W s_{i−1} + U h_j), softmax, weighted sum — derive every term · `28:00` A soft alignment matrix as a byproduct; put their French–English heatmap on screen · `38:00` Bidirectional encoder; the "annotations" framing · `46:00` Why this is differentiable soft dictionary lookup — the framing that makes Day 129 easy
▸ **Nail these:** the context vector is now *per-decoder-step*, which is the entire fix; the alignment is learned, never supervised, and it recovers linguistically correct word order swaps; performance no longer degrades with sentence length — show their plot; introduce query/key/value language *here*, retroactively, so Day 129 is a small step rather than a leap.

**D127 · Effective Approaches to Attention-based NMT (Luong attention)** — Luong, Pham & Manning, *EMNLP* 2015 (arXiv:1508.04025) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Simplifying and systematizing attention · `4:00` Global vs local attention · `12:00` Three scoring functions: dot, general (bilinear), concat — compared empirically · `20:00` Input feeding: passing the attentional vector to the next step · `26:00` Why dot-product won, and what it costs
▸ **Nail these:** dot-product scoring requires matching dimensions and is far cheaper than additive — this is precisely why the transformer uses it; local attention with a predicted alignment position and a Gaussian window is a forgotten precursor to sparse attention ⟵ D156; input feeding is a coverage mechanism in disguise.

**D128 · Convolutional Sequence to Sequence Learning (ConvS2S)** — Gehring, Auli, Grangier, Yarats & Dauphin, *ICML* 2017 (arXiv:1705.03122) · 30 min · L3
`0:00` The non-recurrent seq2seq that came three months before the transformer · `4:00` Stacked convolutions for a fixed hierarchical receptive field · `12:00` Gated linear units; residual connections; careful initialization for variance control · `20:00` Multi-step attention: attention at every decoder layer · `26:00` Full parallelism over sequence positions — the argument that the transformer then perfected
▸ **Nail these:** the motivation is identical to the transformer's (kill the sequential dependency for GPU parallelism) but the mechanism is convolutional; O(n/k) layers to connect distant positions vs O(1) for self-attention — this comparison appears in Table 1 of the transformer paper; position embeddings appear here first; a great "almost got there" story.

**D129 · Attention Is All You Need** — Vaswani et al., *NIPS* 2017 (arXiv:1706.03762) · 90 min · L3 · **THE keystone episode — make it your best**
`0:00` Cold open: this architecture is behind essentially everything in Months 6–12 · `5:00` Scaled dot-product attention: Q, K, V, and softmax(QKᵀ/√d_k)V — build it from Day 126's equations · `20:00` Why divide by √d_k — derive the variance argument, then show softmax saturation without it · `28:00` Multi-head attention: project, attend in parallel, concat, project — with shapes on screen at every step · `42:00` Position-wise FFN: two linear layers, d_model=512 → d_ff=2048, and what it's for · `50:00` Sinusoidal positional encoding, plotted; the relative-offset linearity property · `60:00` Encoder and decoder stacks; causal masking; cross-attention · `70:00` Table 1: complexity per layer, sequential ops, max path length — read all three columns aloud · `78:00` Training details: Adam with warmup, label smoothing 0.1, dropout 0.1, base vs big · `85:00` What the title claims, and what the paper actually removes
▸ **Nail these:** the √d_k derivation (dot product of two random d-dim vectors has variance d); shapes at every step — (B, h, n, d_k) — because shape confusion is where every implementation dies; masking implemented as −∞ before softmax; the FFN is ~2/3 of the parameters and is where most of the "knowledge" sits (relevant on Day 312); the O(n²·d) vs O(n·d²) tradeoff that Month 6 spends thirty days attacking. Consider making this a two-part episode if it runs long — do not compress it.

**D130 · Neural Machine Translation of Rare Words with Subword Units (BPE)** — Sennrich, Haddow & Birch, *ACL* 2016 (arXiv:1508.07909) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` The open-vocabulary problem · `4:00` Byte Pair Encoding, originally a 1994 compression algorithm · `11:00` Run the merge algorithm by hand on a tiny corpus — this is the episode · `22:00` Vocabulary size as a compression/granularity dial · `28:00` Joint BPE across languages for shared subwords · `32:00` Byte-level BPE and why GPT-2 uses it ⟵ D144
▸ **Nail these:** do the merges live on a 5-word corpus; the vocabulary is learned once and frozen, so it encodes corpus statistics permanently; BPE has no linguistic notion of morphemes and splits nonsensically sometimes — show examples; tokenization explains a surprising number of LLM failures (arithmetic, character counting, non-English cost), so foreshadow that.

**D131 · SentencePiece + WordPiece** — Kudo & Richardson *EMNLP* 2018 (arXiv:1808.06226); Kudo *ACL* 2018 (arXiv:1804.10959) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Tokenization without whitespace assumptions · `5:00` SentencePiece treats the raw stream as a sequence of characters, including spaces (▁) · `13:00` WordPiece's likelihood-based merge criterion vs BPE's frequency criterion · `21:00` The unigram language model tokenizer, and EM-based vocabulary pruning · `29:00` Subword regularization: sampling different segmentations as augmentation
▸ **Nail these:** reversibility — SentencePiece can reconstruct the exact input, BPE-with-pretokenization cannot; WordPiece picks the merge that maximizes training-data likelihood, not raw count; the unigram model works top-down (prune) rather than bottom-up (merge); subword regularization gives real BLEU gains and almost nobody uses it.

**D132 · RoFormer: Enhanced Transformer with Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE)** — Su, Lu, Pan, Wen & Liu, 2021 (arXiv:2104.09864) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` The positional encoding in nearly every LLM you use ⟵ D129 · `6:00` The design requirement: make ⟨q_m, k_n⟩ depend only on m−n · `16:00` The 2D rotation solution, then the block-diagonal generalization to d dimensions · `28:00` Implementation: pairwise rotation, applied to Q and K only, never V · `35:00` Long-range decay, and the θ base (10000) as a frequency schedule · `40:00` Context extension: position interpolation, NTK-aware scaling, YaRN
▸ **Nail these:** derive the 2D case fully with the rotation matrix and show the inner product collapses to a function of m−n — this is a satisfying five-line derivation; RoPE is relative in effect but absolute in application, which is why it's cheap; changing the θ base is how you extend context, and that's why "RoPE scaling" appears in every long-context release note.

**D133 · Train Short, Test Long: ALiBi** — Press, Smith & Lewis, *ICLR* 2022 (arXiv:2108.12409) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Extrapolation, not interpolation · `4:00` Add a static linear bias −m·|i−j| to attention scores. That's it. · `12:00` The head-specific slopes as a geometric sequence · `19:00` The extrapolation plots: train on 1024, evaluate at 3072+ · `25:00` The recency-bias critique, and where ALiBi loses to RoPE
▸ **Nail these:** no learned parameters at all, and no embeddings added to the input — the simplicity is the argument; the slope schedule 2^(−8i/n) matters and they ablate it; ALiBi hard-codes a monotonic recency prior, which is fine for language modeling and wrong for retrieval-in-context tasks; present the RoPE-vs-ALiBi choice as a real, still-live tradeoff.

**D134 · Deep Contextualized Word Representations (ELMo)** — Peters et al., *NAACL* 2018 (arXiv:1802.05365) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` The same word, different vectors · `5:00` A bidirectional LSTM language model, forward and backward trained separately · `14:00` The learned per-task layer weighting: Σ s_j h_j, scaled by γ · `23:00` What the layers learn: syntax lower, semantics higher — their probing evidence · `29:00` Why it's "feature-based" not "fine-tuned", and what that cost
▸ **Nail these:** the forward and backward LMs are *independent* and concatenated, not jointly conditioned — this is the exact weakness BERT's MLM fixes ⟵ D137; the learned scalar mixture is a nice, simple idea worth stealing; +~2–5 points across six tasks with a frozen encoder; ELMo is the "pretraining works for NLP" proof of concept.

**D135 · Universal Language Model Fine-tuning (ULMFiT)** — Howard & Ruder, *ACL* 2018 (arXiv:1801.06146) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` The fine-tuning playbook, written before it was standard · `5:00` Three stages: general LM pretraining, target-domain LM fine-tuning, classifier fine-tuning · `14:00` Discriminative fine-tuning: different learning rates per layer · `21:00` Slanted triangular learning rates ⟵ D40 · `27:00` Gradual unfreezing, last layer first · `32:00` 100 labeled examples matching 10k-from-scratch — the data-efficiency claim
▸ **Nail these:** the intermediate domain-adaptive LM step is still underused in 2026 and it's free performance; gradual unfreezing exists to prevent catastrophic forgetting, and naming that failure mode explicitly is useful; the recipe is fussy — modern practice replaced most of it with "AdamW + warmup + low LR" — so present it as historically pivotal rather than currently optimal.

**D136 · Improving Language Understanding by Generative Pre-Training (GPT-1)** — Radford, Narasimhan, Salimans & Sutskever, 2018 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` A decoder-only transformer, pretrained on books · `5:00` 12 layers, 768 dims, 12 heads, 117M parameters, BooksCorpus · `13:00` Task-specific input transformations: entailment, similarity, multiple choice — traversal-style, drawn · `24:00` The auxiliary LM objective during fine-tuning (λ=0.5) · `29:00` Zero-shot behavior as a function of pretraining steps — the figure that predicted GPT-2/3 · `35:00` Why decoder-only, and what that decision bought long-term
▸ **Nail these:** the zero-shot-transfer-grows-with-pretraining figure is the single most prophetic plot in the paper — put it on screen and say what it implied; input transformations are how you did multi-task before prompting existed, and the contrast with Day 145 is the story; the auxiliary objective improves generalization; BooksCorpus was ~7000 books, and its provenance later became contested.

**D137 · BERT: Pre-training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers** — Devlin, Chang, Lee & Toutanova, *NAACL* 2019 (arXiv:1810.04805) · 55 min · L2
`0:00` Eleven state-of-the-art results in one paper · `6:00` Why left-to-right is a handicap for understanding tasks ⟵ D134, D136 · `14:00` Masked language modeling: the 80/10/10 corruption scheme, and the reason for each part · `26:00` Next Sentence Prediction, [CLS], [SEP], segment embeddings · `35:00` BERT-base vs BERT-large; the fine-tuning protocol (2–4 epochs, LR 2e-5 to 5e-5) · `43:00` GLUE, SQuAD, and the ablations: which component actually mattered · `50:00` The pretrain-finetune paradigm as a field-wide reorganization
▸ **Nail these:** the 80/10/10 scheme exists because [MASK] never appears at fine-tuning time — explain the train/test mismatch it's fixing; MLM only predicts 15% of tokens, so it's less sample-efficient than causal LM, and that's a real cost; NSP was later shown to be near-useless ⟵ D138; BERT cannot generate text, and viewers conflate BERT and GPT constantly — kill that confusion explicitly.

**D138 · RoBERTa: A Robustly Optimized BERT Pretraining Approach** — Liu et al., 2019 (arXiv:1907.11692) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` BERT was undertrained. That's the paper. · `5:00` Dynamic masking instead of static · `12:00` Dropping NSP: full-sentences vs doc-sentences ablation · `19:00` Bigger batches (8k), more data (160GB), longer training, byte-level BPE · `26:00` The result: BERT-large architecture, better numbers than every successor to date · `31:00` The methodological lesson about baselines and ablation hygiene
▸ **Nail these:** every gain here is from training decisions, zero from architecture — this is the most important recurring lesson in the entire curriculum; large-batch training with the LR scaled accordingly; the NSP removal result invalidated a widely-copied design; RoBERTa is the strongest argument for "replicate before you innovate."

**D139 · ALBERT: A Lite BERT** — Lan, Chen, Goodman, Gimpel, Sharma & Soricut, *ICLR* 2020 (arXiv:1909.11942) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Two parameter-reduction techniques, and one better objective · `4:00` Factorized embedding parameterization: V×H → V×E + E×H, with the arithmetic · `13:00` Cross-layer parameter sharing, and its regularizing side effect · `21:00` Sentence Order Prediction: harder than NSP because it removes the topic shortcut · `27:00` The catch: fewer parameters, same or worse *latency*
▸ **Nail these:** compute the embedding-factorization savings for V=30k, H=1024, E=128 on screen; sharing all layers costs ~1 point but cuts parameters 10×; SOP works because NSP's negatives came from different documents, so topic prediction solved it — a beautiful example of a task being accidentally trivial; parameter count ≠ speed, and that confusion is worth a full minute.

**D140 · ELECTRA: Pre-training Text Encoders as Discriminators** — Clark, Luong, Le & Manning, *ICLR* 2020 (arXiv:2003.10555) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Learning from every token instead of 15% ⟵ D137 · `5:00` Generator/discriminator setup, and why it is *not* a GAN · `14:00` Replaced token detection: binary classification over all positions · `22:00` Generator size sweep — ¼ to ½ the discriminator size is optimal, and why · `28:00` Weight sharing of embeddings only · `32:00` ELECTRA-small matching much larger models at a fraction of the compute
▸ **Nail these:** the sample efficiency argument is the core claim — 100% of positions provide signal; explicitly say why it isn't adversarial (the generator is trained with MLE, not to fool the discriminator, because discrete sampling blocks gradients); too-strong a generator hurts, which is counterintuitive and worth dwelling on; compute-matched comparison curves are the right way to evaluate and this paper does them well.

**D141 · XLNet: Generalized Autoregressive Pretraining** — Yang, Dai, Yang, Carbonell, Salakhutdinov & Le, *NeurIPS* 2019 (arXiv:1906.08237) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Getting bidirectional context without masking · `6:00` Permutation language modeling — factorization order, not word order · `18:00` Two-stream self-attention: content stream and query stream, and exactly why one stream is insufficient · `30:00` Target-aware representations and partial prediction · `37:00` Transformer-XL as the backbone ⟵ D148 · `41:00` Why XLNet lost to simpler methods despite being more elegant
▸ **Nail these:** the permutation is over the *factorization order* while positions stay fixed — this is the single most misunderstood point in the paper and worth five minutes; the two-stream mechanism exists because you must know *which* position you're predicting without seeing its content; complexity of implementation vs benefit is a real theme, and RoBERTa ⟵ D138 largely closed the gap with none of it.

**D142 · Exploring the Limits of Transfer Learning with a Unified Text-to-Text Transformer (T5)** — Raffel et al., *JMLR* 2020 (arXiv:1910.10683) · 55 min · L3
`0:00` Every NLP task as text-in, text-out · `6:00` The unified format and task prefixes · `14:00` The systematic study: architecture, objective, corpus, training strategy, scaling — this is a survey disguised as a model · `28:00` Span corruption, and the ablation across objectives · `36:00` C4: the Common Crawl cleaning pipeline, in detail · `44:00` Relative position buckets — T5's positional scheme · `50:00` The final recipe and what generalizes from their ablations
▸ **Nail these:** the ablation tables are the paper — pick four and read them properly; encoder-decoder with denoising beats decoder-only *at this scale*, and noting the scale qualifier is important given Month 6; the C4 cleaning heuristics (the "bad words" filter, deduplication, the three-sentence rule) are cited constantly; span corruption with mean span 3, 15% rate.

**D143 · BART: Denoising Sequence-to-Sequence Pre-training** — Lewis et al., *ACL* 2020 (arXiv:1910.13461) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` BERT's encoder plus GPT's decoder · `4:00` Five noising functions: token masking, deletion, text infilling, sentence permutation, document rotation · `13:00` The ablation over noise types — text infilling wins · `20:00` Fine-tuning for classification, generation, and translation (with the added source encoder) · `26:00` BART vs T5: two answers to the same question
▸ **Nail these:** text infilling forces the model to predict *how many* tokens are missing, which masking doesn't — that's the finding; the noise ablation is the whole scientific content; the translation setup with a randomly-initialized source encoder is a clever piece of engineering; summarization is where BART dominated for years.

**D144 · Language Models are Unsupervised Multitask Learners (GPT-2)** — Radford, Wu, Child, Luan, Amodei & Sutskever, 2019 · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Zero-shot task performance from pure language modeling · `6:00` WebText: 8M documents, 40GB, curated by Reddit karma ≥ 3 · `14:00` 117M → 1.5B parameters; the four model sizes and their consistent scaling · `22:00` Zero-shot results on summarization ("TL;DR:"), translation, QA — and how weak they actually were · `31:00` Pre-LN, modified init scaling by 1/√N, byte-level BPE ⟵ D130 · `38:00` The staged release decision, and how that debate is remembered now
▸ **Nail these:** the log-linear scaling across four sizes with no saturation is the observation that motivates Day 146; zero-shot numbers were far below supervised SOTA and the paper says so — the significance was the *trend*, not the absolute level; "the task is a subset of language modeling" as the conceptual claim; handle the release controversy factually and without editorializing.

**D145 · Language Models are Few-Shot Learners (GPT-3)** — Brown et al., *NeurIPS* 2020 (arXiv:2005.14165) · 60 min · L3
`0:00` 175 billion parameters, and a new way to use a model · `7:00` In-context learning: zero-, one-, and few-shot defined precisely · `17:00` The architecture is GPT-2 with alternating dense and locally-banded sparse attention — that's *all* that changed · `25:00` The 8 model sizes and the smooth scaling curves across dozens of tasks · `35:00` Where it shines (translation, cloze, arithmetic to 3 digits) and where it fails (NLI, WiC) · `44:00` Data contamination analysis — the paper's own honest section · `52:00` No gradient updates at inference: what that implies about what pretraining stored
▸ **Nail these:** in-context learning requires no weight updates, which was genuinely new and is the seed of all prompting work in Month 7; few-shot gains grow with model size — the "emergence" framing starts here ⟶ D206; 3-digit addition works and 5-digit largely doesn't, a great concrete capability boundary; the training compute (~3.6k petaflop/s-days) and the fact that they couldn't afford to retrain after finding a data bug — they say so in a footnote.

**D146 · Scaling Laws for Neural Language Models** — Kaplan, McCandlish, Henighan et al., 2020 (arXiv:2001.08361) · 55 min · L3
`0:00` Loss as a predictable power law in compute, data, and parameters · `6:00` The three power laws, and the log-log plots that make them obvious · `18:00` Architecture details (depth vs width) barely matter within reason — the strongest claim in the paper · `26:00` The L(N, D) joint formula and the compute-optimal allocation they derive · `36:00` Their conclusion: at fixed compute, prefer bigger models trained on less data · `44:00` Sample efficiency of large models; critical batch size · `50:00` Why this paper changed lab strategy overnight
▸ **Nail these:** power law in *loss*, and loss is not capability — hold that distinction firmly; the seven-orders-of-magnitude fits are the evidence, so show the plots; the ~N^0.73 vs D^0.27 allocation is what Day 147 overturns; critical batch size and the gradient-noise-scale connection (McCandlish et al. 2018) is worth two minutes.

**D147 · Training Compute-Optimal Large Language Models (Chinchilla)** — Hoffmann et al., *NeurIPS* 2022 (arXiv:2203.15556) · 50 min · L3
`0:00` Everyone had been training models too big on too little data · `6:00` Three independent estimation approaches, and why agreement across all three is the argument · `18:00` The result: scale N and D roughly equally; ~20 tokens per parameter · `26:00` Chinchilla-70B beating Gopher-280B ⟵ D172 on almost everything · `35:00` Why Kaplan's analysis differed — the LR schedule confound ⟵ D146 · `42:00` What Chinchilla-optimal means for *inference* cost, and why labs now overtrain past it ⟶ D351
▸ **Nail these:** the LR-schedule explanation for the discrepancy is subtle and important — Kaplan used a fixed schedule across runs, which penalized short runs; the 20:1 ratio is *training*-compute-optimal, not deployment-optimal, and Llama ⟵ D168 deliberately violates it; the isoFLOP curves are the clearest of the three methods — teach that one; data becomes the binding constraint, which is the whole story of 2023–2026.

**D148 · Transformer-XL: Attentive Language Models Beyond a Fixed-Length Context** — Dai, Yang, Yang, Carbonell, Le & Salakhutdinov, *ACL* 2019 (arXiv:1901.02860) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Context fragmentation, illustrated · `4:00` Segment-level recurrence: cache the previous segment's hidden states, stop-gradient · `14:00` Why absolute positional encodings break under recurrence · `21:00` Relative positional encoding: the four-term decomposition of the attention score, derived · `30:00` 1800× faster evaluation; state reuse at inference
▸ **Nail these:** the four-term expansion (content-content, content-position, global content bias, global position bias) is the technical heart and it's very teachable; the memory is detached from the graph, so it costs memory but not gradient computation; this is the direct ancestor of KV caching ⟶ D338; fragmentation is why fixed-window LMs lose context at every boundary.

**D149 · Longformer and Big Bird** — Beltagy, Peters & Cohan 2020 (arXiv:2004.05150); Zaheer et al. *NeurIPS* 2020 (arXiv:2007.14062) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` The O(n²) wall, and two structured escapes · `5:00` Longformer: sliding window + dilated window + global tokens, drawn as attention masks · `16:00` Big Bird: window + global + *random* attention · `24:00` The theory: Big Bird is a universal approximator and Turing complete — and the catch (it may need many more layers) · `32:00` Custom CUDA kernels; why sparse attention is hard to make actually fast · `37:00` Which tasks genuinely need 4096+ tokens
▸ **Nail these:** draw the attention masks — this month is full of paper-as-picture episodes and this is one; global tokens are what preserve BERT-style [CLS] behavior; the theoretical results are real but the constants are bad, and saying that is more useful than repeating the headline; hardware efficiency of sparse patterns is the reason FlashAttention ⟶ D153 beat all of these with *dense* attention.

**D150 · Reformer: The Efficient Transformer** — Kitaev, Kaiser & Levskaya, *ICLR* 2020 (arXiv:2001.04451) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Three independent efficiency ideas in one paper · `5:00` LSH attention: bucket by angular locality-sensitive hashing, attend within buckets · `18:00` The shared-QK constraint that LSH requires, and its cost · `25:00` Reversible residual layers: recompute activations instead of storing them ⟵ D341 · `34:00` Chunked feed-forward layers · `38:00` Why Reformer is admired more than used
▸ **Nail these:** LSH gives O(n log n) but with large constants and it only helps at very long sequences — be precise about the crossover; multi-round hashing to reduce the probability of missing a match; reversible layers are a genuinely reusable idea independent of the rest; this is a good episode for the theme "asymptotics are not speed."

---

# MONTH 6 — LLM Architecture, Efficiency, and Training at Scale
### Days 151–180 · Level 3 · How a frontier model is actually built

Month 5 gave you the transformer. This month is everything that had to be invented to train one with a hundred billion parameters across ten thousand GPUs without it diverging, and to serve it without bankrupting you.

---

**D151 · Linformer: Self-Attention with Linear Complexity** — Wang, Li, Khabsa, Fang & Ma, 2020 (arXiv:2006.04768) · 30 min · L3
`0:00` The low-rank observation · `5:00` Spectral analysis of attention matrices: the top eigenvalues carry most of the mass — their evidence · `13:00` Projecting K and V to a fixed length k with learned linear maps · `21:00` The Johnson–Lindenstrauss argument for why k can be O(d/ε²), independent of n · `27:00` The fatal restriction: sequence length must be fixed at training time
▸ **Nail these:** the low-rank claim is empirical and architecture-dependent — don't overstate it; parameter sharing across heads and layers as the practical variant; the fixed-n limitation rules out autoregressive decoding entirely, which is why this is an encoder-only idea; a good example of a clean method with a disqualifying constraint.

**D152 · Rethinking Attention with Performers** — Choromanski et al., *ICLR* 2021 (arXiv:2009.14794) · 40 min · L4
`0:00` Approximating softmax attention with random features · `6:00` The kernel view: softmax(qᵀk) as a kernel, then φ(q)ᵀφ(k) · `16:00` FAVOR+: positive orthogonal random features, and why positivity is required for stability · `27:00` Reassociating the matrix product to get O(n) — the algebra on screen · `34:00` Unbiasedness and variance bounds; where the approximation visibly degrades
▸ **Nail these:** the reassociation ((φQ)(φKᵀ V)) vs ((φQ φKᵀ) V) is the entire computational trick, and it's two lines of algebra that people find genuinely illuminating; trigonometric random features have negative values and blow up the variance — the positivity fix is the paper's real contribution; approximation error compounds across layers.

**D153 · FlashAttention: Fast and Memory-Efficient Exact Attention with IO-Awareness** — Dao, Fu, Ermon, Rudra & Ré, *NeurIPS* 2022 (arXiv:2205.14135) · 50 min · L3
`0:00` Attention was never compute-bound. It was memory-bound. · `6:00` GPU memory hierarchy: SRAM ~19 TB/s vs HBM ~1.5 TB/s, and the size difference · `15:00` Standard attention's HBM traffic: write the n×n matrix out, read it back, twice · `24:00` Tiling and the online softmax trick — derive the running max/sum rescaling · `36:00` Recomputation in the backward pass instead of storing the attention matrix · `43:00` The IO complexity result: O(n²d²/M) vs O(nd + n²) · `47:00` Exact, not approximate — why that killed most of the last two episodes
▸ **Nail these:** the online softmax rescaling derivation is the single highest-value five minutes of Month 6 — do it slowly with numbers; the paper's thesis is that the entire efficient-attention literature optimized the wrong resource; 2–4× wall-clock speedup and 10–20× memory reduction; being *exact* is the strategic advantage over Days 149–152.

**D154 · FlashAttention-2 and FlashAttention-3** — Dao 2023 (arXiv:2307.08691); Shah et al. 2024 · 30 min · L3
`0:00` From 25% to 70%+ of theoretical peak FLOPs · `4:00` Fewer non-matmul FLOPs, and why they cost 16× more than matmul on tensor cores · `12:00` Parallelizing over sequence length, not just batch and heads · `19:00` Better warp-level work partitioning to reduce shared-memory communication · `25:00` FA-3: asynchrony, warp specialization, and FP8 on Hopper
▸ **Nail these:** the non-matmul FLOP argument is the most instructive part — modern GPUs have wildly asymmetric throughput and algorithms must be written to that; parallelizing over sequence length matters for long-context, low-batch inference; this pair of episodes is where viewers learn that "the kernel is the model."

**D155 · Multi-Query and Grouped-Query Attention** — Shazeer 2019 (arXiv:1911.02150); Ainslie et al. *EMNLP* 2023 (arXiv:2305.13245) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` The real bottleneck at inference: the KV cache · `5:00` Compute the KV cache size for a 70B model at 32k context — put the gigabytes on screen · `13:00` MQA: one shared K and V head across all query heads · `20:00` The quality loss, and MQA's training instability · `26:00` GQA: G groups as the interpolation; uptraining an existing MHA checkpoint with 5% of original compute · `32:00` Why every major open model since 2023 ships GQA
▸ **Nail these:** the arithmetic-intensity argument — decoding is memory-bandwidth-bound, so shrinking the KV cache directly buys throughput; the uptraining procedure (mean-pool the K/V heads within a group, then continue training briefly) is the practical contribution; MLA ⟶ D175 as the next step; this connects to Day 338's serving economics.

**D156 · Generating Long Sequences with Sparse Transformers** — Child, Gray, Radford & Sutskever, 2019 (arXiv:1904.10509) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Attention patterns learned by dense transformers are already sparse — start with their visualizations · `6:00` Strided and fixed sparse patterns, drawn as masks · `15:00` Factorized attention: O(n√n) via two-step connectivity · `23:00` The engineering: block-sparse kernels, gradient checkpointing, mixed precision · `30:00` Images, audio, and text at length 12,288 from one architecture
▸ **Nail these:** the empirical attention-pattern figures motivate the design rather than the other way round, which is good scientific hygiene; every position must reach every other in at most two steps — that's the design constraint; this is the alternating dense/sparse attention GPT-3 ⟵ D145 used; the kernels mattered as much as the math.

**D157 · Outrageously Large Neural Networks: The Sparsely-Gated Mixture-of-Experts Layer** — Shazeer et al., *ICLR* 2017 (arXiv:1701.06538) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` 137 billion parameters in 2017 · `6:00` Conditional computation: decouple parameter count from FLOPs per token · `14:00` The noisy top-k gating network, written out · `24:00` Load balancing: the importance loss and the load loss, and why you need both · `33:00` The shrinking-batch problem and the mixing of data and model parallelism · `40:00` Why this idea slept for four years
▸ **Nail these:** the "rich get richer" self-reinforcing gating collapse is the central failure mode of every MoE paper since — introduce it properly here; importance (soft) vs load (hard) balancing are different quantities and both are needed; top-k with k=2 or 4; the batch-shrinking analysis is a systems insight that predicts all the later expert-parallel work.

**D158 · GShard: Scaling Giant Models with Conditional Computation and Automatic Sharding** — Lepikhin et al., *ICLR* 2021 (arXiv:2006.16668) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` 600B parameters, 100 languages, 4 days · `5:00` MoE placed in every other transformer layer · `13:00` Expert capacity, capacity factor, and token dropping — the mechanism nobody mentions · `21:00` Local group dispatching and the auxiliary balancing loss · `27:00` The annotation API: `shard`, `replicate`, and compiler-driven SPMD partitioning · `32:00` Why "the compiler does the parallelism" was the durable idea
▸ **Nail these:** tokens *are dropped* when an expert overflows its capacity, and this is a real quality cost that summaries omit; capacity factor 1.25–2.0 as the tuning dial; random routing for the second expert; the separation of model code from sharding annotations is what later became GSPMD and JAX's `shard_map` ⟶ D348.

**D159 · Switch Transformers: Scaling to Trillion Parameter Models** — Fedus, Zoph & Shazeer, *JMLR* 2022 (arXiv:2101.03961) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Top-1 routing: the simplification that made MoE practical · `6:00` Why k=1 was believed necessary to be ≥2, and why that was wrong · `14:00` The simplified load-balancing loss: α·N·Σ f_i·P_i — derive why this is differentiable when f is not · `24:00` Selective precision: route in float32, everything else in bfloat16, and the instability it fixes · `31:00` Expert dropout; smaller initialization scale · `36:00` 7× pretraining speedup at equal FLOPs; distillation back to dense
▸ **Nail these:** the balancing loss multiplies a non-differentiable fraction by a differentiable probability, which is a neat trick worth unpacking; routing instability in bfloat16 is a genuinely important practical finding; "same FLOPs, more parameters, faster convergence" is the MoE value proposition in one line; expert count vs quality curves.

**D160 · Mixtral of Experts** — Jiang et al., 2024 (arXiv:2401.04088) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` An open MoE that matched much larger dense models · `4:00` 8 experts, top-2 routing, 47B total / 13B active — get these numbers exactly right · `12:00` The router as a simple linear layer per layer · `19:00` Router analysis: experts specialize by *syntax and position*, not by topic — the negative result that surprised people · `27:00` Memory vs compute: you must hold all 47B in VRAM even though you use 13B · `32:00` DeepSeekMoE's fine-grained + shared-expert refinement ⟶ D176
▸ **Nail these:** the "experts are not topic specialists" finding is the most interesting thing in the paper and it's counterintuitive — dwell on it; consecutive-token routing repetition; the deployment reality (memory-bound, not compute-bound) explains why MoE is a datacenter technique and not a laptop one; sliding-window attention in Mistral's base architecture ⟵ carries over from D171.

**D161 · GLU Variants Improve Transformer** — Shazeer, 2020 (arXiv:2002.05202) · 25 min · L2
`0:00` A two-page paper in every modern LLM · `3:00` GLU: (xW) ⊗ σ(xV) — gating in the feed-forward block · `9:00` The variants: ReGLU, GEGLU, SwiGLU, and the ablation table · `16:00` Keeping parameters constant by scaling d_ff to ⅔ — the detail everyone gets wrong · `21:00` The paper's own closing line about divine benevolence, and what it says about ML empiricism
▸ **Nail these:** SwiGLU uses three weight matrices instead of two, so d_ff must shrink to 8/3·d_model to stay parameter-matched — this exact number appears in Llama's config and viewers should know why; the gains are small (~0.5% perplexity) but free; the paper offers no explanation and says so, which is a great teaching moment about the field's honesty and its limits.

**D162 · Root Mean Square Layer Normalization (RMSNorm)** — Zhang & Sennrich, *NeurIPS* 2019 (arXiv:1910.07467) · 25 min · L2
`0:00` Delete the mean subtraction. Nothing breaks. · `4:00` LayerNorm decomposed into re-centering and re-scaling ⟵ D48 · `11:00` The hypothesis: only re-scaling invariance matters · `17:00` RMSNorm's formula; 7–64% speedup on RNN benchmarks · `21:00` Partial RMSNorm; why every Llama-family model uses this
▸ **Nail these:** the ablation isolating re-centering from re-scaling is the scientific content — it's a clean hypothesis-and-test paper, rare in this space; no bias parameter either; the speedup is from fewer reduction operations, which matters at scale ⟵ D154's non-matmul-FLOP argument; pairs naturally with Day 163.

**D163 · On Layer Normalization in the Transformer Architecture (Pre-LN)** — Xiong et al., *ICML* 2020 (arXiv:2002.04745) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Why the original transformer needed a learning-rate warmup, and why yours doesn't · `5:00` Post-LN vs Pre-LN, drawn as residual-stream diagrams · `13:00` Mean-field analysis: Post-LN gradients at the output layer scale as O(√d·ln d) at init; Pre-LN as O(√(d ln d / L)) · `24:00` The consequence: Pre-LN trains without warmup and tolerates larger learning rates · `31:00` The catch — Pre-LN's residual stream grows with depth, and the final-LN fix
▸ **Nail these:** the gradient-scale-at-init argument is the whole paper and it's the best available explanation for a hyperparameter (warmup) that most people treat as magic; Post-LN often reaches slightly better final quality if you can train it — the tradeoff is stability, not quality; DeepNet and Sandwich-LN as later attempts to get both.

**D164 · Megatron-LM: Training Multi-Billion Parameter Models Using Model Parallelism** — Shoeybi, Patwary, Puri, LeGresley, Casper & Catanzaro, 2019 (arXiv:1909.08053) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` When the model doesn't fit on one GPU · `6:00` Tensor parallelism for the MLP: split the first matrix column-wise, the second row-wise — draw the shapes and show why only *one* all-reduce is needed · `20:00` Tensor parallelism for attention: split by head, which is embarrassingly natural · `28:00` The f and g conjugate operators; forward all-reduce, backward identity, and vice versa · `35:00` Vocabulary-parallel embeddings and the fused cross-entropy · `40:00` Why TP must stay inside a node: NVLink vs InfiniBand bandwidth
▸ **Nail these:** the column-then-row splitting is the cleverest single idea in distributed training and it's fully explainable with two matrix diagrams — this is your visual set piece for the episode; communication volume analysis; TP degree is limited by intra-node interconnect, which is why 8 is the usual number; this composes with Days 165 and 166 into 3D parallelism.

**D165 · ZeRO: Memory Optimizations Toward Training Trillion Parameter Models** — Rajbhandari, Rasley, Ruwase & He, *SC* 2020 (arXiv:1910.02054) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` The memory audit: what actually occupies your GPU · `6:00` Mixed-precision Adam states: 16 bytes per parameter — derive the number term by term ⟵ D37, D340 · `16:00` ZeRO-1 (optimizer states), ZeRO-2 (+gradients), ZeRO-3 (+parameters), with the memory formula for each stage · `28:00` Communication analysis: stages 1 and 2 are *free* in volume terms, stage 3 costs 1.5× · `36:00` ZeRO-Offload and ZeRO-Infinity to CPU and NVMe · `41:00` ZeRO vs tensor parallelism: partitioning state vs partitioning compute
▸ **Nail these:** the 16-bytes-per-parameter breakdown (fp32 params, fp32 momentum, fp32 variance, plus fp16 copies) is the number every practitioner should have memorized; ZeRO-3 turns data parallelism into something with model-parallel memory characteristics; the reduce-scatter + all-gather equivalence to all-reduce is why stages 1–2 are volume-neutral; FSDP is this idea in PyTorch.

**D166 · GPipe: Efficient Training of Giant Neural Networks using Pipeline Parallelism** — Huang et al., *NeurIPS* 2019 (arXiv:1811.06965) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` The third axis of parallelism · `5:00` Partition layers across devices; the naive version leaves everyone idle · `12:00` Micro-batching to fill the pipeline; the bubble fraction (K−1)/(M+K−1) — derive it · `22:00` Re-materialization to cut activation memory ⟵ D341 · `28:00` PipeDream's 1F1B schedule and the weight-staleness problem · `33:00` 3D parallelism: TP × PP × DP, and how a real cluster is laid out
▸ **Nail these:** derive the bubble formula and then plug in real numbers (M=4·K is the usual rule of thumb); pipeline parallelism has the lowest communication volume of the three, which is why it crosses node boundaries; the layer-partitioning must balance *time*, not parameter count; end with a concrete 3D layout for a 70B model — it's the payoff for Days 164–166.

**D167 · PaLM: Scaling Language Modeling with Pathways** — Chowdhery et al., *JMLR* 2023 (arXiv:2204.02311) · 50 min · L3
`0:00` 540B parameters across 6144 TPU v4 chips at 46.2% model FLOPs utilization · `7:00` The architecture deltas: SwiGLU ⟵ D161, parallel attention+MLP layers, multi-query attention ⟵ D155, RoPE ⟵ D132, no biases · `20:00` The parallel-layer formulation and its 15% speedup · `26:00` Pathways: two-way pod-level data parallelism over 6144 chips · `34:00` Discontinuous jumps on specific tasks at 540B — the emergence claim ⟶ D206 · `41:00` Chain-of-thought results on GSM8K ⟵ D190 · `46:00` Training instabilities: loss spikes, and the "rewind and skip data" mitigation
▸ **Nail these:** MFU as the right efficiency metric and how it's computed; the loss-spike mitigation is a fascinating piece of real-world practice and it's in the paper — they restart from an earlier checkpoint and skip ~200–500 batches; removing biases improves stability at scale; the architecture is a checklist of Month 6 techniques, so use this episode as the integration point.

**D168 · LLaMA: Open and Efficient Foundation Language Models** — Touvron et al., 2023 (arXiv:2302.13971) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` The release that created the open-weights ecosystem · `6:00` Deliberately overtrained past Chinchilla-optimal, and the inference-cost argument for doing so ⟵ D147 · `15:00` The architecture: RMSNorm pre-norm, SwiGLU, RoPE — the now-standard stack · `24:00` 1.4T tokens; the fully public data mix (CommonCrawl, C4, GitHub, Wikipedia, Books, arXiv, StackExchange) with percentages · `33:00` LLaMA-13B outperforming GPT-3-175B on most benchmarks · `39:00` 2048 A100s, 21 days, and the carbon accounting they published
▸ **Nail these:** the training-vs-inference-compute argument is the most important idea in this paper and it's an economics argument, not an ML one; the data table is worth showing in full because it's rare; every number here is checkable, which is precisely why this paper mattered; note the license was research-only, which is what Llama 2 changes.

**D169 · Llama 2: Open Foundation and Fine-Tuned Chat Models** — Touvron et al., 2023 (arXiv:2307.09288) · 50 min · L3
`0:00` A 77-page engineering report, and the best RLHF documentation published at the time · `7:00` Pretraining changes: 2T tokens, 4k context, GQA on the 34B/70B ⟵ D155 · `15:00` SFT: 27,540 high-quality annotations, and their finding that quality beat quantity by a wide margin · `24:00` Two separate reward models — helpfulness and safety — and why splitting them was necessary · `33:00` Iterative RLHF over five rounds; rejection sampling then PPO ⟵ D183 · `41:00` Ghost Attention (GAtt) for multi-turn instruction persistence · `46:00` The safety evaluations and the over-refusal cost they document
▸ **Nail these:** the helpfulness/safety reward-model tension is the central alignment lesson and they show the tradeoff curve; rejection sampling (best-of-N against the reward model) before PPO is a practical recipe most people miss; GAtt is a clever synthetic-data trick worth explaining; be factual about the license — it is not OSI open source.

**D170 · The Llama 3 Herd of Models** — Grattafiori et al., 2024 (arXiv:2407.21783) · 55 min · L3
`0:00` A 90-page paper that reads like an operations manual · `8:00` 15T tokens, 405B parameters, 128k vocabulary, 128k context · `18:00` Data curation: dedup at three levels, model-based quality filtering, and the annealing-on-high-quality-data finding · `28:00` 16k H100s; 4D parallelism (TP+PP+CP+DP); the failure statistics — 466 job interruptions in 54 days · `38:00` Post-training: SFT → rejection sampling → DPO ⟵ D185, and why they chose DPO over PPO · `46:00` Long-context extension in six stages; the multimodal adapters · `51:00` Scaling laws applied to *downstream benchmark* prediction, not just loss
▸ **Nail these:** the hardware-failure table is the most honest infrastructure disclosure in any ML paper — GPU failures, HBM failures, and one section on how power fluctuation from synchronized training stressed the datacenter; predicting benchmark accuracy from compute via a two-stage fit is a real methodological advance over Day 146; the "quality annealing" trick; dense rather than MoE, and their stated reason.

**D171 · Mistral 7B** — Jiang et al., 2023 (arXiv:2310.06825) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Punching several weight classes up · `4:00` Sliding-window attention: window 4096, and the receptive field growing as W×L across layers — compute it · `13:00` The rolling buffer KV cache: fixed size regardless of sequence length · `20:00` Pre-fill and chunking for long prompts · `25:00` GQA + SWA together; Apache 2.0 and what that unlocked
▸ **Nail these:** the layered receptive field argument (32 layers × 4096 window = 131k theoretical span) is elegant but is an *attenuated* path, not full attention — be precise; the rolling buffer is a concrete, implementable idea that viewers can code in 20 lines; this is the clearest single episode on inference-time memory management before Day 338.

**D172 · Scaling Language Models: Methods, Analysis & Insights from Training Gopher** — Rae et al., 2021 (arXiv:2112.11446) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` 280B parameters, and 152 tasks analyzed by category · `6:00` The six model sizes from 44M to 280B, and the consistent-scaling methodology · `14:00` Where scale helps a lot (knowledge-heavy, reading comprehension) and where it barely helps at all (logical and mathematical reasoning) — the paper's central finding · `26:00` MassiveText and the data-quality pipeline · `33:00` Toxicity and bias analysis scaling *with* model size · `37:00` Why this paper is the necessary setup for Chinchilla ⟵ D147
▸ **Nail these:** the category-by-category scaling breakdown is more informative than any aggregate benchmark score and it directly predicts the reasoning-focused work of Month 7; Gopher was later shown to be substantially undertrained, which is a great humility story to tell alongside its own thoroughness; bfloat16 with fp32 parameter copies as a stability measure.

**D173 · OPT: Open Pre-trained Transformer Language Models (+ the logbook)** — Zhang et al., 2022 (arXiv:2205.01068) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` The paper is fine. The logbook is the reason to watch this. · `6:00` 175B parameters at 1/7th the carbon of GPT-3, weights released to researchers · `14:00` Loss spikes, hardware failures, and 35+ manual restarts · `24:00` The logbook's day-by-day record: lowering LR, switching optimizers mid-run, dropping to a prior checkpoint · `33:00` Publishing failure as a scientific contribution · `37:00` What later work (Llama 3's failure table ⟵ D170) inherited from this norm
▸ **Nail these:** read three or four logbook entries verbatim-adjacent (paraphrase, don't quote at length) — the texture of real large-scale training is something no other paper conveys; gradient clipping changes and the norm-spike correlation; this is the best available antidote to the belief that frontier training is smooth; pair it with a discussion of reproducibility.

**D174 · BLOOM: A 176B-Parameter Open-Access Multilingual Language Model** — BigScience Workshop, 2022 (arXiv:2211.05100) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` A thousand researchers, one model, entirely in public · `6:00` ROOTS: 46 natural languages and 13 programming languages, with community-led data governance · `16:00` ALiBi ⟵ D133 and embedding LayerNorm as the architectural choices, with their ablation reasoning · `24:00` 384 A100s on Jean Zay; the full carbon accounting · `31:00` The RAIL license and behavioral-use restrictions · `36:00` Multilingual performance, and the tokenizer-fairness issue
▸ **Nail these:** tokenizer fairness — under-represented languages consume 2–4× more tokens for the same content, so they cost more and get less context, and this is a concrete equity issue rooted in Day 130; the open governance process is as much the contribution as the model; ALiBi was chosen after ablation, not by fashion, and they show the comparison.

**D175 · DeepSeek-V2: Multi-head Latent Attention** — DeepSeek-AI, 2024 (arXiv:2405.04434) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` A KV cache 93% smaller than MHA ⟵ D155 · `6:00` MLA: compress K and V into a shared low-rank latent, cache only that · `16:00` The up-projection absorbed into the query and output weight matrices — the trick that makes it free at inference · `26:00` Decoupled RoPE: why RoPE breaks the absorption, and the separate rotary dimensions that fix it · `33:00` DeepSeekMoE: fine-grained experts plus always-on shared experts · `37:00` 236B total / 21B active
▸ **Nail these:** the weight-absorption argument is subtle and worth doing on the whiteboard with explicit matrix shapes — it's why MLA beats GQA on both memory *and* quality; the RoPE incompatibility is a genuine engineering wrinkle that shows how positional encoding constrains architecture; shared experts capture common knowledge so specialized experts don't duplicate it.

**D176 · DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report** — DeepSeek-AI, 2024 (arXiv:2412.19437) · 50 min · L4
`0:00` 671B parameters trained for roughly 2.8M H800 GPU-hours · `8:00` Auxiliary-loss-free load balancing: per-expert bias terms adjusted by a control rule instead of a gradient penalty ⟵ D157, D159 · `20:00` Multi-token prediction as a training objective, and its use for speculative decoding ⟶ D336 · `29:00` FP8 mixed-precision training: fine-grained tile/block quantization and high-precision accumulation · `39:00` DualPipe: overlapping computation and communication to nearly erase the pipeline bubble ⟵ D166 · `46:00` Why the reported cost figure is widely misquoted
▸ **Nail these:** the auxiliary-loss-free balancing directly attacks the interference between the balancing loss and the language-modeling loss, which is a real problem Day 157 introduced; FP8 training required fixing accumulation precision inside the tensor cores, a hardware-level detail; be precise and neutral that the headline cost covers the final pretraining run only, not research, data, or ablations.

**D177 · Gemini 1.5: Unlocking Multimodal Understanding Across Millions of Tokens** — Gemini Team, 2024 (arXiv:2403.05530) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Ten million tokens of context · `6:00` The needle-in-a-haystack evaluation protocol, and its limitations as a benchmark · `16:00` Near-perfect retrieval at extreme length; multimodal needles in video and audio · `24:00` The Kalamang case study: learning a low-resource language from a grammar book in context · `32:00` MoE architecture, and how little the report actually reveals · `36:00` Long context vs RAG ⟵ D287 as competing designs
▸ **Nail these:** needle-in-a-haystack measures retrieval, not reasoning over long context — say this clearly and cite the harder benchmarks that followed; the Kalamang result is the strongest in-context-learning evidence published ⟵ D145; technical reports with withheld details are now the norm, and it's worth one honest minute on what that costs the field.

**D178 · Mamba: Linear-Time Sequence Modeling with Selective State Spaces** — Gu & Dao, 2023 (arXiv:2312.00752) · 55 min · L4
`0:00` A serious challenger to attention · `7:00` Recap: SSMs as continuous systems, discretized ⟵ D179 · `16:00` The selection mechanism: make Δ, B, C input-dependent — and why that breaks the convolutional form · `28:00` The hardware-aware parallel scan: kernel fusion, recomputation, keeping state in SRAM ⟵ D153 · `38:00` The Mamba block; 5× inference throughput, linear scaling in sequence length · `45:00` Induction-heads and copying tasks: where selectivity is provably necessary · `50:00` The honest scorecard vs transformers in 2026
▸ **Nail these:** selectivity is what lets the model *forget* — content-based filtering is exactly what LTI systems cannot do, and the toy copying/induction tasks make this concrete; the parallel scan is the same "recompute rather than store" philosophy as FlashAttention; constant memory per step at inference is the deployment argument; Mamba-2's SSD framework connecting SSMs and attention is worth naming.

**D179 · Efficiently Modeling Long Sequences with Structured State Spaces (S4)** — Gu, Goel & Ré, *ICLR* 2022 (arXiv:2111.00396) · 50 min · L4
`0:00` Solving Long Range Arena's Path-X, which every transformer had failed · `7:00` The state space model x' = Ax + Bu, y = Cx, and bilinear discretization · `18:00` HiPPO: the matrix A that provably compresses history via orthogonal polynomial projection · `29:00` Three views of the same model — recurrent, convolutional, continuous — and when to use each · `38:00` The normal-plus-low-rank parameterization and the Cauchy-kernel computation that makes it O(n log n) · `45:00` Why the math is heavy and why it paid off
▸ **Nail these:** the three-views framing is the pedagogical key — convolutional for parallel training, recurrent for O(1) inference, continuous for principled initialization; HiPPO initialization is not optional, and random A fails badly (they show it); Path-X (16k-length) at 88% after every transformer scored chance level is the result that made people pay attention; expect this to be a hard episode and say so up front.

**D180 · RWKV: Reinventing RNNs for the Transformer Era** — Peng et al., *EMNLP Findings* 2023 (arXiv:2305.13048) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Train like a transformer, infer like an RNN · `6:00` Time-mixing and channel-mixing blocks replacing attention and FFN · `16:00` The WKV operator: exponential-decay weighted sums, and the numerically-stable formulation · `26:00` Token shift as a cheap one-step lookback · `32:00` Linear-time training via the parallel formulation; constant memory inference · `36:00` The tradeoffs: weaker in-context retrieval, and prompt-order sensitivity
▸ **Nail these:** the dual formulation (parallelizable during training, recurrent during inference) is exactly Mamba's and S4's promise reached by a different route — group these three papers in the viewer's mind; the numerical stabilization with a running maximum is essential and mirrors online softmax ⟵ D153; the retrieval weakness is a real, measurable limitation and honest coverage of it makes the episode credible.

---

# MONTH 7 — Alignment, Instruction Following, and Reasoning
### Days 181–210 · Level 3 · How a pretrained model becomes an assistant

A base model predicts text. An assistant follows instructions, refuses some requests, and reasons through problems. Everything in that gap was invented in the papers below, almost all of them since 2020.

---

**D181 · Trust Region Policy Optimization** — Schulman, Levine, Moritz, Jordan & Abbeel, *ICML* 2015 (arXiv:1502.05477) · 50 min · L4
`0:00` Why policy gradients collapse, and what a trust region buys you · `7:00` Policy gradient recap and the step-size problem ⟵ D213 · `16:00` The surrogate objective and the monotonic improvement theorem · `28:00` The KL constraint; the practical shift from penalty to hard constraint · `36:00` Conjugate gradient + Fisher-vector products, so you never form the Hessian · `45:00` Line search with backtracking to enforce the true constraint
▸ **Nail these:** the improvement bound and where the approximation enters; natural gradient as the underlying idea (Kakade 2002); the second-order machinery is exactly what PPO deletes tomorrow — set that up explicitly; importance-sampling ratio ρ = π_new/π_old appears here first and is the object PPO clips.

**D182 · Proximal Policy Optimization Algorithms** — Schulman, Wolski, Dhariwal, Radford & Klimov, 2017 (arXiv:1707.06347) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` The RL algorithm inside every RLHF pipeline · `5:00` The clipped surrogate objective — plot it for A>0 and A<0 and read off what clipping actually prevents · `18:00` Multiple epochs on the same batch: the sample-efficiency win TRPO couldn't take · `26:00` The full loss: policy + value + entropy bonus, with coefficients · `33:00` Implementation details that dominate results (Engstrom et al. 2020) — advantage normalization, value clipping, orthogonal init, LR annealing · `40:00` PPO in RLHF: the KL-to-reference penalty added on top
▸ **Nail these:** the two-sided clip is asymmetric in effect and the plot makes that obvious — draw it; "the code-level optimizations account for most of PPO's advantage over TRPO" is a real and well-supported finding, so present it; ε=0.2 typical; in RLHF the reward is a learned model and the KL term keeps the policy near the SFT model, which is a different purpose from the clip.

**D183 · Deep Reinforcement Learning from Human Preferences** — Christiano, Leike, Brown, Martic, Legg & Amodei, *NeurIPS* 2017 (arXiv:1706.03741) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Teaching an agent a goal you can't write down · `6:00` The three-part loop: policy training, preference collection, reward-model fitting · `16:00` The Bradley–Terry model over trajectory segments — derive the loss · `26:00` The backflip result: ~900 human comparisons for a behavior with no reward function · `33:00` Asynchronous training, reward normalization, ensembling, and query selection by uncertainty · `40:00` Reward hacking: what happens when the policy outruns the reward model
▸ **Nail these:** Bradley–Terry is the mathematical foundation of every RLHF paper for the next decade, so derive P(σ₁ ≻ σ₂) = exp(r₁)/(exp(r₁)+exp(r₂)) carefully; ~0.1% of the agent's experience needs human labels; the reward model is trained *concurrently*, not once; preference labels are cheaper and more reliable than demonstrations or scalar ratings, and saying why is the practical takeaway.

**D184 · Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback (InstructGPT)** — Ouyang et al., *NeurIPS* 2022 (arXiv:2203.02155) · 55 min · L3
`0:00` A 1.3B model preferred over a 175B one · `7:00` The three stages: SFT on demonstrations, reward model on comparisons, PPO against the reward model ⟵ D182, D183 · `20:00` The reward model: 6B, trained on K-way rankings with all (K choose 2) pairs in one batch — and why batching them that way prevents overfitting · `30:00` PPO-ptx: mixing in pretraining gradients to fight the alignment tax · `38:00` The alignment tax measured on public NLP benchmarks · `44:00` Labeler agreement rates, the screening process, and the values question they raise themselves · `50:00` What "alignment" means in this paper, precisely
▸ **Nail these:** the 1.3B-beats-175B result is the headline and it's about *format and intent*, not capability; the K-way ranking batching detail is a real engineering insight; the KL penalty term β and the reward it's subtracted from; the paper is unusually candid that "aligned to whom" is unresolved — quote the concern in your own words and don't editorialize past it.

**D185 · Training a Helpful and Harmless Assistant with RLHF** — Bai et al., 2022 (arXiv:2204.05862) · 45 min · L3
