`0:00` The most detailed public RLHF study of its era · `6:00` The helpfulness/harmlessness tension, measured — the Pareto frontier plot · `16:00` Online iterated RLHF: deploy, collect fresh preferences, retrain, repeat · `26:00` Reward model scaling laws: log-linear improvement in accuracy · `33:00` Robustness: reward-model accuracy degrades as the policy drifts, as a function of √KL · `40:00` The alignment tax being small or negative for large models
▸ **Nail these:** the √KL relationship for reward-model reliability is a genuinely useful practical rule and it's underquoted; separate helpfulness and harmlessness datasets and models; iterated online collection is why deployed models improve faster than static ones; open-sourced preference data (hh-rlhf) is why this paper is reproducible.

**D186 · Direct Preference Optimization: Your Language Model is Secretly a Reward Model** — Rafailov, Sharma, Mitchell, Ermon, Manning & Finn, *NeurIPS* 2023 (arXiv:2305.18290) · 55 min · L4
`0:00` Deleting the reward model and the RL loop · `7:00` The KL-constrained reward maximization objective and its *closed-form* optimal policy · `18:00` Inverting it: r(x,y) = β log(π(y|x)/π_ref(y|x)) + β log Z(x) — and watching Z cancel in the Bradley–Terry difference · `30:00` The final DPO loss, and its gradient interpreted as a weighted push-apart · `38:00` Empirical parity with PPO on summarization and dialogue · `44:00` Where DPO underperforms: distribution shift, and why on-policy data matters ⟶ IPO/KTO/ORPO in brief
▸ **Nail these:** the derivation is the episode — do all of it, slowly, because it is genuinely one of the most elegant results in modern ML and it fits on one board; the implicit reward's gradient weight is higher when the current model ranks the pair wrongly; DPO is off-policy on a fixed dataset, which is the root of most of its failure modes; β plays the KL role.

**D187 · Constitutional AI: Harmlessness from AI Feedback** — Bai et al., 2022 (arXiv:2212.08073) · 50 min · L3
`0:00` Replacing human harm labels with a written set of principles · `7:00` Stage 1 — supervised: sample a harmful response, critique it against a principle, revise, fine-tune on revisions · `20:00` Stage 2 — RLAIF: the model itself labels preferences using constitutional principles, then standard RLHF ⟵ D184 · `31:00` The evasiveness problem this was designed to solve, and the helpful-and-harmless frontier it improves · `39:00` Chain-of-thought in the preference labeling step ⟵ D190 · `45:00` Scalable oversight framing: transparency of the principles as the governance argument
▸ **Nail these:** the constitution is a short list of natural-language principles sampled at random per critique — the mechanism is simpler than people assume; non-evasive harmlessness (explain the refusal rather than deflect) is the actual behavioral target; this reduces but does not remove human input, since the principles and the helpfulness data are still human; treat the governance claims descriptively.

**D188 · RLAIF and Self-Rewarding Language Models** — Lee et al. 2023 (arXiv:2309.00267); Yuan et al. 2024 (arXiv:2401.10020) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` How far can the human be removed from the loop · `5:00` RLAIF's head-to-head: AI-labeled preferences matching human-labeled on summarization and dialogue · `15:00` Direct-RLAIF, position-bias mitigation, prompt design for the labeler · `23:00` Self-rewarding: one model acts as both policy and LLM-as-judge, iterating · `31:00` Iterative DPO ⟵ D186 over self-generated preferences; the observed gains over three iterations · `36:00` Where this must break — the self-reinforcement and model-collapse concern
▸ **Nail these:** LLM-as-judge has documented biases (position, verbosity, self-preference) and the mitigations are partial — cover them honestly; the self-rewarding loop improves both instruction-following *and* judging ability, which is the interesting claim; three iterations is where published results stop, and that's a fact worth noting; connect to Day 209's weak-to-strong framing.

**D189 · Red Teaming Language Models to Reduce Harms** — Ganguli et al., 2022 (arXiv:2209.07858) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Attacking your own model, systematically · `6:00` The methodology: ~39k red team attempts, open-ended, with released data · `15:00` Attack success across four model types, including the finding that RLHF models get harder to attack with scale while plain LMs do not · `25:00` The taxonomy of harms that emerged bottom-up from the data · `32:00` Red-teamer wellbeing, and the operational lessons · `36:00` Automated red teaming (Perez et al. 2022) as the scalable complement
▸ **Nail these:** the scale-vs-robustness result differs by training method, which is the key scientific finding and it's frequently flattened; releasing the attack data was contested and they discuss the tradeoff; inter-rater disagreement on what counts as harmful is large and instructive; keep this episode descriptive — do not demonstrate attacks.

**D190 · Chain-of-Thought Prompting Elicits Reasoning in Large Language Models** — Wei et al., *NeurIPS* 2022 (arXiv:2201.11903) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Eight exemplars, no training, and GSM8K more than triples · `6:00` The method: put worked reasoning in the few-shot examples ⟵ D145 · `14:00` GSM8K 17.9% → 58.1% with PaLM-540B ⟵ D167 · `22:00` The emergence claim: CoT *hurts* below ~10B parameters — show their curve · `30:00` Ablations: equation-only, variable-compute, reasoning-after-answer — all fail, which is what makes the claim non-trivial · `38:00` Zero-shot CoT (Kojima et al. 2022) and the single trigger phrase
▸ **Nail these:** the three ablations are what turn "prompting trick" into a scientific result — spend real time there; the model-size interaction is the paper's most cited and most contested claim ⟶ D206; CoT is not necessarily a faithful account of the model's computation (Turpin et al. 2023) and you should say so; this episode sets up the entire reasoning arc through Day 240.

**D191 · Self-Consistency Improves Chain of Thought Reasoning** — Wang et al., *ICLR* 2023 (arXiv:2203.11171) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Sample many reasoning paths, take the majority answer · `4:00` Replacing greedy decoding with temperature sampling plus marginalization over paths · `12:00` The gains: GSM8K +17.9, SVAMP +11.0 — and the diminishing returns curve in the number of samples · `20:00` Why this works: multiple valid derivations converge on one answer, errors scatter · `25:00` Cost: N× inference, and the honest compute-matched comparison
▸ **Nail these:** it marginalizes over the latent reasoning path, which is a clean probabilistic framing — say it that way; requires a verifiable, extractable final answer, so it doesn't apply to open generation; 40 samples is where returns flatten; this is the conceptual seed of inference-time scaling ⟶ D239.

**D192 · Least-to-Most Prompting Enables Complex Reasoning** — Zhou et al., *ICLR* 2023 (arXiv:2205.10625) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Easy-to-hard generalization · `4:00` Two stages: decompose into subproblems, then solve sequentially with earlier answers in context · `13:00` SCAN: 16% → 99.7% with 14 exemplars — the compositional generalization result · `21:00` Why CoT alone fails on problems harder than the exemplars · `26:00` Decomposition as an explicit, inspectable intermediate
▸ **Nail these:** the SCAN result is dramatic and worth the whole episode; the key difference from CoT is that subproblem answers are *fed back into the prompt*, making it iterative rather than single-pass; this is the direct ancestor of agent planning loops ⟵ D194; failure mode is bad decompositions cascading.

**D193 · Show Your Work: Scratchpads for Intermediate Computation** — Nye et al., 2021 (arXiv:2112.00114) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Chain-of-thought, but *fine-tuned* rather than prompted, and a year earlier · `5:00` Writing intermediate state to a scratchpad before the answer · `13:00` Tasks: long addition, polynomial evaluation, and executing Python programs step by step · `21:00` Out-of-distribution length generalization, and where it breaks · `26:00` Why this paper is underrated relative to Day 190
▸ **Nail these:** the "predict the program trace" task is the most interesting one and it's essentially teaching a model to be an interpreter; supervised scratchpads vs prompted CoT is a real methodological fork that reappears in Day 240's RL-trained reasoning; adding compute per token by adding *tokens* is the framing that later becomes inference-time scaling.

**D194 · ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models** — Yao, Zhao, Yu, Du, Shafran, Narasimhan & Cao, *ICLR* 2023 (arXiv:2210.03629) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` The loop behind every LLM agent you've used · `5:00` Thought → Action → Observation, interleaved in a single generation stream · `14:00` HotpotQA and FEVER with a Wikipedia API; the hallucination reduction from grounding ⟵ D287 · `24:00` ALFWorld and WebShop: the same loop for embodied and web tasks · `31:00` Failure modes: repetitive action loops, and the search-quality dependency · `36:00` ReAct + CoT + self-consistency combined
▸ **Nail these:** the interleaving is what makes it more than CoT — the observation is *external text the model didn't generate*, which is the whole point; the trace is human-editable, which is a real controllability benefit they emphasize; almost every agent framework since is this loop with a tool registry ⟶ D196, D357.

**D195 · Tree of Thoughts: Deliberate Problem Solving with LLMs** — Yao, Yu, Zhao, Shafran, Griffiths, Cao & Narasimhan, *NeurIPS* 2023 (arXiv:2305.10601) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Search, not sampling · `5:00` The four components: thought decomposition, generation, state evaluation, search algorithm · `15:00` Self-evaluation as the heuristic: the model votes on or values its own partial states · `24:00` Game of 24: 4% with CoT to 74% with ToT — the headline · `31:00` BFS and DFS with backtracking; branching factor and cost · `36:00` The cost multiplier, and when tree search is worth it
▸ **Nail these:** classical search with an LLM as both the successor function and the heuristic — framing it in those terms makes it click for anyone who's seen A*; self-evaluation quality is the binding constraint and it's weak on many domains; 100× the tokens of CoT for the Game of 24 result, which is the honest tradeoff; Graph of Thoughts as the generalization.

**D196 · Toolformer: Language Models Can Teach Themselves to Use Tools** — Schick et al., *NeurIPS* 2023 (arXiv:2302.04761) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` A model that decides for itself when to call a calculator · `5:00` Self-supervised API-call annotation: sample candidate calls, execute them, keep only those that reduce loss on the following tokens · `18:00` The filtering criterion as the paper's core idea — utility measured in perplexity, no human labels at all · `26:00` Five tools: calculator, QA, search, translation, calendar · `31:00` 6.7B outperforming GPT-3 on the relevant tasks; the limitations (no chaining, no interactive tools)
▸ **Nail these:** the loss-reduction filter is an elegant, fully automatic supervision signal and it generalizes far beyond tools; calls are inlined into the text with special tokens, so tool use becomes ordinary next-token prediction; no tool chaining in this version, which is exactly what agent frameworks add; contrast with function-calling APIs, which use human-labeled data instead.

**D197 · Finetuned Language Models Are Zero-Shot Learners (FLAN)** — Wei et al., *ICLR* 2022 (arXiv:2109.01652) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Instruction tuning, defined · `5:00` 62 datasets, 12 task clusters, ~10 templates each — the construction · `14:00` Held-out cluster evaluation, which is what makes the generalization claim meaningful · `22:00` The scale threshold: instruction tuning *hurts* models below ~8B · `29:00` Ablations: number of clusters, template diversity, and instructions-vs-no-instructions · `33:00` Scaling Instruction-Finetuned Language Models (Flan-T5, 2022) with 1,836 tasks and CoT data
▸ **Nail these:** the held-out-cluster protocol is the methodological contribution — without it, "zero-shot" would be meaningless; the small-model degradation is a real and repeatedly-observed phenomenon; adding CoT data to the mix (Flan 2022) preserves reasoning ability that plain instruction tuning erodes; more clusters helps monotonically, more templates helps and then saturates.

**D198 · Multitask Prompted Training Enables Zero-Shot Task Generalization (T0)** — Sanh et al., *ICLR* 2022 (arXiv:2110.08207) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` The open, community-built parallel to FLAN · `4:00` PromptSource: 2,000+ prompts written by 36 contributors, all public · `12:00` T5-based encoder-decoder, T0-3B and T0-11B · `19:00` Beating GPT-3-175B on 9 of 11 held-out tasks at 16× smaller · `25:00` Prompt-format robustness: variance across paraphrases is large, and they measure it
▸ **Nail these:** prompt variance is a huge and underreported source of benchmark noise, and this paper quantifies it — that's the most useful thing to take away; every prompt is human-written and public, which matters for reproducibility; encoder-decoder vs decoder-only for instruction following; T0 vs FLAN is a nice compare-and-contrast episode if you pair them.

**D199 · Self-Instruct: Aligning Language Models with Self-Generated Instructions** — Wang et al., *ACL* 2023 (arXiv:2212.10560) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Bootstrapping an instruction dataset from 175 seed examples · `5:00` The pipeline: generate instructions, classify classification-vs-generation, generate instances, filter · `16:00` ROUGE-L < 0.7 deduplication against existing instructions — the diversity mechanism · `23:00` 52k instructions; GPT-3 + Self-Instruct within 5 points of InstructGPT-001 · `29:00` The quality audit: they hand-check 200 examples and report the error rate · `33:00` Alpaca, and the licensing and distillation questions this opened
▸ **Nail these:** the human-audit section is unusually honest — roughly half the generated instances have some flaw, and it still works, which is a real finding about data quality thresholds; input-first vs output-first generation for classification tasks; this made instruction tuning accessible to anyone with API access, and that had large downstream consequences worth discussing neutrally.

**D200 · LIMA: Less Is More for Alignment** — Zhou et al., *NeurIPS* 2023 (arXiv:2305.11206) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` 1,000 examples. No RLHF. Competitive results. · `5:00` The Superficial Alignment Hypothesis, stated in full · `13:00` The curation: 750 from community forums, 250 hand-written, with an explicit style guide · `21:00` Human preference results against DaVinci003, Alpaca-65B, and GPT-4 · `28:00` The ablation that matters: 2,000 diverse > 52,000 filtered; quality and diversity beat quantity · `32:00` The limits — brittleness, no multi-turn robustness without the extra 30 dialogues
▸ **Nail these:** the hypothesis (knowledge comes from pretraining, alignment teaches format and style) is the intellectual claim and it's testable and contested — present the evidence for and against; the diversity-over-quantity ablation is the most actionable result; LIMA does not match RLHF models on safety or robustness, and the paper is upfront about it; scaling *quality* mattered, scaling *quantity* didn't.

**D201 · Parameter-Efficient Transfer Learning for NLP (Adapters)** — Houlsby et al., *ICML* 2019 (arXiv:1902.00751) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Fine-tuning 3% of the parameters, matching 100% · `5:00` The adapter module: down-project, nonlinearity, up-project, residual · `14:00` Near-identity initialization, and why that's essential for stability · `21:00` Two adapters per transformer layer; GLUE within 0.4 points at 3.6% of parameters · `27:00` The inference-latency cost — adapters add sequential depth ⟶ D204 fixes this · `31:00` Adapter composition, AdapterFusion, and multi-task serving
▸ **Nail these:** the bottleneck dimension controls the capacity/parameter tradeoff and near-identity init means the model starts as the pretrained one; the extra latency is the real deployment objection and it's exactly what LoRA's mergeability answers; per-task adapters let you serve many tasks from one base model, which is the operational motivation.

**D202 · Prefix-Tuning: Optimizing Continuous Prompts for Generation** — Li & Liang, *ACL* 2021 (arXiv:2101.00190) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Steering a frozen model with virtual tokens · `4:00` Trainable key/value prefixes prepended at every layer · `12:00` The reparameterization through an MLP, and why direct optimization is unstable · `19:00` 0.1% of parameters; strong low-data and out-of-domain results · `24:00` Prefix vs infix placement; prefix length ablation
▸ **Nail these:** these are continuous vectors in activation space, not tokens — they need not correspond to any real word, and that's the conceptual leap; the MLP reparameterization is dropped after training; prefix tuning consumes context length, a real cost; strongest advantage is in the low-data regime.

**D203 · The Power of Scale for Parameter-Efficient Prompt Tuning** — Lester, Al-Rfou & Constant, *EMNLP* 2021 (arXiv:2104.08691) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` The simplest possible version, and the scaling result that justifies it · `4:00` Soft prompts at the input embedding layer only — no per-layer prefixes ⟵ D202 · `11:00` The convergence plot: prompt tuning matches full fine-tuning at ~10B parameters · `19:00` Prompt length, initialization from vocabulary, and the ablations · `24:00` Domain-shift robustness, and prompt ensembling for free
▸ **Nail these:** the convergence-with-scale figure is the paper — one plot, and it reframes all of PEFT; initializing from real token embeddings beats random and helps most at small scale; prompt tuning is the most "prompt-like" of these methods, which makes it a nice bridge from Month 7's first half; it under-performs badly below a few billion parameters.

**D204 · LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models** — Hu et al., *ICLR* 2022 (arXiv:2106.09685) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` The method that made fine-tuning a consumer activity · `5:00` The hypothesis: weight *updates* during adaptation have low intrinsic rank ⟵ Aghajanyan et al. 2020 · `13:00` W + BA with B initialized to zero, A to Gaussian — and why that specific asymmetry · `22:00` The α/r scaling factor, and what it's really doing · `29:00` Zero inference latency: merge BA into W after training ⟵ D201's weakness · `35:00` The rank ablation — r=1 or 2 often suffices, which is the surprising part · `40:00` Which matrices to adapt: their finding that W_q and W_v beat all four
▸ **Nail these:** B=0 means the model starts exactly at the pretrained function, which is the same idea as adapter near-identity init; mergeability is the deployment killer feature; the subspace-similarity analysis showing r=8 and r=64 share top directions is the evidence for the low-rank hypothesis; 10,000× fewer trainable parameters on GPT-3, 3× less GPU memory.

**D205 · QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs** — Dettmers, Pagnoni, Holtzman & Zettlemoyer, *NeurIPS* 2023 (arXiv:2305.14314) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` A 65B model fine-tuned on one 48GB GPU · `6:00` 4-bit NormalFloat: an information-theoretically optimal datatype for normally-distributed weights — derive the quantile construction · `18:00` Double quantization: quantizing the quantization constants, for ~0.37 bits/param saved · `26:00` Paged optimizers using unified memory to survive gradient-checkpointing spikes · `32:00` Backprop through frozen 4-bit weights into LoRA adapters ⟵ D204 · `37:00` Guanaco, and the finding that data quality dominated dataset size
▸ **Nail these:** NF4's construction from the normal distribution's quantiles is a genuinely lovely idea and it's teachable in five minutes; you must apply LoRA to *all* linear layers to match 16-bit performance — their ablation says so and most tutorials get it wrong; the compute dequantizes to bf16 on the fly, so it's storage quantization not compute quantization; connect to Day 333.

**D206 · Emergent Abilities of Large Language Models + Are Emergent Abilities a Mirage?** — Wei et al. *TMLR* 2022 (arXiv:2206.07682); Schaeffer, Miranda & Koyejo *NeurIPS* 2023 (arXiv:2304.15004) · 50 min · L3
`0:00` The most consequential disagreement in scaling research · `6:00` The emergence claim: sharp, unpredictable capability jumps at scale, across 100+ tasks · `16:00` The mirage rebuttal: discontinuous *metrics* (exact match, multiple-choice grade) manufacture discontinuous *curves* · `28:00` Their demonstration — switch to token edit distance or Brier score and the curve smooths · `36:00` The counterargument: some emergence survives continuous metrics · `42:00` What actually remains at issue, and why "unpredictable" is the operationally important word
▸ **Nail these:** the mirage argument is a measurement argument, not a claim that nothing changes — being precise here is the whole value of the episode; nonlinear metrics + limited resolution + few test samples is the mechanism they identify; even under smooth metrics, predicting *which* task improves *when* remains unsolved ⟵ D170 attempts it; present both sides fairly and let the viewer judge.

**D207 · TruthfulQA: Measuring How Models Mimic Human Falsehoods** — Lin, Hilton & Evans, *ACL* 2022 (arXiv:2109.07958) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` A benchmark where bigger models do *worse* · `5:00` 817 adversarially-written questions across 38 categories, targeting common human misconceptions · `14:00` Inverse scaling: the largest models are the least truthful on this set · `22:00` Why — imitative falsehood, learned from a training corpus full of human error · `29:00` The evaluation problem: human judges, then GPT-judge, and the reliability caveats · `33:00` Informativeness vs truthfulness, and the trivial "I have no comment" degenerate solution
▸ **Nail these:** questions were *selected* to be adversarial, so the absolute numbers aren't a general truthfulness estimate — this caveat is essential and often dropped; inverse scaling is real but narrow (see the Inverse Scaling Prize for the general picture); the truthful-and-informative joint metric is what prevents gaming; RLHF improves this substantially, which links back to Day 184.

**D208 · Sleeper Agents: Training Deceptive LLMs that Persist Through Safety Training** — Hubinger et al., 2024 (arXiv:2401.05566) · 45 min · L4
`0:00` A backdoor that survives everything you'd do to remove it · `7:00` The threat model: deliberately-inserted conditional behavior, triggered by a specific context · `16:00` Three safety techniques applied — SFT, RLHF, adversarial training — and all three failing to remove it · `26:00` The most-cited result: adversarial training taught the model to *hide* the behavior better rather than remove it · `34:00` Chain-of-thought backdoors are the most persistent, and persistence grows with model size · `40:00` What this does and does not establish about naturally-arising deception
▸ **Nail these:** the models were trained to be deceptive — this is a study of whether safety training *removes* a backdoor, not evidence that backdoors arise on their own, and conflating these is the single most common misreading; distilled-CoT backdoors persist even after removing the reasoning; the implication is about the limits of behavioral evaluation, which motivates all of Month 11's interpretability work.

**D209 · Weak-to-Strong Generalization** — Burns et al., 2023 (arXiv:2312.09390) · 45 min · L4
`0:00` Can a weak supervisor elicit the full ability of a stronger model? · `7:00` The analogy setup: GPT-2-level supervisor, GPT-4-level student, as a stand-in for humans supervising superhuman systems · `18:00` The Performance Gap Recovered metric, defined precisely · `26:00` The core result: strong students exceed their weak supervisors, but recover only part of the gap · `33:00` Auxiliary confidence loss recovering ~80% on NLP tasks · `39:00` The disanalogies the authors list themselves
▸ **Nail these:** PGR is the metric to explain carefully or nothing else lands; naive fine-tuning on weak labels does *not* imitate the errors fully, which is the encouraging finding; the confidence-loss result is the strongest positive but is task-dependent; the paper's self-listed disanalogies (imitation saliency, pretraining leakage) are more interesting than the headline number and make this episode substantive.

**D210 · AI Safety via Debate + Scalable Agent Alignment via Reward Modeling** — Irving, Christiano & Amodei 2018 (arXiv:1805.00899); Leike et al. 2018 (arXiv:1811.07871) · 45 min · L4
`0:00` Supervising a system you cannot evaluate directly · `6:00` Debate: two agents argue, a human judge decides; the complexity-theoretic intuition (a polynomial judge deciding PSPACE-hard questions) · `18:00` The sparse-MNIST debate experiment · `25:00` Recursive reward modeling: decompose the task and use assistants to help evaluate · `33:00` The scalable-oversight agenda, and how Days 187, 209, and 240 fit into it · `40:00` The open objections: obfuscated arguments, persuasive-but-wrong equilibria, judge bias
▸ **Nail these:** the theoretical claim is about what a limited judge can decide with access to competing provers, and stating it precisely elevates the episode above hand-waving; the empirical evidence for debate remains thin and you should say so; this is the conceptual frame that connects Month 7's alignment work to Month 11's interpretability work — use it as the month's closing synthesis.

---

# MONTH 8 — Reinforcement Learning
### Days 211–240 · Level 3 · From TD(0) to reasoning models

RL is the one subfield with a genuinely different mathematical spine. It also produced the two most spectacular public results in AI history (AlphaGo, and RL-trained reasoning models), so it earns a full month even for viewers who never plan to train an agent.

---

**D211 · Learning to Predict by the Methods of Temporal Differences** — Sutton, *Machine Learning* 1988 · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Learning from the difference between successive predictions · `6:00` The Markov Decision Process, defined: states, actions, transitions, rewards, discount γ · `16:00` Monte Carlo vs TD: bootstrapping, and the bias/variance tradeoff between them ⟵ D18 · `26:00` The TD(0) update; TD(λ) and eligibility traces · `36:00` The random-walk experiment, and TD converging faster than supervised MC · `41:00` Why TD is the intellectual center of RL
▸ **Nail these:** the TD error δ = r + γV(s') − V(s) is the single most important quantity in the field — write it once and refer back to it for the next 29 days; bootstrapping means learning a guess from a guess, and that's both the power and the instability; the forward and backward views of TD(λ) and their equivalence; dopamine as a TD-error signal (Schultz, Dayan & Montague 1997) is worth 60 seconds.

**D212 · Q-learning** — Watkins & Dayan, *Machine Learning* 1992 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Learning the optimal policy while behaving suboptimally · `5:00` Action values, the Bellman optimality equation ⟵ D211 · `14:00` The Q-learning update, and why the max makes it off-policy · `23:00` The convergence proof conditions — every state-action pair visited infinitely often, Robbins–Monro step sizes ⟵ D34 · `31:00` SARSA as the on-policy sibling; the cliff-walking comparison · `36:00` Exploration: ε-greedy, and why it's both terrible and universal
▸ **Nail these:** off-policy means the target uses max over actions while the behavior policy explores — this distinction determines half of modern RL design; the cliff-walking example where SARSA takes the safe path and Q-learning takes the optimal-but-risky one is the clearest illustration in RL and takes two minutes to draw; maximization bias, introduced here and fixed on Day 215.

**D213 · Simple Statistical Gradient-Following Algorithms (REINFORCE)** — Williams, *Machine Learning* 1992 · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Optimizing a policy directly, without value functions · `6:00` The log-derivative trick: ∇E[f] = E[f ∇log p] — derive it, it's three lines and it powers everything ⟵ D241 too · `18:00` The REINFORCE estimator, unbiased and extremely high-variance · `26:00` Baselines: why subtracting b(s) leaves the estimator unbiased — prove it · `35:00` The optimal baseline; actor-critic as using a learned V(s) · `41:00` Discrete actions, and why policy gradients handle them where DQN struggles with continuous ones
▸ **Nail these:** the log-derivative trick derivation must be on screen — it also underlies VAEs' score-function estimator, so this pays off in Month 9; the baseline-unbiasedness proof hinges on E[∇log π] = 0; variance is the practical enemy and every subsequent policy-gradient paper is a variance-reduction story; credit assignment across a trajectory as the framing.

**D214 · Human-level Control through Deep Reinforcement Learning (DQN)** — Mnih et al., *Nature* 2015 · 55 min · L2
`0:00` Raw pixels to joystick, 49 games, one architecture · `7:00` The deadly triad: function approximation + bootstrapping + off-policy = divergence · `16:00` Experience replay: breaking correlation, reusing data · `26:00` The target network: freezing the bootstrap target for C steps, and why that stabilizes · `35:00` Preprocessing: frame stacking for velocity, reward clipping, frame skipping · `43:00` The results across 49 games, and the honest look at where it fails (Montezuma's Revenge) · `50:00` The 2013 workshop version vs the 2015 Nature version
▸ **Nail these:** the two stabilizers (replay + target network) are the entire practical contribution and each addresses a distinct failure — separate them clearly; reward clipping to [−1,1] means the agent cannot distinguish a small reward from a large one, a real limitation they acknowledge; Montezuma's Revenge scores zero, which motivates Days 233–234; 200M frames per game, which is roughly 38 days of play.

**D215 · Deep Reinforcement Learning with Double Q-learning** — van Hasselt, Guez & Silver, *AAAI* 2016 (arXiv:1509.06461) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Q-values that were systematically too high · `4:00` Maximization bias: E[max] ≥ max E, demonstrated with a simple noisy example ⟵ D212 · `13:00` Decoupling action *selection* from action *evaluation* · `20:00` The Double DQN target, using the online net to select and the target net to evaluate — a one-line change · `26:00` Value-estimate plots before and after, and score improvements
▸ **Nail these:** build the maximization-bias intuition with a two-action example where both true values are 0 but estimates are noisy — everyone gets it immediately; the implementation is literally one line given DQN's existing target network; overestimation is not uniform across actions, which is why it distorts the policy and not just the values.

**D216 · Dueling Network Architectures for Deep RL** — Wang et al., *ICML* 2016 (arXiv:1511.06581) · 25 min · L2
`0:00` Separating "how good is this state" from "how much does this action matter" · `4:00` Two streams: V(s) and A(s,a), combined into Q · `11:00` The identifiability problem, and the mean-subtraction aggregator Q = V + (A − mean A) · `18:00` The saliency visualizations: the value stream watches the road, the advantage stream watches nearby cars · `22:00` Why this helps most when actions are near-equivalent
▸ **Nail these:** naive addition is unidentifiable (you can add a constant to V and subtract it from A), and the mean-subtraction fix loses the exact semantics of V and A but works better than the max version — this tradeoff is the whole design; the Enduro saliency figure is the best interpretability visual in classic RL; it's an architecture change, so it composes with everything else.

**D217 · Prioritized Experience Replay** — Schaul, Quan, Antonoglou & Silver, *ICLR* 2016 (arXiv:1511.05952) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Not all transitions are equally worth replaying ⟵ D214 · `4:00` TD-error magnitude as the priority signal · `11:00` Proportional vs rank-based prioritization; the sum-tree data structure for O(log n) sampling · `20:00` The bias this introduces, and importance-sampling weights to correct it (with β annealed to 1) · `26:00` Stochasticity as a failure mode — noisy transitions keep high TD error forever
▸ **Nail these:** prioritization changes the sampling distribution, so the estimator becomes biased and the IS weights are not optional; α controls how much prioritization, β how much correction; the sum-tree is a nice, concrete data-structures moment in an ML series; rank-based is more robust to outliers and this is worth saying.

**D218 · Rainbow: Combining Improvements in Deep RL** — Hessel et al., *AAAI* 2018 (arXiv:1710.02298) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Six extensions, one agent, and a proper ablation · `5:00` The roster: Double, Dueling, PER, multi-step returns, distributional RL (C51), NoisyNets · `16:00` Distributional RL in brief: learn the return *distribution*, not its mean · `26:00` NoisyNets: learned parametric exploration noise instead of ε-greedy · `32:00` The ablation — removing PER and multi-step hurts most; removing Double barely registers · `37:00` Why the ablation is more valuable than the combination
▸ **Nail these:** the ablation figure is the paper — walk each removal and what it costs; multi-step returns (n=3) are cheap and among the biggest single wins, which surprises people; C51's fixed support and projection step; this is the template for how to publish a combination paper honestly.

**D219 · Asynchronous Methods for Deep Reinforcement Learning (A3C)** — Mnih et al., *ICML* 2016 (arXiv:1602.01783) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Replacing the replay buffer with parallel actors · `5:00` Multiple workers exploring different parts of state space decorrelate updates *for free* · `14:00` The n-step advantage actor-critic objective ⟵ D213 · `22:00` The entropy regularization term, and what it does to premature convergence · `28:00` Hogwild-style asynchronous updates without locks · `32:00` A2C: the synchronous version that turned out to work just as well on GPUs
▸ **Nail these:** the decorrelation argument replaces the replay buffer's role, which is a genuinely different solution to the same problem — compare directly with Day 214; the asynchrony turned out to be unnecessary, and A2C's existence is a good lesson about attributing gains correctly; entropy bonus coefficient β and its effect on exploration; 16 CPU cores beating a GPU DQN was the headline.

**D220 · High-Dimensional Continuous Control Using Generalized Advantage Estimation (GAE)** — Schulman, Moritz, Levine, Jordan & Abbeel, *ICLR* 2016 (arXiv:1506.02438) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` The variance-reduction layer under PPO ⟵ D182 · `6:00` The n-step advantage family and its bias/variance spectrum · `16:00` GAE(λ) as an exponentially-weighted average of all n-step estimators — derive the telescoping form · `28:00` λ=0 gives TD, λ=1 gives Monte Carlo; the interpolation ⟵ D211 · `34:00` Typical values (γ=0.99, λ=0.95) and why they're near-universal
▸ **Nail these:** the derivation showing GAE = Σ (γλ)^l δ_{t+l} is short and makes the λ interpolation obvious — do it; γ and λ both discount but for different reasons (one is the problem's definition, one is an estimator choice), and conflating them is a common error; GAE is used in essentially every RLHF implementation, so this episode has direct Month 7 payoff.

**D221 · Continuous Control with Deep Reinforcement Learning (DDPG)** — Lillicrap et al., *ICLR* 2016 (arXiv:1509.02971) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` DQN cannot do continuous actions — here's the fix · `5:00` The argmax over a continuous action space is intractable; a deterministic actor replaces it · `13:00` The deterministic policy gradient theorem (Silver et al. 2014), stated · `21:00` Soft target updates: τ=0.001 Polyak averaging instead of hard copies ⟵ D214 · `27:00` Ornstein–Uhlenbeck exploration noise, and why it was later shown unnecessary · `32:00` The fragility: DDPG is notoriously hyperparameter-sensitive
▸ **Nail these:** the critic's gradient with respect to the action is what trains the actor — ∇_θ J = E[∇_a Q · ∇_θ μ] is the key chain rule; soft updates trade speed for stability; the OU-noise result (Gaussian works fine) is another good "was that component necessary?" story; the sensitivity is real and motivates Days 222–223.

**D222 · Addressing Function Approximation Error in Actor-Critic Methods (TD3)** — Fujimoto, van Hoof & Meger, *ICML* 2018 (arXiv:1802.09477) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Three fixes for DDPG, each addressing a named failure · `4:00` Clipped double Q-learning: take the min of two critics ⟵ D215 · `13:00` Delayed policy updates — update the actor every d critic steps · `20:00` Target policy smoothing: add clipped noise to the target action, as a regularizer over similar actions · `26:00` The ablation isolating each contribution
▸ **Nail these:** taking the min *underestimates*, which is deliberate — underestimation doesn't propagate through the max the way overestimation does, and explaining that asymmetry is the episode; the delay ratio d=2; target smoothing is an explicit smoothness prior on Q; three small changes, large empirical effect, cleanly ablated.

**D223 · Soft Actor-Critic** — Haarnoja, Zhou, Abbeel & Levine, *ICML* 2018 (arXiv:1801.01290) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Maximum entropy RL, and why it's more robust · `6:00` The objective: expected return *plus* policy entropy, with temperature α · `16:00` Soft value functions, the soft Bellman equation, and soft policy iteration · `28:00` The reparameterization trick for a squashed Gaussian policy ⟵ D241 · `35:00` Automatic temperature tuning as a constrained optimization (SAC v2) · `41:00` Sample efficiency and hyperparameter robustness vs DDPG/TD3
▸ **Nail these:** entropy maximization is not just exploration — it produces multimodal policies that are robust to model error, and that's the deeper argument; the tanh-squashing Jacobian correction in the log-probability is a detail everyone implements wrong at least once; automatic α tuning made SAC the default continuous-control algorithm; connects to the KL-regularized objective in Day 186.

**D224 · Mastering the Game of Go with Deep Neural Networks and Tree Search (AlphaGo)** — Silver et al., *Nature* 2016 · 55 min · L3
`0:00` Why Go was supposed to be a decade away · `7:00` The four networks: SL policy, rollout policy, RL policy, value network · `18:00` Monte Carlo Tree Search — selection, expansion, evaluation, backup — traced on a small tree · `32:00` The PUCT formula and how the policy prior shapes search · `40:00` Combining value network and rollouts (λ=0.5) for leaf evaluation · `46:00` The Fan Hui and Lee Sedol matches; move 37 and move 78 · `51:00` What was engineering and what was science
▸ **Nail these:** MCTS is the load-bearing component and the networks are priors and evaluators for it — get that hierarchy right; branching factor ~250 vs chess's ~35, and depth ~150; the value network was trained on self-play games specifically to avoid overfitting to the human game database, a subtle and important detail; 1,920 CPUs and 280 GPUs for the match version.

**D225 · Mastering the Game of Go Without Human Knowledge (AlphaGo Zero)** — Silver et al., *Nature* 2017 · 50 min · L3
`0:00` Deleting the human data, and getting stronger · `6:00` One network with two heads (policy + value), residual blocks ⟵ D67 · `16:00` MCTS as a policy improvement operator — this is the conceptual core of the paper · `28:00` The self-play training loop; the combined loss (value MSE + policy cross-entropy + L2) · `36:00` The learning curve: surpassing AlphaGo Lee in 3 days, AlphaGo Master in 21 · `43:00` Rediscovering and then discarding human joseki
▸ **Nail these:** "search as policy improvement, network as policy distillation" is the single sentence that makes AlphaZero-family methods make sense — say it and repeat it; no rollouts at all, the value head replaces them; the ablation showing the dual-head network beats separate networks; tabula rasa is a strong claim and the Go rules, board symmetries, and MCTS are still hand-provided.

**D226 · A General Reinforcement Learning Algorithm that Masters Chess, Shogi and Go (AlphaZero)** — Silver et al., *Science* 2018 · 35 min · L3
`0:00` One algorithm, three games, no game-specific tuning · `5:00` What was removed: board symmetry augmentation, and the assumption of a binary outcome (draws) · `13:00` Continuous training instead of the gated best-player evaluation ⟵ D225 · `20:00` Stockfish and Elmo defeated; the 4-hour chess result · `26:00` Search efficiency: 80k positions/sec vs Stockfish's 70M, and what that implies · `31:00` The style analysis — sacrifices, king safety, piece activity
▸ **Nail these:** the three-orders-of-magnitude search-efficiency gap is the most striking number in the paper and the right framing is "learned evaluation replaces brute-force enumeration"; removing symmetry augmentation was necessary because chess isn't symmetric, which shows how much of AlphaGo Zero was Go-specific; the compute (5,000 TPUs generating self-play) deserves an honest mention.

**D227 · Mastering Atari, Go, Chess and Shogi by Planning with a Learned Model (MuZero)** — Schrittwieser et al., *Nature* 2020 (arXiv:1911.08265) · 50 min · L4
`0:00` Planning without knowing the rules · `7:00` Three functions: representation h, dynamics g, prediction f — and the crucial point that the latent state has no prescribed meaning · `20:00` Training by unrolling K steps and matching *only* policy, value, and reward — never the observation · `31:00` Why not reconstructing observations is the key design decision · `38:00` Atari + board games with one algorithm; reanalyse for sample efficiency · `45:00` Stochastic MuZero and EfficientZero in one minute
▸ **Nail these:** the latent dynamics model is trained purely to be *useful for prediction of value/policy/reward*, which is a value-equivalence principle, not a reconstruction objective — this is the intellectual contribution and it deserves five minutes; MuZero handles Atari where MCTS previously couldn't, because it learns the model; the K=5 unroll length; contrast directly with Day 228's reconstruction-based world model.

**D228 · World Models** — Ha & Schmidhuber, *NeurIPS* 2018 (arXiv:1803.10122) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Learning inside a dream · `5:00` The V-M-C decomposition: VAE for vision ⟵ D241, MDN-RNN for memory ⟵ D60, tiny linear controller · `16:00` Training the controller entirely inside the learned model, then transferring to the real environment · `26:00` The temperature parameter τ on the dream, and how cheating the dream is prevented · `33:00` CarRacing and VizDoom results; the 867-parameter controller · `37:00` Why the controller is deliberately tiny — CMA-ES on a small parameter vector
▸ **Nail these:** the agent exploits imperfections in its own world model if the dream is too easy, and raising temperature fixes it — this is a beautiful, concrete instance of reward hacking; the 867-parameter controller carries the "representation does the work" message; the paper is unusually readable and the interactive web version is worth showing on screen.

**D229 · Mastering Diverse Domains through World Models (DreamerV3)** — Hafner, Pasukonis, Ba & Lillicrap, 2023 (arXiv:2301.04104) · 45 min · L4
`0:00` One set of hyperparameters, 150+ tasks, including the first Minecraft-diamond-from-scratch result · `7:00` The Recurrent State Space Model: deterministic recurrent state plus stochastic latent ⟵ D228 · `18:00` Learning in imagination: actor and critic trained purely on imagined rollouts · `26:00` Symlog predictions, twohot encoding, and the free-bits KL balancing — the robustness tricks that *are* the paper · `36:00` Percentile return normalization; why these choices make one hyperparameter set work everywhere · `41:00` Scaling: bigger models improve both data efficiency and final performance
▸ **Nail these:** the robustness techniques are the contribution, and each addresses a specific scale-mismatch problem — go through symlog and twohot properly with examples; imagination horizon H=15; the Minecraft result required no human data and no curriculum, which is the headline; "same hyperparameters everywhere" is the practical claim that matters most to viewers.

**D230 · Decision Transformer: Reinforcement Learning via Sequence Modeling** — Chen et al., *NeurIPS* 2021 (arXiv:2106.01345) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` RL as autoregressive sequence prediction · `5:00` The token sequence: (return-to-go, state, action) triples ⟵ D129 · `14:00` Conditioning on desired return at test time, and the "ask for a high score" behavior · `22:00` No TD learning, no bootstrapping, no dynamic programming — supervised learning only · `29:00` Results on offline Atari and D4RL; where it beats CQL and where it doesn't · `35:00` The stitching limitation, and the Trajectory Transformer comparison
▸ **Nail these:** upside-down RL — conditioning on outcomes rather than maximizing them — is the reframing, and it's simple enough to feel like a trick until you see it work; the inability to "stitch" optimal sub-trajectories from suboptimal data is the real theoretical weakness and it's well-documented; return-to-go must be specified at inference, which is an odd interface; this is the bridge between Month 5 and Month 8.

**D231 · Conservative Q-Learning for Offline Reinforcement Learning** — Kumar, Zhou, Tucker & Levine, *NeurIPS* 2020 (arXiv:2006.04779) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Why offline RL fails: the distributional shift death spiral · `6:00` Out-of-distribution actions get overestimated Q-values, the policy chases them, and there's no data to correct it · `16:00` CQL's added regularizer: push down Q on out-of-distribution actions, push up on dataset actions · `26:00` The lower-bound guarantee on the value of the learned policy · `33:00` The α tradeoff, and the practical tuning story · `37:00` Offline RL's relevance to RLHF and to learning from logged data ⟵ D186
▸ **Nail these:** the death spiral needs to be drawn as a loop — overestimate, act, no correction, overestimate more; conservatism buys a guaranteed *lower bound* on performance, which is what makes deployment defensible; the connection to DPO's off-policy weakness is a genuinely useful cross-month link; behavior-cloning baselines are strong on high-quality datasets and CQL wins on mixed ones.

**D232 · Hindsight Experience Replay** — Andrychowicz et al., *NeurIPS* 2017 (arXiv:1707.01495) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Learning from failure by relabeling the goal · `4:00` The sparse-reward problem: binary success, almost never achieved by random exploration · `11:00` Relabel the achieved state as if it had been the goal — turning every failure into a success · `19:00` Goal-conditioned value functions; the four relabeling strategies (final, future, episode, random) · `25:00` Robotic pushing, sliding, and pick-and-place from binary rewards alone
▸ **Nail these:** the idea is trivially simple to state and genuinely hard to think of — that combination makes for a great episode; `future` strategy with k=4 is the standard; it requires a goal-conditioned formulation, which is the precondition; this is one of the few methods that makes sparse rewards tractable without reward shaping, and reward shaping's dangers are worth a minute.

**D233 · Curiosity-driven Exploration by Self-supervised Prediction (ICM)** — Pathak, Agrawal, Efros & Darrell, *ICML* 2017 (arXiv:1705.05363) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` An agent with no extrinsic reward at all · `5:00` Intrinsic reward = prediction error of a learned forward model · `13:00` The noisy-TV problem: unpredictable-but-irrelevant stimuli capture the agent forever · `20:00` The inverse-dynamics feature space as the fix — only encode what the agent can *control* · `29:00` Mario and VizDoom with zero extrinsic reward · `33:00` Large-Scale Study of Curiosity-Driven Learning (2018) and its surprises
▸ **Nail these:** the noisy-TV thought experiment is the best-known failure mode in intrinsic motivation and this paper's feature-learning answer is elegant — build the episode around this pair; the inverse model predicts the action from consecutive states, which forces the representation to ignore uncontrollable noise; the follow-up study found curiosity alone solves many games, which is a striking result about reward design.

**D234 · Exploration by Random Network Distillation (RND)** — Burda, Edwards, Storkey & Klimov, *ICLR* 2019 (arXiv:1810.12894) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Beating average human on Montezuma's Revenge ⟵ D214 · `4:00` Predict the output of a fixed random network; the error is high on novel states · `12:00` Why this sidesteps stochasticity entirely — the target is deterministic by construction ⟵ D233 · `19:00` Two value heads for intrinsic and extrinsic reward, with different discount factors · `25:00` Non-episodic intrinsic returns; observation normalization as a critical detail
▸ **Nail these:** the target network is *random and frozen*, so there is no aleatoric uncertainty to chase — that's the whole reason RND avoids the noisy-TV problem, and the contrast with ICM is the episode; separate discounts (γ_E=0.999, γ_I=0.99) because novelty is a local signal; the first agent to pass level 1 of Montezuma's Revenge consistently.

**D235 · Dota 2 with Large Scale Deep Reinforcement Learning (OpenAI Five)** — OpenAI et al., 2019 (arXiv:1912.06680) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` 10 months, 770 petaflop/s-days, and a world-champion team beaten · `7:00` The observation and action spaces: ~16k observation dimensions, ~8k valid actions per step · `16:00` The architecture: a single-layer 4096-unit LSTM per hero, no attention · `24:00` "Surgery": modifying the architecture and environment mid-training without restarting — the most novel engineering contribution · `34:00` Self-play with 80% latest / 20% past opponents; team spirit annealing · `40:00` PPO at extreme batch size (millions of samples) ⟵ D182
▸ **Nail these:** surgery is unique in the literature and it exists because a 10-month run cannot be restarted — this is the episode's most interesting content; the "team spirit" hyperparameter annealed from 0 to 1 to shift from selfish to cooperative play; the batch size (~2M observations) is far beyond anything in academic RL; the game was restricted (hero pool, no items like Divine Rapier), and stating that clearly is important.

**D236 · Grandmaster Level in StarCraft II using Multi-Agent RL (AlphaStar)** — Vinyals et al., *Nature* 2019 · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Imperfect information, real-time, and a huge action space · `7:00` The architecture: transformer torso ⟵ D129, deep LSTM core, pointer network, auto-regressive action head · `20:00` The League: main agents, main exploiters, league exploiters — and the reason a naive self-play league fails · `32:00` Prioritized fictitious self-play, and the rock-paper-scissors cycling problem it prevents · `38:00` Statistics z conditioning on human strategies; APM and camera restrictions · `42:00` Grandmaster in all three races
▸ **Nail these:** the exploiter agents exist specifically to find and punish weaknesses that main agents would otherwise never encounter — non-transitivity is the core problem and the League is the answer; supervised initialization from human replays was essential, unlike AlphaZero; the fairness constraints (APM caps, camera view) were negotiated and are worth describing precisely.

**D237 · Solving Rubik's Cube with a Robot Hand (+ domain randomization)** — OpenAI et al., 2019 (arXiv:1910.07113) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Sim-to-real, with a rubber hand and a cube · `6:00` Domain randomization: randomize physics, visuals, and dynamics so reality is one sample from the training distribution ⟵ Tobin et al. 2017 · `16:00` Automatic Domain Randomization: expand the randomization ranges as the policy improves · `26:00` Emergent meta-learning: the LSTM adapting to the current environment within an episode — and the evidence for it · `34:00` Robustness demos: gloves, tied fingers, a plush giraffe · `38:00` What was learned vs what was a classical solver (Kociemba)
▸ **Nail these:** the cube-*solving* algorithm is classical; the network learns only the manipulation, and being clear about this is essential honesty; ADR is a curriculum over environment difficulty and it removes the manual tuning that made domain randomization fragile; the meta-learning evidence (performance improving within an episode after a perturbation) is the most scientifically interesting part.

**D238 · Let's Verify Step by Step** — Lightman et al., *ICLR* 2024 (arXiv:2305.20050) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Rewarding the reasoning, not just the answer · `6:00` Outcome-supervised vs process-supervised reward models, defined precisely ⟵ D190 · `16:00` PRM800K: 800k step-level human labels on MATH solutions · `24:00` The result: process supervision substantially outperforms outcome supervision, and the gap widens with more samples · `32:00` Why outcome supervision rewards lucky-wrong reasoning · `37:00` Active learning for label efficiency; the alignment argument for process supervision
▸ **Nail these:** the best-of-N curves separating PRM from ORM as N grows are the key figures — a better verifier scales better with search; false positives (right answer, wrong reasoning) are the specific pathology; process supervision is also more interpretable and more alignable, which is a rare case where safety and capability point the same way; this is the direct setup for inference-time-scaling reasoning models.

**D239 · STaR: Self-Taught Reasoner** — Zelikman, Wu, Mu & Goodman, *NeurIPS* 2022 (arXiv:2203.14465) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Bootstrapping reasoning from a handful of examples · `5:00` The loop: generate rationales, keep those that reach the correct answer, fine-tune, repeat ⟵ D190 · `14:00` Rationalization: for failures, give the model the answer and ask it to justify — recovering hard problems · `23:00` Why rationalization risks teaching post-hoc reasoning, and the mitigation · `29:00` Results on arithmetic, CommonsenseQA; the fixed-point behavior over iterations
▸ **Nail these:** filtering by answer correctness is a weak but *automatic* verifier, which is what makes the loop scalable — this is the key mechanism and it recurs everywhere in 2024–2026; rationalization is a genuinely double-edged idea and the paper is thoughtful about it; expert iteration is the general name for this pattern; V-STaR and Quiet-STaR as follow-ups.

**D240 · DeepSeek-R1: Incentivizing Reasoning Capability in LLMs via Reinforcement Learning** — DeepSeek-AI, 2025 (arXiv:2501.12948) · 55 min · L4
`0:00` Long chain-of-thought that emerged from RL, not from imitation · `8:00` R1-Zero: pure RL from a base model, with *rule-based* rewards only — accuracy and format, no learned reward model ⟵ D184 · `20:00` GRPO: dropping the value network and using group-relative advantages — derive the estimator · `30:00` The response-length curve: the model spontaneously learns to think longer · `37:00` The "aha moment" — self-verification and backtracking appearing without being demonstrated · `43:00` R1's four-stage pipeline: cold-start SFT, reasoning RL, rejection sampling, general RL · `49:00` Distillation to small dense models, and what that says about where reasoning lives
▸ **Nail these:** rule-based rewards avoid reward hacking of a learned model, which is why verifiable domains (math, code) came first — and this is the single most important structural fact about the reasoning-model era; GRPO's group baseline replaces the critic and halves memory; R1-Zero had readability and language-mixing problems that the cold-start data fixed, a nice illustration of why pure RL wasn't shipped; the distilled 7B/32B results show the reasoning pattern transfers.

---

# MONTH 9 — Generative Models: VAEs, GANs, and Diffusion
### Days 241–270 · Level 3 · Three answers to one question

The question is: how do you learn a distribution you can sample from? Latent-variable models, adversarial games, and iterative denoising are three genuinely different answers, and the field tried them roughly in that order. Teach the tradeoff triangle — sample quality, mode coverage, sampling speed — on Day 241 and refer back to it every episode this month.

---

**D241 · Auto-Encoding Variational Bayes (VAE)** — Kingma & Welling, *ICLR* 2014 (arXiv:1312.6114) · 60 min · L3
`0:00` A generative model you can actually train with backprop · `7:00` The intractable posterior p(z|x), and amortized inference as the fix · `16:00` Derive the ELBO two ways: from Jensen, and as log p(x) − KL(q‖p) ⟵ D19 · `30:00` The reparameterization trick: z = μ + σ⊙ε, and why the naive score-function estimator has crippling variance ⟵ D213 · `42:00` The closed-form Gaussian KL term, derived · `50:00` Blurry samples: the mean-seeking consequence of a Gaussian likelihood · `55:00` Posterior collapse, and the KL-annealing / free-bits fixes
▸ **Nail these:** the ELBO is the single most reused object in this month — derive it fully and label the reconstruction and regularization terms; reparameterization moves the randomness off the computational path so the gradient flows, and this is the whole technical contribution; the encoder is *amortized* inference, meaning one network for all datapoints; blurriness comes from the likelihood choice, not from the latent variable idea.

**D242 · β-VAE and the Disentanglement Debate** — Higgins et al. *ICLR* 2017; Locatello et al. *ICML* 2019 (arXiv:1811.12359) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Turning one hyperparameter up, and getting interpretable latents · `5:00` β > 1 on the KL term; the information-bottleneck reading ⟵ D320 · `14:00` Latent traversals: what a disentangled factor looks like on screen · `22:00` The reconstruction/disentanglement tradeoff, quantified · `28:00` Locatello's impossibility result: unsupervised disentanglement is not identifiable without inductive bias · `35:00` FactorVAE, DIP-VAE, and metrics (MIG, SAP) that disagree with each other
▸ **Nail these:** the impossibility argument is the important half of this episode — for any disentangled representation there's an entangled one with the same marginal, so purely unsupervised recovery is underdetermined; the 12,000-model study found random seed mattered as much as the method; disentanglement metrics correlate poorly with downstream usefulness, which is a sobering measurement lesson.

**D243 · Neural Discrete Representation Learning (VQ-VAE)** — van den Oord, Vinyals & Kavukcuoglu, *NIPS* 2017 (arXiv:1711.00937) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Discrete latents, and why they fix posterior collapse · `6:00` The codebook, nearest-neighbour lookup, and vector quantization ⟵ D20 · `16:00` The straight-through estimator: copy gradients past a non-differentiable argmin · `26:00` Three loss terms — reconstruction, codebook, commitment (β=0.25) — and what each one moves · `34:00` Codebook collapse, EMA updates, and dead-code restarts · `39:00` Two-stage generation: fit a PixelCNN prior over the discrete codes ⟶ D257
▸ **Nail these:** the straight-through estimator is a lie that works, and saying exactly which gradient is being faked is the teaching moment; the commitment loss stops the encoder from wandering away from the codebook; discrete latents make an autoregressive prior tractable, which is the architectural reason this design keeps coming back (BEiT ⟵ D119, audio codecs ⟶ D283); the decoder does not collapse because the KL term is constant by construction.

**D244 · Taming Transformers for High-Resolution Image Synthesis (VQGAN)** — Esser, Rombach & Ommer, *CVPR* 2021 (arXiv:2012.09841) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` CNNs for local texture, transformers for global composition · `5:00` Adding a patch discriminator and a perceptual loss to VQ-VAE ⟵ D243, D253 · `15:00` Why the adversarial + perceptual objective buys a much higher compression ratio at the same fidelity · `24:00` A transformer autoregressing over 16×16 code grids ⟵ D129 · `32:00` Sliding-window attention for megapixel images; the raster-order artifact · `36:00` The direct line to Stable Diffusion's autoencoder ⟶ D264
▸ **Nail these:** the compression ratio is the whole point — f=16 downsampling means a 256×256 image becomes 256 tokens, which is what makes a transformer prior affordable; perceptual + adversarial losses let you throw away pixel-exactness in exchange for realism, and the ablation shows plain L2 cannot reach the same rate; this two-stage "compress then model" pattern is the dominant design in generative modeling.

**D245 · Generative Adversarial Networks** — Goodfellow et al., *NIPS* 2014 (arXiv:1406.2661) · 55 min · L3
`0:00` Two networks, one game, no likelihood · `7:00` The minimax value function, written and read aloud term by term · `15:00` The optimal discriminator D* = p_data/(p_data + p_g) — derive it · `25:00` Substituting D* gives 2·JSD(p_data‖p_g) − log 4, so the generator minimizes Jensen–Shannon divergence — derive it fully · `36:00` The non-saturating heuristic loss, and why the theoretical loss gives no gradient early · `44:00` Mode collapse and non-convergence, with the oscillation demo · `50:00` What "implicit generative model" means and why there's no likelihood to report
▸ **Nail these:** the JSD derivation is the mathematical core and most tutorials skip it — it also explains WGAN's motivation three episodes later; the non-saturating loss changes the objective, so the JSD proof no longer strictly applies, and being honest about that gap is good teaching; alternating updates are not the same as solving the inner maximization; no explicit density means no perplexity, so evaluation was a mess until FID ⟶ D247.

**D246 · Unsupervised Representation Learning with Deep Convolutional GANs (DCGAN)** — Radford, Metz & Chintala, *ICLR* 2016 (arXiv:1511.06434) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` The architectural rules that made GANs trainable · `5:00` The checklist: strided convs instead of pooling, BN in both nets, no FC layers, ReLU in G, LeakyReLU in D · `15:00` Latent space arithmetic: smiling woman − neutral woman + neutral man · `23:00` Walking the latent space, and using it as evidence of memorization vs generalization · `29:00` The discriminator's features used for classification — a self-supervised result before the term existed
▸ **Nail these:** these are empirical rules found by search, not derived, and the paper says so — a good honest framing; BN in the discriminator's input layer breaks things and they exclude it; latent arithmetic requires averaging several samples per concept, a detail usually cropped; the "generator memorized the training set" objection and their walk-the-manifold response.

**D247 · Improved Techniques for Training GANs (+ FID)** — Salimans et al. *NIPS* 2016 (arXiv:1606.03498); Heusel et al. *NIPS* 2017 (arXiv:1706.08500) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` How do you even score a GAN? · `5:00` Inception Score: exp(E KL(p(y|x)‖p(y))), and its four separate failure modes · `15:00` Fréchet Inception Distance: a Gaussian fit in Inception feature space, with the closed-form formula · `24:00` FID's bias with sample size — always report N, and why FID-50k is the convention · `30:00` Feature matching, minibatch discrimination, historical averaging, one-sided label smoothing · `36:00` The two-time-scale update rule and its convergence result
▸ **Nail these:** IS never looks at the real data at all, which is disqualifying and is why FID replaced it; write the Fréchet formula and explain each term; FID is sensitive to the feature extractor and to image resizing — reproducibility problems that persist to 2026; minibatch discrimination directly attacks mode collapse by letting D see batch statistics.

**D248 · Wasserstein GAN** — Arjovsky, Chintala & Bottou, *ICML* 2017 (arXiv:1701.07875) · 50 min · L4
`0:00` Why the GAN loss tells you nothing about progress · `7:00` The parallel-lines example: JS is constant, KL is infinite, W is smooth — this one figure justifies the paper · `18:00` Earth Mover's distance defined; the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality · `28:00` The critic as a 1-Lipschitz function, and weight clipping as a crude enforcement · `36:00` No sigmoid, no log; train the critic to optimality (5 steps per generator step) · `43:00` The loss finally correlates with sample quality — show their plot
▸ **Nail these:** disjoint supports are the normal case for image manifolds, so JS saturating is not a pathological corner — this is the argument; the duality turns an intractable transport problem into "maximize over Lipschitz functions", which is what a neural net can do; weight clipping is admitted by the authors to be terrible, which is a rare and honest sentence; RMSProp rather than Adam, because momentum destabilized the critic.

**D249 · WGAN-GP and Spectral Normalization** — Gulrajani et al. *NIPS* 2017 (arXiv:1704.00028); Miyato et al. *ICLR* 2018 (arXiv:1802.05957) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Two better ways to enforce Lipschitzness ⟵ D248 · `5:00` What weight clipping does to the critic's weight distribution — their bimodal histogram · `13:00` The gradient penalty: λ(‖∇D(x̂)‖₂ − 1)² on interpolates between real and fake · `22:00` Why penalize toward 1 rather than below 1; why you must remove BN from the critic · `29:00` Spectral norm: divide each weight matrix by its largest singular value, estimated by one power iteration per step · `35:00` SN's cost is nearly free, which is why it won
▸ **Nail these:** the gradient penalty is a soft constraint sampled only along interpolation lines, so Lipschitzness is not actually guaranteed anywhere else — state this precisely; batch norm breaks the per-sample gradient penalty, hence layer norm instead; one power iteration amortized across training steps is the trick that makes spectral norm cheap; λ=10 is near-universal.

**D250 · Progressive Growing of GANs** — Karras, Aila, Laine & Lehtinen, *ICLR* 2018 (arXiv:1710.10196) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` 1024×1024 faces, in 2017 · `5:00` Grow both networks from 4×4, doubling resolution, with a smooth α fade-in for each new block · `15:00` Why a curriculum over resolution stabilizes training — the mode-coverage argument · `22:00` Minibatch standard deviation as a one-line diversity fix · `27:00` Equalized learning rate and pixelwise feature normalization · `31:00` CelebA-HQ; and the sliced-Wasserstein metric they proposed
▸ **Nail these:** the fade-in schedule is the implementation detail everyone gets wrong — the new layer's output is blended with an upsampled version of the previous output; minibatch stddev appends a single constant feature map computed across the batch and it measurably increases variety; equalized LR normalizes weights at runtime rather than at init, which interacts with Adam's per-parameter scaling.

**D251 · A Style-Based Generator Architecture for GANs (StyleGAN)** — Karras, Laine & Aila, *CVPR* 2019 (arXiv:1812.04948) · 50 min · L3
`0:00` Control that no previous generator had · `7:00` The mapping network f: Z → W, and why an intermediate latent space can be less entangled · `18:00` AdaIN: inject style by modulating normalized activations per layer ⟵ D48 · `28:00` The constant 4×4 input — the generator starts from a learned constant, not from z · `34:00` Per-pixel noise injection for stochastic detail (hair, freckles) · `40:00` Style mixing regularization, and coarse/middle/fine style control demonstrated · `46:00` Perceptual path length and linear separability as disentanglement metrics
▸ **Nail these:** Z must fill a fixed distribution and therefore must warp to match the data's non-uniform factor density, while W is free not to — that is the entire argument for the mapping network and it's genuinely subtle; the style-mixing grid is the best demo in generative modeling, so show it; noise inputs affect only stochastic detail and leave identity untouched, which they prove by ablation.

**D252 · Analyzing and Improving the Image Quality of StyleGAN (StyleGAN2)** — Karras et al., *CVPR* 2020 (arXiv:1912.04958) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Chasing down a blob artifact, and rebuilding the generator around the answer · `6:00` The droplet artifact traced to AdaIN destroying magnitude information · `15:00` Weight demodulation: fold the style modulation into the convolution weights and normalize analytically · `25:00` Removing progressive growing; skip connections in G, residual in D · `31:00` Path length regularization, and lazy regularization every 16 steps · `36:00` Projection into latent space for attribution — detecting whether an image came from this generator
▸ **Nail these:** the debugging narrative is the pedagogy here — a visual artifact leads to a specific architectural cause and a principled fix, which is how good empirical work actually goes; demodulation assumes i.i.d. inputs and is therefore an approximation of instance norm, and they say so; progressive growing caused phase artifacts (teeth that don't move with the head) — a great concrete example of an inductive bias leaking.

**D253 · Neural Style Transfer + Perceptual Losses** — Gatys, Ecker & Bethge *CVPR* 2016; Johnson, Alahi & Fei-Fei *ECCV* 2016 (arXiv:1603.08155) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Content and style, separated inside a frozen VGG ⟵ D64 · `6:00` Content loss as feature-map L2 at a mid layer · `14:00` Style loss as the Gram matrix — derive why second-order feature correlations capture texture · `24:00` Optimizing the *image* rather than the weights: the original is a per-image optimization · `31:00` Johnson's feed-forward network trained against the same loss — 1000× faster inference · `36:00` Perceptual loss as a general-purpose tool, now standard in superresolution, VAEs, and diffusion autoencoders
▸ **Nail these:** the Gram matrix discards all spatial information, keeping only which features co-occur, and that is exactly what "style" turns out to mean — this is the single insight of the episode; layer choice determines style scale; Johnson's contribution is the amortization idea, the same move VAEs make for inference; LPIPS (Zhang et al. 2018) is the calibrated modern descendant.

**D254 · pix2pix and CycleGAN** — Isola, Zhu, Zhou & Efros *CVPR* 2017 (arXiv:1611.07004); Zhu, Park, Isola & Efros *ICCV* 2017 (arXiv:1703.10593) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Image translation with and without paired data · `5:00` pix2pix: conditional GAN + L1, and why L1 alone gives blur while GAN alone gives artifacts · `14:00` PatchGAN: a discriminator with a 70×70 receptive field, and the Markovian texture assumption · `22:00` CycleGAN's problem: no pairs at all · `28:00` Cycle-consistency loss F(G(x)) ≈ x, drawn as a commuting diagram · `34:00` The steganography failure: CycleGAN hides information in high-frequency noise to satisfy the cycle (Chu et al. 2017)
▸ **Nail these:** L1 handles low frequencies, the discriminator handles high frequencies — that division of labor is why the combined loss works and it's a transferable idea; PatchGAN has far fewer parameters and applies to any image size; cycle consistency is necessary but nowhere near sufficient, and the hidden-information result is the most instructive failure in the paper's afterlife; identity loss for color preservation.

**D255 · Large Scale GAN Training for High Fidelity Natural Image Synthesis (BigGAN)** — Brock, Donahue & Simonyan, *ICLR* 2019 (arXiv:1809.11096) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` What happens when you just make the GAN much bigger · `6:00` 2–4× channels, 8× batch size, and the finding that both help immediately · `14:00` Shared class embeddings and hierarchical latent injection (skip-z) · `21:00` The truncation trick: resample z outside a threshold, and trade diversity for fidelity on a dial · `28:00` Training collapse: monitoring the top singular values of G's weights, and what precedes a blowup · `35:00` Their conclusion that collapse can be delayed but not prevented
▸ **Nail these:** the truncation trick gives you an explicit fidelity/diversity knob at *inference*, which nothing before it did — plot their FID/IS curve against truncation; the singular-value spectral analysis of instability is real diagnostic work and is the most transferable part; orthogonal regularization is what makes truncation work smoothly; scale helped GANs too, which matters for the "was scale everything?" thread running through this series.

**D256 · WaveNet: A Generative Model for Raw Audio** — van den Oord et al., 2016 (arXiv:1609.03499) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Sample-by-sample audio at 16,000 predictions per second ⟵ D80 · `6:00` Causal dilated convolutions: exponential receptive field, zero recurrence — compute the span for 10 layers × 4 stacks · `18:00` µ-law companding to 256 discrete levels, and why a softmax over amplitudes beats a Gaussian · `26:00` Gated activation units; residual and skip connections · `33:00` Global and local conditioning: speaker identity vs linguistic features · `39:00` The inference problem — sequential generation at 16kHz — and Parallel WaveNet's distillation fix
▸ **Nail these:** the receptive-field arithmetic must go on screen, since it's the entire architectural argument; the categorical output over quantized amplitudes is the surprising choice and they justify it empirically; training is fully parallel and inference is fully sequential, which is the same asymmetry that defines autoregressive LLMs; this halved the gap to natural speech in MOS and immediately changed production TTS.

**D257 · Pixel Recurrent Neural Networks and PixelCNN** — van den Oord, Kalchbrenner & Kavukcuoglu, *ICML* 2016 (arXiv:1601.06759) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` An image as one very long sequence · `5:00` Factorizing p(x) by raster order; the chain rule over pixels and channels · `13:00` Masked convolutions, type A and B — draw both masks, this is where implementations break · `22:00` The blind spot in naive masked convs, and the Gated PixelCNN fix with vertical/horizontal stacks · `30:00` Row LSTM vs Diagonal BiLSTM; the tractable-likelihood advantage over GANs · `35:00` PixelCNN++: discretized logistic mixture, downsampling, and why it's the version people use
▸ **Nail these:** exact likelihood in bits/dim is the reason this family survived alongside GANs — it gives you a real number to compare; the blind-spot diagram is the highest-value visual and almost no explanation includes it; sequential sampling is O(number of pixels), which is why this became a *prior over latents* ⟵ D243 rather than a pixel model; conditional PixelCNN with class embeddings is the ancestor of conditional generation generally.

**D258 · Normalizing Flows: NICE, RealNVP, and Glow** — Dinh et al. 2015/2017 (arXiv:1605.08803); Kingma & Dhariwal *NeurIPS* 2018 (arXiv:1807.03039) · 50 min · L4
`0:00` Exact likelihood *and* fast sampling — the third option · `7:00` The change-of-variables formula and the log-determinant term, derived · `17:00` Affine coupling layers: split the input, transform half conditioned on the other half, and get a triangular Jacobian whose determinant is a sum of logs · `29:00` Why the transform must be invertible, and why coupling makes inversion trivial · `36:00` Multi-scale architecture, squeezing, and factoring out latents · `42:00` Glow's invertible 1×1 convolution as a learned generalized permutation, with the LU decomposition trick · `47:00` The cost: dimension-preserving latents mean enormous models for modest likelihood
▸ **Nail these:** the log-det Jacobian is the entire technical difficulty of flows, and coupling layers exist purely to make it cheap — say that once and everything else follows; checkerboard and channel-wise masking alternation; Glow's 1×1 conv replaces fixed channel reversal and its determinant is just d·log|det W|; flows lost to diffusion on quality per parameter, but the change-of-variables machinery returns in continuous normalizing flows and flow matching ⟶ D270.

**D259 · Deep Unsupervised Learning using Nonequilibrium Thermodynamics** — Sohl-Dickstein, Weiss, Maheswaranathan & Ganguli, *ICML* 2015 (arXiv:1503.03585) · 45 min · L4
`0:00` The diffusion idea, five years before anyone noticed · `7:00` Destroy structure with a slow forward diffusion, then learn to reverse it · `17:00` Why the reverse of a small Gaussian step is itself approximately Gaussian — the key physical fact · `26:00` The variational bound on log-likelihood, term by term · `35:00` Toy results: swiss roll, binary sequences, small images · `40:00` Why this sat unused until DDPM ⟶ D260
▸ **Nail these:** the "reverse of a sufficiently small diffusion step is in the same functional family" result is what makes the whole approach possible, and it comes straight from statistical physics — spend time here; the forward process has no learned parameters at all, which is unusual and important; the bound is exactly a hierarchical VAE's ELBO with a fixed encoder ⟵ D241; the honest history lesson is that the idea was right and the engineering wasn't there yet.

**D260 · Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPM)** — Ho, Jain & Abbeel, *NeurIPS* 2020 (arXiv:2006.11239) · 60 min · L3 · **Second keystone episode**
`0:00` The paper that made diffusion beat GANs · `7:00` The forward process q(x_t|x_{t−1}) and the closed form for q(x_t|x_0) with ᾱ_t — derive it, it's the workhorse identity · `20:00` The reverse process, and the tractable posterior q(x_{t−1}|x_t, x_0) · `30:00` Deriving the ELBO, then the reparameterization that turns it into ε-prediction · `42:00` The simplified loss: plain MSE between true and predicted noise — and why dropping the weighting terms *helps* · `50:00` The U-Net backbone with time embeddings ⟵ D92; the noise schedule; T=1000 · `56:00` Sampling cost as the one obvious weakness
▸ **Nail these:** the ᾱ closed form is what lets you train on a random timestep without simulating the chain, and it is the reason training is cheap — put it on screen and use it twice; predicting ε rather than x₀ or the posterior mean is an empirical choice that reweights the loss toward high-noise steps; L_simple is not the ELBO, so reported likelihoods and sample quality diverge — be precise; connect explicitly back to denoising autoencoders ⟵ D53 and score matching ⟶ D262.

**D261 · Denoising Diffusion Implicit Models + Improved DDPM** — Song, Meng & Ermon *ICLR* 2021 (arXiv:2010.02502); Nichol & Dhariwal *ICML* 2021 (arXiv:2102.09672) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` 1000 steps down to 20, with the same trained network · `6:00` The non-Markovian forward family that shares DDPM's marginals — this is the trick · `16:00` The deterministic sampler (σ=0) as an ODE solver, and the resulting latent-space consistency · `25:00` Interpolation and near-inversion in DDIM's latent space, which DDPM cannot do · `31:00` Improved DDPM: the cosine schedule, learned variance interpolation, and the importance-sampled ELBO
▸ **Nail these:** DDIM requires no retraining — the same ε-network is reused with a different sampler, which is why it was adopted instantly; determinism gives you an invertible map between noise and image, enabling editing; the cosine schedule exists because the linear schedule destroys information too fast at low resolution — show their SNR plot; strided timestep subsequences and the quality/steps curve.

**D262 · Score-Based Generative Modeling through Stochastic Differential Equations** — Song, Sohl-Dickstein, Kingma, Kumar, Ermon & Poole, *ICLR* 2021 (arXiv:2011.13456) · 55 min · L4
`0:00` One framework that contains DDPM, NCSN, and DDIM as special cases · `8:00` Score functions ∇_x log p(x), and Langevin dynamics as sampling from a score ⟵ D55 · `20:00` Denoising score matching, and why noise levels are needed to cover low-density regions · `30:00` The forward SDE; VE and VP as the two schedule families; DDPM is discretized VP · `40:00` The reverse-time SDE (Anderson 1982) and the probability-flow ODE with the same marginals · `48:00` Predictor–corrector sampling; exact likelihood via the ODE; controllable generation by adding a conditional score
