▸ **Nail these:** the score is the object being learned, and ε-prediction is a rescaled score estimate — writing that equivalence explicitly unifies the whole month; the probability-flow ODE is why deterministic samplers and likelihood evaluation both exist; conditional generation by summing scores ∇log p(x|y) = ∇log p(x) + ∇log p(y|x) is the equation that makes Day 263 obvious; this is the most mathematically demanding episode of Month 9 — warn viewers and give them Day 261 as the fallback.

**D263 · Classifier Guidance and Classifier-Free Guidance** — Dhariwal & Nichol *NeurIPS* 2021 (arXiv:2105.05233); Ho & Salimans 2022 (arXiv:2207.12598) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` The knob that made text-to-image usable · `6:00` Classifier guidance: shift the sampling mean by s·Σ∇log p(y|x_t), using a classifier trained on *noisy* images ⟵ D262 · `16:00` Diffusion Models Beat GANs: the architecture ablations (attention resolutions, BigGAN up/down blocks, adaptive group norm) plus guidance · `26:00` Classifier-free guidance: jointly train conditional and unconditional by dropping the condition ~10% of the time · `34:00` The extrapolation formula ε̃ = (1+w)ε(x,c) − w·ε(x,∅), and what w does to diversity · `40:00` Guidance scale as a fidelity/diversity dial, and the oversaturation failure at high w
▸ **Nail these:** classifier guidance needs a separate noise-aware classifier, which is a deployment burden CFG removes entirely — that practical difference is why CFG won; the extrapolation is *away* from the unconditional prediction, which is why it sharpens and why it eventually breaks; guidance trades FID for Inception Score in a measurable way; typical w of 7.5 in Stable Diffusion and 1.5–3 for photorealism.

**D264 · High-Resolution Image Synthesis with Latent Diffusion Models (Stable Diffusion)** — Rombach, Blattmann, Lorenz, Esser & Ommer, *CVPR* 2022 (arXiv:2112.10752) · 50 min · L3
`0:00` Diffusion that runs on a consumer GPU · `7:00` The two-stage split: a perceptual autoencoder for the "perceptual compression" phase, diffusion for the "semantic" phase ⟵ D244, D253 · `18:00` The rate–distortion argument for where to put the boundary — their bits/dim figure · `26:00` Downsampling factor ablation: f=4 and f=8 win; f=16 loses detail; f=1 is just pixel diffusion · `33:00` KL-reg vs VQ-reg latent spaces, and the tiny KL weight that keeps the latent near-Gaussian · `39:00` Cross-attention conditioning: text, layout, or class injected into the U-Net ⟵ D129 · `46:00` The public release and what changed downstream
▸ **Nail these:** the compute saving is roughly f² per spatial dimension, so f=8 means ~64× fewer pixels to denoise — do that arithmetic on screen; cross-attention is the general conditioning mechanism and it is why one architecture serves text, layout, and images; the autoencoder is frozen during diffusion training, which decouples the two stages; the latent is *not* semantically meaningful, and viewers routinely assume otherwise.

**D265 · Zero-Shot Text-to-Image Generation (DALL·E)** — Ramesh et al., *ICML* 2021 (arXiv:2102.12092) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Text-to-image as plain autoregressive modeling · `5:00` Stage 1: a discrete VAE compressing 256×256 to a 32×32 grid of 8192-way tokens ⟵ D243 · `14:00` The Gumbel-softmax relaxation with an annealed temperature, and why they used it instead of straight-through · `22:00` Stage 2: a 12B decoder-only transformer over 256 text tokens + 1024 image tokens · `30:00` Reranking with CLIP ⟵ D271, and how much of the demo quality that accounted for · `35:00` Compositionality successes and failures; the mixed-precision and sharding engineering
▸ **Nail these:** there is no diffusion here at all — this is a language model whose vocabulary happens to include image patches, and that framing is the point; the CLIP reranking over 512 samples does substantial work and should be stated plainly when showing cherry-picked outputs; the dVAE tokenizer is what BEiT ⟵ D119 borrowed; per-resample compositional errors (attribute binding) foreshadow a persistent weakness.

**D266 · Hierarchical Text-Conditional Image Generation (unCLIP/DALL·E 2) + Imagen** — Ramesh et al. 2022 (arXiv:2204.06125); Saharia et al. *NeurIPS* 2022 (arXiv:2205.11487) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Two contemporaneous systems that disagree about what the text encoder should be · `6:00` unCLIP: a prior mapping text embedding → CLIP image embedding, then a decoder diffusing on that embedding · `16:00` Diffusion prior vs autoregressive prior; the interpolation and variation demos the embedding space enables · `24:00` Imagen: a *frozen T5-XXL* text encoder ⟵ D142 and their finding that scaling the text encoder beats scaling the U-Net · `32:00` Dynamic thresholding for high guidance weights; cascaded super-resolution at 64 → 256 → 1024 · `39:00` DrawBench, and the limits of human-preference evaluation
▸ **Nail these:** "a language-only text encoder beats a vision-language one" is a genuinely surprising result and it's Imagen's central claim — give it real time; unCLIP's decoder is invariant to non-semantic detail, which is why its variations preserve meaning and change style; dynamic thresholding fixes the saturation artifact from Day 263's high-w regime; cascades let each stage specialize, which is the same idea as progressive growing ⟵ D250.

**D267 · Adding Conditional Control to Text-to-Image Diffusion Models (ControlNet)** — Zhang, Rao & Agrawala, *ICCV* 2023 (arXiv:2302.05543) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Precise spatial control without retraining the base model · `5:00` Clone the encoder blocks, freeze the original, and connect with zero-initialized 1×1 convolutions · `14:00` Why zero convolutions matter: the first training step is exactly the unmodified model, so no noise is injected into a good checkpoint ⟵ D204's B=0 · `22:00` Conditions: Canny edges, depth, pose, segmentation, scribbles · `28:00` The "sudden convergence" phenomenon they report · `32:00` Composing multiple ControlNets; T2I-Adapter as the lighter alternative
▸ **Nail these:** zero-init is the same trick as LoRA's B=0 and adapter near-identity init — point at all three together, it's one of the best cross-month connections available; the frozen base preserves the billion-image prior while the trainable copy learns the condition; 50k images is enough for a new condition, which is the practical headline; this is the paper that turned diffusion models into controllable tools rather than slot machines.

**D268 · Consistency Models** — Song, Dhariwal, Chen & Sutskever, *ICML* 2023 (arXiv:2303.01469) · 40 min · L4
`0:00` One-step generation from a diffusion-quality model · `6:00` The self-consistency property: every point on a probability-flow ODE trajectory maps to the same origin ⟵ D262 · `16:00` The parameterization with skip and output scalings that enforces the boundary condition f(x, ε) = x · `24:00` Two training modes: consistency distillation from a teacher, and consistency training from scratch · `32:00` Multi-step sampling as a quality dial; where 1-step still loses to 50-step diffusion · `36:00` Latent Consistency Models and LCM-LoRA as the practical descendants
▸ **Nail these:** the ODE trajectory framing from Day 262 is a hard prerequisite — do not attempt this episode without it; the boundary condition must be architecturally enforced, not learned, and that's the design subtlety; distillation and from-scratch training are genuinely different regimes with different quality ceilings; this is the paper that made real-time diffusion plausible.

**D269 · Flow Matching and Rectified Flow** — Lipman et al. *ICLR* 2023 (arXiv:2210.02747); Liu, Gong & Liu *ICLR* 2023 (arXiv:2209.03003) · 45 min · L4
`0:00` The formulation most 2025–2026 image and video models actually use · `7:00` Continuous normalizing flows and why maximum-likelihood training of them was too expensive ⟵ D258 · `16:00` Conditional flow matching: regress the vector field against a *known* conditional path, and the theorem that this gives the right marginal field · `28:00` Optimal-transport paths: straight lines between noise and data, and why straight paths need fewer solver steps · `36:00` Rectified flow and the reflow procedure for progressively straightening trajectories · `41:00` Diffusion as a special case with a curved path
▸ **Nail these:** the central theorem — that regressing the *conditional* vector field yields the correct *marginal* field — is the whole paper and it fits on one slide; straightness is the thing that buys sampling speed, and that reframes the entire fast-sampling literature; the linear interpolation path x_t = (1−t)x₀ + t·x₁ is almost embarrassingly simple; SD3 and Flux use this, so it's the current production answer.

**D270 · Scalable Diffusion Models with Transformers (DiT)** — Peebles & Xie, *ICCV* 2023 (arXiv:2212.09748) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Replacing the U-Net, and getting scaling laws for diffusion · `6:00` Patchify the latent, then a plain ViT ⟵ D115 operating in latent space ⟵ D264 · `15:00` Four conditioning designs compared: in-context, cross-attention, adaLN, and adaLN-Zero · `24:00` adaLN-Zero wins — and it is the zero-init idea again ⟵ D267 · `30:00` Gflops vs FID: the clean monotone relationship that makes this a scaling paper · `35:00` Why DiT is the architecture behind modern video generation ⟶ D298
▸ **Nail these:** the finding that FID tracks model Gflops almost independently of parameter count is the paper's real contribution — diffusion inherits transformer scaling behaviour; the U-Net's inductive bias turns out to be unnecessary given enough compute, which is exactly the ViT-vs-CNN story replayed ⟵ D115; patch size 2 dominates and it is the most expensive knob; adaLN-Zero's ablation is a tidy four-way comparison worth reading in full.

---

# MONTH 10 — Multimodal, Speech, Retrieval, Graphs, and Science
### Days 271–300 · Level 2–3 · Everything that isn't text-only

Four arcs: vision-language models, speech and audio, retrieval (the other way to give a model knowledge), and graph/scientific ML. This is the month that shows viewers the field is much larger than chatbots.

---

**D271 · Learning Transferable Visual Models From Natural Language Supervision (CLIP)** — Radford et al., *ICML* 2021 (arXiv:2103.00020) · 55 min · L2
`0:00` Zero-shot ImageNet at ResNet-50 accuracy, with no ImageNet training images · `7:00` 400M image–text pairs from the web; why natural language is a broader supervision signal than a fixed label set · `16:00` The contrastive objective: N×N similarity matrix, symmetric cross-entropy, learned temperature — write the loss · `27:00` Why they abandoned the predictive (captioning) objective — the 4× efficiency figure · `34:00` Zero-shot classification as prompt engineering: "a photo of a {label}", and the 80-prompt ensemble · `42:00` Robustness to distribution shift: the ImageNet-A/R/Sketch results, which are the deepest finding · `48:00` Failures: counting, spatial relations, fine-grained species, OCR-driven shortcuts
▸ **Nail these:** the effective-robustness plot (CLIP sits on a different line from every supervised model) is the most important figure and it's about the *training distribution*, not the architecture; the temperature is learned and clipped, a small detail with real stability impact; prompt templates change accuracy by several points, which is a measurement warning; CLIP's embedding space is the substrate for Days 265–266 and for most retrieval-augmented vision.

**D272 · ALIGN and SigLIP** — Jia et al. *ICML* 2021 (arXiv:2102.05918); Zhai, Mustafa, Kolesnikov & Beyer *ICCV* 2023 (arXiv:2303.15343) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Two follow-ups that change one thing each · `5:00` ALIGN: 1.8B noisy alt-text pairs with almost no filtering — scale substituting for curation ⟵ D271 · `14:00` The finding that dataset scale compensates for label noise, and where that stops being true · `21:00` SigLIP: replace softmax-over-batch with a pairwise sigmoid loss · `29:00` Why that decouples the loss from batch size and removes the all-gather — the systems argument · `33:00` Small-batch training reaching the same quality; the learned bias term
▸ **Nail these:** CLIP's softmax normalizes over the batch, so the loss definition depends on batch size and requires expensive cross-device communication — SigLIP's sigmoid removes both problems, and that's the whole idea; ALIGN's message is uncomfortable but real: noisy web data at scale beat clean curated data; SigLIP trains well at batch 4k where CLIP wanted 32k.

**D273 · Flamingo: a Visual Language Model for Few-Shot Learning** — Alayrac et al., *NeurIPS* 2022 (arXiv:2204.14198) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Few-shot learning with images interleaved in the prompt ⟵ D145 · `7:00` Freeze a pretrained LM, freeze a pretrained vision encoder, and learn only the bridge · `16:00` The Perceiver Resampler: variable numbers of visual features → a fixed 64 tokens · `25:00` Gated cross-attention layers inserted between frozen LM blocks, with tanh gating initialized at zero · `33:00` Interleaved image-text training data (M3W) and the per-image attention masking scheme · `40:00` Few-shot results beating fine-tuned SOTA on several benchmarks
▸ **Nail these:** zero-initialized tanh gating means the model starts as the unmodified language model — the third appearance of this idea after LoRA ⟵ D204 and ControlNet ⟵ D267, and by now viewers should recognize it instantly; each text token attends to only the most recent preceding image, a masking choice that's essential and rarely mentioned; freezing both towers is what makes this affordable and what preserves LM ability.

**D274 · BLIP-2: Bootstrapping Language-Image Pre-training with Frozen Encoders** — Li, Li, Savarese & Hoi, *ICML* 2023 (arXiv:2301.12597) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Connecting any vision encoder to any LLM with 188M trainable parameters · `5:00` The Q-Former: 32 learned queries that extract language-relevant visual features · `14:00` Stage 1 — representation learning with three objectives (contrastive, matching, grounded captioning) and different attention masks for each · `24:00` Stage 2 — generative pretraining against a frozen LLM through a single linear projection · `30:00` Why two stages: without stage 1 the Q-Former outputs features the LLM can't use
▸ **Nail these:** the "modality gap" framing — the Q-Former's job is to output something already in the LLM's input distribution, and the two-stage design exists because that's hard to learn end-to-end; the three shared-parameter objectives with different masks is a clean trick worth drawing; this is the template that made VLMs cheap enough for academic labs.

**D275 · Visual Instruction Tuning (LLaVA)** — Liu, Li, Wu & Lee, *NeurIPS* 2023 (arXiv:2304.08485) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` A capable VLM built in a day on eight GPUs · `5:00` The data trick: feed captions and bounding boxes to a text-only GPT-4 and have it generate conversations, descriptions, and reasoning about images it never sees ⟵ D199 · `15:00` 158k samples across three response types, and the ablation over the mix · `22:00` The architecture: CLIP ViT-L/14 + one linear projection + Vicuna — deliberately minimal ⟵ D274 · `28:00` Two-stage training: align the projection, then instruction-tune end-to-end · `32:00` LLaVA-1.5's changes (MLP projector, higher resolution, academic VQA data) and what each was worth
▸ **Nail these:** the data-generation method is the contribution and the architecture is deliberately boring — say that clearly, it's an important lesson about where value sits in 2023-era work; symbolic scene representations as a proxy for the image is a reusable idea; the reasoning-type responses are what produce the conversational ability, per their ablation; contrast the single linear layer with Flamingo's and BLIP-2's elaborate bridges — simpler won at this scale.

**D276 · GPT-4 Technical Report (and System Card)** — OpenAI, 2023 (arXiv:2303.08774) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` A capabilities report with no architecture in it · `7:00` What is disclosed: exam results, benchmark scores, multimodal examples, and the deliberate withholding of size, data, and method · `18:00` Predictable scaling: loss and HumanEval pass rate forecast from runs 1,000–10,000× smaller — the single most technically substantive claim ⟵ D146, D170 · `28:00` The inverse-scaling-prize hindsight-neglect reversal · `34:00` The System Card: red-teaming, the ARC autonomous-replication evaluation, and the pre/post-mitigation comparison · `40:00` Contamination analysis, and how to read exam-score claims skeptically
▸ **Nail these:** predicting a downstream capability metric before training is a genuine methodological advance and should be separated from the marketing around the release; be neutral and factual about the non-disclosure — state the stated reasons (competition, safety) and the cost to reproducibility, without editorializing; the system card's dangerous-capability evaluations are the template that later became frontier safety frameworks ⟶ D370.

**D277 · Connectionist Temporal Classification** — Graves, Fernández, Gomez & Schmidhuber, *ICML* 2006 · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Training a sequence model when you don't know the alignment · `7:00` The blank symbol, and the many-to-one collapse from frame labels to output labels · `17:00` The forward–backward algorithm over the extended label sequence, traced on a small lattice ⟵ D25 · `30:00` The CTC loss as the log-sum over all valid alignments — this is dynamic programming, do it on screen · `38:00` Greedy vs beam-search decoding; combining with an external language model · `42:00` The conditional-independence assumption, and what it costs
▸ **Nail these:** CTC removes the need for frame-level alignment, which is what killed the HMM-GMM pipeline for speech — that's the historical significance; the blank symbol is what allows repeated characters and variable timing; outputs are conditionally independent given the input, so CTC alone is a poor language model and always wants an external LM; RNN-T fixes exactly that and is worth naming.

**D278 · Listen, Attend and Spell + Deep Speech 2** — Chan, Jaitly, Le & Vinyals *ICASSP* 2016 (arXiv:1508.01211); Amodei et al. *ICML* 2016 (arXiv:1512.02595) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Two paths to end-to-end speech recognition · `5:00` LAS: attention-based seq2seq for speech ⟵ D126, with the pyramidal encoder that downsamples time by 8× · `15:00` Why downsampling is mandatory — 100 frames per second against 15 characters per second · `22:00` Deep Speech 2: CTC ⟵ D277 at scale, in English and Mandarin with the same architecture · `29:00` Batch dispatch, GPU kernels, and the systems half of the paper · `33:00` Attention vs CTC: streaming, monotonicity, and why RNN-T won production
▸ **Nail these:** attention is not monotonic and speech is, so LAS can and does produce catastrophic repetition and skipping — this is the failure mode that motivated monotonic-attention research; the pyramidal encoder is a cheap and important trick; Deep Speech 2's finding that the same architecture works across two very different languages is a genuine result about learned features vs linguistic engineering.

**D279 · wav2vec 2.0: Self-Supervised Learning of Speech Representations** — Baevski, Zhou, Mohamed & Auli, *NeurIPS* 2020 (arXiv:2006.11477) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` 10 minutes of labeled speech, and a usable recognizer · `7:00` A convolutional feature encoder producing 20ms latent frames · `15:00` Masked span prediction with a contrastive loss over quantized targets ⟵ D137, D243 · `26:00` Product quantization with Gumbel-softmax, and the diversity loss that prevents codebook collapse · `34:00` Fine-tuning with CTC ⟵ D277 on tiny labeled sets · `39:00` The labeled-data scaling curve — this is the figure that changed low-resource speech
▸ **Nail these:** the targets are quantized in a *learned* discrete space while the context representation stays continuous, and that asymmetry is the design; the diversity loss is not optional and the ablation shows why; masking spans rather than single frames matters because speech is locally redundant; XLSR's cross-lingual extension is the practical payoff for the world's low-resource languages.

**D280 · HuBERT: Self-Supervised Speech Learning by Masked Prediction of Hidden Units** — Hsu et al., *IEEE/ACM TASLP* 2021 (arXiv:2106.07447) · 30 min · L3
`0:00` Replacing the contrastive loss with plain classification · `4:00` The bootstrap: k-means on MFCCs gives noisy pseudo-labels, good enough to start ⟵ D20 · `13:00` Masked prediction over those cluster ids, exactly like BERT ⟵ D137 · `20:00` Iteration: re-cluster on the model's own learned features, then retrain — quality improves each round · `26:00` Why prediction-only is simpler and more stable than wav2vec 2.0's contrastive setup
▸ **Nail these:** the insight is that the *consistency* of the clustering matters more than its accuracy, since the model only needs a stable target to organize around — this is a lovely, non-obvious argument; two iterations are usually enough; predicting only masked regions forces a combined acoustic-and-language model; HuBERT units became the standard discrete speech representation for generative audio ⟶ D284.

**D281 · Robust Speech Recognition via Large-Scale Weak Supervision (Whisper)** — Radford, Kim, Xu, Brockman, McLeavey & Sutskever, *ICML* 2023 (arXiv:2212.04356) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Zero-shot speech recognition that beats fine-tuned systems on real audio · `6:00` 680,000 hours of weakly-supervised web audio, 117k of it non-English · `15:00` The multitask format: special tokens for language id, transcribe-vs-translate, timestamps, and no-speech ⟵ D142 · `25:00` Automatic filtering of machine-generated transcripts — the detection heuristics they used · `31:00` The robustness argument: matching human error rates on out-of-distribution sets while LibriSpeech-tuned models fall apart · `36:00` Hallucinated transcripts on silence, and the long-form decoding heuristics
▸ **Nail these:** the thesis is that dataset breadth beats dataset cleanliness for robustness — the same lesson as ALIGN ⟵ D272 and CLIP ⟵ D271, and calling out the pattern is the point of putting them in the same month; supervised-but-weak is a third category between self-supervised and clean-supervised; the hallucination failure on silence is real, well-documented, and important to mention given medical transcription use.

**D282 · Natural TTS Synthesis by Conditioning WaveNet on Mel Spectrogram Predictions (Tacotron 2)** — Shen et al., *ICASSP* 2018 (arXiv:1712.05884) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Text to speech, in two clean stages · `4:00` Stage 1: an attention-based seq2seq producing mel spectrograms ⟵ D126 · `13:00` Location-sensitive attention, and why plain content attention fails on long utterances · `20:00` Stage 2: a modified WaveNet vocoder conditioned on mel frames ⟵ D256 · `25:00` The MOS result, and what "close to human" means and doesn't · `28:00` FastSpeech's duration predictor as the non-autoregressive successor
▸ **Nail these:** the mel spectrogram is a deliberately low-dimensional intermediate that decouples "what to say" from "how it sounds" — that interface is the design; location-sensitive attention adds cumulative attention weights as a feature, which enforces rough monotonicity and prevents the word-skipping failure ⟵ D278; the stop-token prediction problem; MOS is a subjective 1–5 scale and comparisons across papers are not reliable.

**D283 · Neural Audio Codecs and VALL-E** — Zeghidour et al. (SoundStream) 2021; Défossez et al. (EnCodec) 2022 (arXiv:2210.13438); Wang et al. (VALL-E) 2023 (arXiv:2301.02111) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Turning audio into tokens a language model can read · `6:00` Residual vector quantization: a cascade of codebooks each encoding the previous stage's error ⟵ D243 · `16:00` The encoder–quantizer–decoder with a discriminator; bitrate scalability via quantizer dropout · `24:00` VALL-E: TTS reframed as conditional codec-token language modeling · `31:00` Autoregressive for the first quantizer, non-autoregressive for the rest — the two-model split and why · `36:00` 3-second voice cloning, and the misuse concerns the authors themselves raise
▸ **Nail these:** RVQ is the key primitive — draw the cascade and show how each stage refines; treating audio as tokens is what let the whole LLM toolkit transfer to speech, and this is the conceptual headline; the AR/NAR split exists because the first codebook carries content and the rest carry detail; be direct and non-instructional about voice-cloning misuse, and mention watermarking and consent as the live policy questions.

**D284 · AudioLM and MusicLM** — Borsos et al. 2022 (arXiv:2209.03143); Agostinelli et al. 2023 (arXiv:2301.11325) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Long-term coherent audio generation without any transcript · `5:00` Two token types: semantic tokens from HuBERT-like features ⟵ D280, acoustic tokens from a codec ⟵ D283 · `15:00` The three-stage hierarchy — semantic modeling, coarse acoustic, fine acoustic · `24:00` Why semantic tokens first: they carry structure over tens of seconds that acoustic tokens cannot · `29:00` MusicLM: text conditioning via MuLan's joint text-music embedding, trained without paired text · `33:00` MusicCaps, memorization studies, and the copyright questions
▸ **Nail these:** the semantic/acoustic token split is the whole architecture and it's a clean instance of "separate what from how"; the model generates continuations that stay in key and in style for 30+ seconds, which no prior audio model did; MuLan lets you condition on text without any text-labeled music, which is a neat data trick; the memorization analysis is worth covering factually given how contested music generation is.

**D285 · The Probabilistic Relevance Framework: BM25 and Beyond** — Robertson & Zaragoza, *FnTIR* 2009 · 40 min · L1
`0:00` The baseline that keeps beating neural retrievers on some benchmarks in 2026 · `6:00` TF-IDF, and why raw term frequency is the wrong signal · `15:00` BM25: saturating term frequency with k₁, and length normalization with b — plot the saturation curve · `26:00` The probabilistic derivation behind the formula · `33:00` Inverted indexes; why exact term matching is unbeatable for rare terms and identifiers · `37:00` BEIR results, and hybrid sparse+dense retrieval as current practice
▸ **Nail these:** plot BM25's TF saturation next to linear TF — the picture explains the formula better than the derivation does; k₁≈1.2, b≈0.75 as defaults; lexical retrieval fails on vocabulary mismatch and dense retrieval fails on rare exact terms, so hybrid is not a hedge, it's the correct answer; every RAG system you build should have BM25 as its baseline, and most published RAG papers should have had one.

**D286 · Dense Passage Retrieval for Open-Domain Question Answering** — Karpukhin et al., *EMNLP* 2020 (arXiv:2004.04906) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Learned embeddings finally beating BM25 ⟵ D285 · `5:00` The dual-encoder: separate question and passage towers, dot-product scoring, precomputed passage index · `14:00` In-batch negatives — the trick that makes training cheap, with the arithmetic · `22:00` Hard negatives from BM25, and the ablation showing one hard negative per question does most of the work · `29:00` Top-20 accuracy gains, and the end-task QA improvement
▸ **Nail these:** the dual encoder is what makes retrieval tractable — passages are embedded once, offline, and the query tower runs at request time; in-batch negatives give you B−1 free negatives per example, so batch size is effectively a hyperparameter of the loss ⟵ D272; hard negatives matter more than model size here; cross-encoders are more accurate and unusable at corpus scale, which is exactly what ColBERT ⟶ D289 addresses.

**D287 · Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Intensive NLP Tasks** — Lewis et al., *NeurIPS* 2020 (arXiv:2005.11401) · 45 min · L2
`0:00` Giving a model a library instead of a bigger memory · `6:00` Parametric vs non-parametric memory, stated as a design axis · `14:00` DPR retriever ⟵ D286 + BART generator ⟵ D143, trained jointly with the document as a latent variable · `24:00` RAG-Sequence vs RAG-Token: marginalizing over documents once, or per generated token — derive both · `33:00` Backpropagating into the query encoder while the document index stays frozen · `38:00` Updating knowledge by swapping the index, with no retraining — the operational headline · `42:00` Reduced hallucination and improved factual specificity, with the caveats
▸ **Nail these:** the document is a latent variable marginalized in the output distribution, which is what makes this a probabilistic model rather than string concatenation — most practitioners have never seen this and it's the intellectual content; RAG-Token can use different sources for different parts of one answer; re-indexing the whole corpus is expensive, which is why they freeze the document encoder; every "RAG" system in production today is a simplification of this.

**D288 · REALM: Retrieval-Augmented Language Model Pre-Training** — Guu, Lee, Tung, Pasupat & Chang, *ICML* 2020 (arXiv:2002.08909) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Retrieval trained *during pretraining*, not bolted on after ⟵ D287 · `5:00` Masked language modeling where the retriever is rewarded for finding documents that help fill the mask · `14:00` The asynchronous index refresh: re-embed the corpus every few hundred steps and rebuild the MIPS index · `23:00` The cold-start problem, and the Inverse Cloze Task warm-up that solves it · `29:00` Salient span masking — masking named entities specifically — and how much it was worth · `33:00` Why REALM is more principled than RAG and much harder to run
▸ **Nail these:** the retriever's gradient signal is entirely indirect — it improves because good documents lower the MLM loss, which is an elegant and fully unsupervised objective; the stale-index approximation is a real engineering compromise and they analyze it; salient span masking gave a very large gain, which says something uncomfortable about how much benchmark performance is entity recall.

**D289 · ColBERT: Efficient and Effective Passage Search via Late Interaction** — Khattab & Zaharia, *SIGIR* 2020 (arXiv:2004.12832) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` The middle ground between dual encoders and cross encoders ⟵ D286 · `5:00` Late interaction: keep one embedding per *token*, not per passage · `13:00` MaxSim — for each query token, take the max similarity over passage tokens, then sum — write it out · `21:00` Why this can be precomputed and indexed while still modeling term-level interaction · `27:00` Storage cost, and ColBERTv2's residual compression to ~30× smaller · `31:00` PLAID and the practical latency numbers
▸ **Nail these:** the accuracy/cost spectrum — dual encoder (cheap, coarse), late interaction (middle), cross encoder (expensive, precise) — is the framing that organizes all of modern retrieval, so draw it; MaxSim is soft term matching, which is why ColBERT handles rare terms better than DPR; the index is much larger than a dense index, and that tradeoff is the real deployment question.

**D290 · Efficient Similarity Search: HNSW and FAISS** — Malkov & Yashunin *TPAMI* 2018 (arXiv:1603.09320); Johnson, Douze & Jégou 2017 (arXiv:1702.08734) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` The data structure under every vector database · `6:00` Why exact nearest neighbour is hopeless in high dimensions ⟵ D7 · `13:00` Navigable small-world graphs; greedy routing; the skip-list-like hierarchy · `24:00` M and efConstruction/efSearch, and the recall/latency curve you actually tune · `31:00` Product quantization and IVF for billion-scale indexes on limited memory · `36:00` GPU batching, and the recall-vs-throughput tradeoff
▸ **Nail these:** approximate is not a compromise here — it's the only tractable option, and recall@k is the metric that replaces exactness; the hierarchy lets long-range hops happen at sparse upper layers and refinement at dense lower ones, which is the whole idea; PQ compresses vectors into subspace codebooks ⟵ D243; deletions in HNSW are genuinely awkward and this bites people in production.

**D291 · Improving Language Models by Retrieving from Trillions of Tokens (RETRO)** — Borgeaud et al., *ICML* 2022 (arXiv:2112.04426) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` A 7.5B model matching a 280B one, by reading from a 2-trillion-token database ⟵ D172 · `7:00` Chunked cross-attention: split the input into 64-token chunks and retrieve neighbours for each · `18:00` Frozen BERT embeddings for retrieval — no retriever training at all, which is what makes trillion-scale indexing possible ⟵ D288 · `26:00` The autoregressive-correctness constraint: chunk i may only attend to neighbours of chunk i−1 · `32:00` Retrofitting an existing model with retrieval for ~6% of original compute · `36:00` The leakage analysis, and why it complicates every benchmark number
▸ **Nail these:** the causality constraint on which neighbours a chunk may see is the technical crux and it's easy to get wrong; freezing the retriever is a deliberate scalability tradeoff and the opposite of REALM's choice — compare them directly; their filtered-evaluation section, where they remove train/test overlap and report the reduced gains, is a model of honest evaluation; the "is it retrieval or memorization?" question is the durable one.

**D292 · Self-RAG and Corrective RAG** — Asai, Wu, Wang, Sil & Hajishirzi *ICLR* 2024 (arXiv:2310.11511); Yan et al. 2024 (arXiv:2401.15884) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Retrieving *sometimes*, and checking what you retrieved · `5:00` Reflection tokens: Retrieve, IsRelevant, IsSupported, IsUseful — generated inline as part of the output ⟵ D194 · `16:00` Training them via distillation from a stronger critic model · `23:00` Inference-time control: adjust weights on the reflection tokens to trade fluency against citation precision · `28:00` Corrective RAG: a lightweight retrieval evaluator that triggers web search when the corpus fails · `32:00` What still isn't solved — retrieval decisions on multi-hop questions
▸ **Nail these:** always-retrieve hurts on questions the model already knows, and never-retrieve hurts on the rest — adaptive retrieval is the obvious fix and making it *learned and inspectable* is the contribution; self-assessment of support is a citation-verification mechanism, which is the practically valuable part; the reflection tokens make the pipeline's decisions auditable, which connects to Month 11's interpretability themes.

**D293 · DeepWalk and node2vec** — Perozzi, Al-Rfou & Skiena *KDD* 2014 (arXiv:1403.6652); Grover & Leskovec *KDD* 2016 (arXiv:1607.00653) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` word2vec, but the sentences are random walks ⟵ D121 · `5:00` Truncated random walks as "sentences"; skip-gram over node co-occurrence · `14:00` The power-law degree distribution matching word frequency distributions — why the analogy holds · `21:00` node2vec's biased walk with p and q: interpolating between BFS (structural roles) and DFS (community membership) · `29:00` Homophily vs structural equivalence, shown on the Les Misérables graph · `33:00` The transductive limitation: no embedding for a node you didn't train on
▸ **Nail these:** the p/q parameters give an explicit, tunable dial between two different notions of node similarity, and their visualization of both regimes on the same graph is the money figure; hierarchical softmax carries over directly from Day 122; the inability to embed new nodes is the exact limitation GraphSAGE ⟶ D295 fixes, so end on it.

**D294 · Semi-Supervised Classification with Graph Convolutional Networks** — Kipf & Welling, *ICLR* 2017 (arXiv:1609.02907) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` The paper that made graph neural networks mainstream · `7:00` Spectral graph convolution, the graph Laplacian, and Chebyshev polynomial approximation · `18:00` The first-order simplification: two approximations that collapse it to H' = σ(D̃^(−½)Ã D̃^(−½) H W) — derive both steps · `28:00` The renormalization trick, self-loops, and why symmetric normalization prevents exploding scale · `35:00` Two layers = two-hop receptive field; Cora/Citeseer with 20 labels per class · `40:00` Over-smoothing: why deep GCNs make every node identical, shown empirically
▸ **Nail these:** walk the derivation from spectral filtering down to the one-line propagation rule — it's the best example in the series of theory being simplified into something implementable; each layer averages a node with its neighbours, so K layers means a K-hop receptive field and also K rounds of smoothing; over-smoothing is why GCNs are shallow, which is a genuine structural difference from every other architecture in this course.

**D295 · Inductive Representation Learning on Large Graphs (GraphSAGE)** — Hamilton, Ying & Leskovec, *NIPS* 2017 (arXiv:1706.02216) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Embedding a node the model has never seen ⟵ D293, D294 · `4:00` Learning aggregator *functions* rather than per-node embedding vectors · `12:00` Four aggregators compared: mean, GCN-style, LSTM, max-pool — and the permutation-invariance issue with LSTM ⟵ D102 · `20:00` Neighbourhood sampling with a fixed fan-out, and the memory/variance tradeoff · `26:00` Unsupervised and supervised training; the Reddit and PPI benchmarks
▸ **Nail these:** inductive vs transductive is the axis of this episode and it's what makes GNNs deployable on graphs that change; fixed-size sampling bounds the exponential neighbourhood blowup, which is the only reason minibatch training on graphs is possible; the LSTM aggregator is not permutation invariant and they apply it to random orderings, which is an honest hack worth pointing out.

**D296 · Graph Attention Networks** — Veličković, Cucurull, Casanova, Romero, Liò & Bengio, *ICLR* 2018 (arXiv:1710.10903) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` Letting a node decide which neighbours matter · `4:00` The attention coefficient a(Wh_i, Wh_j), LeakyReLU, and softmax over the neighbourhood ⟵ D126 · `13:00` Masked attention: only compute over actual edges, which is what keeps it sparse · `19:00` Multi-head attention on graphs; concatenate in hidden layers, average at the output ⟵ D129 · `25:00` No dependence on global graph structure, hence inductive by construction
▸ **Nail these:** GAT is self-attention with the adjacency matrix as the attention mask — say it that way and everyone who watched Day 129 gets it immediately; unlike GCN, the aggregation weights are learned and data-dependent rather than fixed by degree; "How Attentive Are GATs?" (Brody et al. 2022) showed the original attention is static and GATv2 fixes it — a nice example of a widely-used method being corrected years later.

**D297 · Neural Message Passing for Quantum Chemistry** — Gilmer, Schoenholz, Riley, Vinyals & Dahl, *ICML* 2017 (arXiv:1704.01212) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` A common framework that contains every GNN in the last three episodes · `5:00` Message, update, readout — the three functions, with GCN and GAT written in this notation · `15:00` Edge features via the edge network; set2set as a permutation-invariant readout · `22:00` QM9: predicting 13 quantum-chemical properties, reaching DFT-level chemical accuracy on 11 · `29:00` Virtual edges and the master node for long-range interaction · `33:00` Where MPNNs are limited — the Weisfeiler–Lehman expressiveness ceiling
▸ **Nail these:** the unification is the contribution, and rewriting Days 294–296 in message-passing notation on one board is the highest-value five minutes; the WL bound (standard message passing cannot distinguish certain non-isomorphic graphs) is the key theoretical limit and GIN (Xu et al. 2019) is the reference; matching DFT at a fraction of the compute is what launched ML for molecular simulation.

**D298 · Video Generation Models as World Simulators (Sora) + Video Diffusion** — OpenAI 2024; Ho et al. 2022 (arXiv:2204.03458) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Minute-long coherent video, and the claim attached to it · `6:00` Spacetime patches: encode video into a latent, then flatten into patch tokens for a diffusion transformer ⟵ D264, D270 · `16:00` Variable duration, resolution, and aspect ratio in one model — training on native sizes rather than crops · `24:00` Re-captioning training videos with a captioner model, the same trick as DALL·E 3 · `30:00` The "emerging simulation capabilities" claims — 3D consistency, object permanence — and how much weight the evidence supports · `35:00` Physics failures, and what a technical report without ablations can and cannot establish
▸ **Nail these:** patch tokenization generalizing from images to spacetime is the architectural through-line of the last two months — make that explicit; training on native resolutions removes the crop bias that made earlier models bad at composition; be careful and evenhanded about "world simulator" — describe the demonstrated behaviours and the absent evaluations without adopting or dismissing the framing.

**D299 · Highly Accurate Protein Structure Prediction with AlphaFold** — Jumper et al., *Nature* 2021 · 60 min · L4
`0:00` A fifty-year-old grand challenge, closed in one CASP · `8:00` The inputs: multiple sequence alignments and templates, and why coevolution carries structural signal · `18:00` Evoformer: axial attention over the MSA, and the pair representation with triangle updates · `32:00` The triangle inequality as an architectural inductive bias — this is the most elegant idea in the paper · `40:00` The structure module: invariant point attention and direct 3D coordinate prediction with residue gas · `48:00` Recycling; the FAPE loss; self-distillation on unlabeled sequences · `54:00` pLDDT as a calibrated confidence, and the disorder-prediction side effect
▸ **Nail these:** triangle multiplicative updates encode a geometric consistency constraint directly into the network — distances must satisfy the triangle inequality, and building that in rather than learning it is the paper's signature move; IPA is SE(3)-equivariant, meaning predictions rotate with the input rather than being memorized in a fixed frame; pLDDT is genuinely well-calibrated and became a de facto disorder predictor, which nobody designed; be clear about limits — single chains, no ligands, static structures.

**D300 · Accurate Structure Prediction of Biomolecular Interactions with AlphaFold 3** — Abramson et al., *Nature* 2024 · 45 min · L4
`0:00` From proteins to everything: DNA, RNA, ligands, ions, modifications · `7:00` Replacing the structure module with a diffusion head that generates coordinates directly ⟵ D260 · `18:00` The simplified Pairformer: less MSA processing, more pair reasoning ⟵ D299 · `26:00` Hallucinated plausible structures in disordered regions, and the cross-distillation fix · `33:00` Results against docking baselines on protein–ligand complexes · `38:00` Access model, reproducibility concerns raised by the community, and what that means for science
▸ **Nail these:** a generative head produces a *distribution* over structures rather than a point estimate, which is conceptually right for flexible molecules but introduces the hallucination failure they had to patch; the reduced MSA reliance is the direction the field moved (see ESMFold's language-model-only approach); cover the access and reproducibility discussion factually and briefly — it's a real methodological issue in computational biology and viewers will ask.

---

# MONTH 11 — Interpretability, Theory, Robustness, and Privacy
### Days 301–330 · Level 3–4 · The papers that ask whether any of this is trustworthy

Months 1–10 were about making things work. This month is about whether we understand them, whether they generalize for the reasons we think, whether they can be attacked, and what they leak. It is the least fashionable month and the one that will age best.

---

**D301 · Deep Inside Convolutional Networks: Visualising Image Classification Models and Saliency Maps** — Simonyan, Vedaldi & Zisserman, *ICLR Workshop* 2014 (arXiv:1312.6034) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` The first attribution method, in three pages · `4:00` Class model visualization: optimize the *input* to maximize a class score, with L2 regularization · `13:00` Saliency as |∂score/∂pixel| — the first-order Taylor argument · `20:00` Using saliency to seed GraphCut segmentation, with zero segmentation labels · `25:00` The equivalence between gradient saliency and deconvnet ⟵ D62, spelled out in the paper
▸ **Nail these:** gradient saliency is a *local linear* approximation, so it tells you about an infinitesimal perturbation and not about the feature's actual importance — this limitation is the reason the next four episodes exist; the class-visualization images are recognizable but adversarial-looking, which is a hint about Day 321; regularization choice dominates the visual result, so these pictures are partly artifacts of the prior.

**D302 · Grad-CAM: Visual Explanations from Deep Networks via Gradient-based Localization** — Selvaraju, Cogswell, Das, Vedantam, Parikh & Batra, *ICCV* 2017 (arXiv:1610.02391) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` The heatmap you've seen in a hundred papers · `5:00` CAM's restriction: it required a global-average-pooling architecture ⟵ D63 · `12:00` Grad-CAM's generalization: gradient-derived channel weights α_k, then a ReLU'd weighted sum of feature maps · `21:00` Why the last conv layer — the semantics/resolution tradeoff · `27:00` Guided Grad-CAM: multiply by guided backprop for pixel-level detail · `31:00` Counterfactual explanations and the bias-detection case study (the doctor/nurse gender example)
▸ **Nail these:** the ReLU keeps only features with positive influence on the class, and dropping it changes the picture substantially; Grad-CAM is coarse because the last conv layer is coarse, and that's a fundamental limit not a bug; guided backprop was later shown to act partly as an edge detector regardless of the class (Adebayo et al. ⟶ D305) — say so, because Guided Grad-CAM images are still published uncritically.

**D303 · "Why Should I Trust You?" Explaining the Predictions of Any Classifier (LIME)** — Ribeiro, Singh & Guestrin, *KDD* 2016 (arXiv:1602.04938) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Explaining a black box you cannot differentiate · `5:00` Local surrogate models: perturb, predict, fit a sparse linear model weighted by proximity · `15:00` The interpretable representation — superpixels for images, binary word presence for text — and why it must differ from the model's input space · `23:00` SP-LIME: submodular pick for choosing a representative set of explanations · `28:00` The husky-vs-wolf snow-detector experiment · `32:00` Instability: rerun LIME twice and get different explanations
▸ **Nail these:** the fidelity/interpretability tradeoff is explicit in the objective, and that honesty is why LIME is worth teaching; explanations are local only, and users systematically over-generalize them; the kernel width is an unprincipled hyperparameter that changes conclusions — Alvarez-Melis & Jaakkola 2018 documented the instability; the snow experiment is the best "model learned the wrong thing" demo in ML and takes 90 seconds.

**D304 · A Unified Approach to Interpreting Model Predictions (SHAP)** — Lundberg & Lee, *NIPS* 2017 (arXiv:1705.07874) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Attribution with actual axioms · `6:00` Shapley values from cooperative game theory: the average marginal contribution over all orderings · `16:00` The four axioms — efficiency, symmetry, dummy, additivity — and the uniqueness theorem · `26:00` Additive feature attribution as the class containing LIME, DeepLIFT, and layer-wise relevance propagation · `33:00` KernelSHAP and TreeSHAP; the exponential cost and how TreeSHAP gets to polynomial · `37:00` The background-distribution choice, and how much it changes the answer
▸ **Nail these:** uniqueness under the axioms is what makes SHAP different from every heuristic in this block — state the axioms precisely; exact Shapley computation is exponential in features, so everything practical is an approximation with its own assumptions; feature independence is assumed by KernelSHAP and is usually false, which produces attribution to impossible feature combinations; TreeSHAP's polynomial algorithm is why SHAP dominates tabular ML ⟵ D13.

**D305 · Axiomatic Attribution + Sanity Checks for Saliency Maps** — Sundararajan, Taly & Yan *ICML* 2017 (arXiv:1703.01365); Adebayo et al. *NeurIPS* 2018 (arXiv:1810.03292) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` One method with proofs, and one paper that invalidated half the field · `5:00` Integrated Gradients: integrate the gradient along a path from a baseline to the input · `14:00` Sensitivity and implementation invariance; why plain gradients violate the first (saturation) · `22:00` The completeness property, and the baseline choice as an unavoidable modeling decision · `29:00` The sanity checks: randomize model weights, randomize labels — and watch several popular saliency methods produce nearly identical maps · `36:00` Which methods pass and which fail
▸ **Nail these:** the model-randomization test is devastating and simple — an explanation that doesn't change when you destroy the model is not explaining the model, it's an edge detector; Guided BackProp and Guided Grad-CAM largely fail it, gradients and GradCAM largely pass; the black-image baseline is not neutral because black pixels are meaningful, and the baseline choice silently determines the attribution; this episode is the single best argument for methodological skepticism in the whole month.

**D306 · Zoom In: An Introduction to Circuits** — Olah, Cammarata, Schubert, Goh, Petrov & Carter, *Distill* 2020 · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Interpretability as biology: features, circuits, and universality · `7:00` The three claims stated as falsifiable hypotheses · `16:00` Curve detectors: the evidence chain — dataset examples, feature visualization, synthetic tuning curves, and weight inspection · `28:00` High-low frequency detectors and the dog-head circuit, read directly off weights · `36:00` Polysemantic neurons as the obstacle, and the first mention of superposition ⟶ D309 · `41:00` Why Distill's format matters for this kind of work
▸ **Nail these:** the standard of evidence is the lesson — no single method is trusted, and a claim about a neuron requires four independent lines of evidence that agree; circuits are weights between features, so you can read an algorithm off the parameters, which is qualitatively different from attribution; universality (the same features recurring across architectures) is the boldest claim and the least settled.

**D307 · A Mathematical Framework for Transformer Circuits** — Elhage et al., *Transformer Circuits Thread* 2021 · 55 min · L4
`0:00` A transformer, rewritten so you can reason about it · `8:00` The residual stream as a communication channel that every layer reads from and writes to ⟵ D67 · `18:00` Attention heads decomposed into QK (where to attend) and OV (what to move) circuits, which act independently · `28:00` Rewriting a one-layer attention-only model as a sum over head paths — the "sum of paths" expansion · `38:00` Skip-trigrams and their bugs in one-layer models · `45:00` Two layers: composition (Q-, K-, and V-composition) and the birth of induction heads ⟶ D308 · `50:00` Why attention-only models are the right toy
▸ **Nail these:** the QK/OV factorization is the core conceptual tool and everything downstream depends on it — spend fifteen minutes there; the residual stream having no privileged basis is why individual neurons are a bad unit of analysis; virtual attention heads formed by composition are the mechanism that makes depth matter; this episode is hard, so state prerequisites (D129, D67) up front and consider splitting it.

**D308 · In-context Learning and Induction Heads** — Olsson et al., *Transformer Circuits Thread* 2022 · 45 min · L4
`0:00` A mechanism for in-context learning, found and tested ⟵ D145 · `7:00` Induction heads: find the previous occurrence of the current token, and copy what followed it — [A][B]…[A] → [B] · `16:00` The two-head circuit: a previous-token head composing with a match-and-copy head ⟵ D307 · `24:00` The phase change: a visible bump in the loss curve where induction heads form, coinciding with a jump in in-context learning score · `33:00` Six lines of evidence, including the co-occurrence, the ablation, and the architectural intervention that shifts the phase change · `40:00` The honest limit: strong evidence in small models, suggestive in large ones
▸ **Nail these:** the loss-curve bump is one of the most striking figures in interpretability — a mechanistic event visible in an aggregate training curve; the in-context learning score (loss at token 500 minus loss at token 50) is a simple, reusable measurement idea; induction heads generalize beyond exact repetition to fuzzy pattern completion, which is what makes them a plausible ICL mechanism; the argument is causal, not correlational, and the interventions are what earn that.

**D309 · Toy Models of Superposition** — Elhage et al., *Transformer Circuits Thread* 2022 · 50 min · L4
`0:00` Why neurons mean several things at once · `7:00` The setup: sparse features, more features than dimensions, a tiny linear-plus-ReLU model · `18:00` The result: models store more features than dimensions by accepting interference, when features are sparse · `28:00` Geometry: antipodal pairs, triangles, pentagons, and the tegum-product structures that appear · `36:00` The phase diagram over sparsity and feature importance · `42:00` Superposition as the explanation for polysemanticity ⟵ D306, and the argument that it obstructs interpretability by default
▸ **Nail these:** the compression argument — sparse features can be packed almost orthogonally, and ReLU cleans up small interference — is the whole mechanism, and the toy model makes it fully reproducible in a notebook; the discrete geometric phases are a genuinely surprising empirical finding; the strategic implication is that you need to *find* the feature basis rather than read neurons, which sets up Day 310 exactly; run the toy model live, it takes seconds.

**D310 · Towards Monosemanticity: Decomposing Language Models with Dictionary Learning** — Bricken et al., *Transformer Circuits Thread* 2023 · 50 min · L4
`0:00` Recovering interpretable features from a polysemantic model ⟵ D309 · `7:00` Sparse autoencoders: overcomplete, L1-penalized, reconstruct the activations ⟵ D54 · `18:00` Why an overcomplete basis with sparsity is the right shape for the superposition hypothesis · `26:00` Evaluating features: activating examples, logit effects, ablation, and automated interpretability scoring · `35:00` Feature splitting — more dictionary features means finer-grained concepts, not just more of them · `42:00` Dead features, shrinkage from the L1 penalty, and the reconstruction/sparsity frontier
▸ **Nail these:** this is Olshausen & Field's 1996 objective ⟵ D54 applied to a language model's activations, and naming that lineage is satisfying and true; feature splitting means there is no canonical "number of features" — the dictionary size is a resolution dial, which is conceptually important; the L1 penalty biases magnitudes downward, which later work fixes with different activation functions; run a small SAE on a toy model as the demo.

**D311 · Scaling Monosemanticity + Sparse Autoencoder Follow-ups** — Templeton et al., *Transformer Circuits Thread* 2024 · 40 min · L4
`0:00` Dictionary learning on a production model ⟵ D310 · `6:00` Millions of features, including abstract and multilingual and multimodal ones · `16:00` Feature steering: clamp a feature's activation and watch behaviour change — causal evidence, not correlation · `24:00` Safety-relevant features (deception, sycophancy, unsafe code) and the strong caution the authors attach to them · `31:00` The gap: features found ≪ features present, and the scaling cost argument · `35:00` JumpReLU, TopK, and Gated SAEs; and the 2024–2025 critiques of SAE evaluation
▸ **Nail these:** steering is what turns interpretability from description into intervention, and it's the reason this line of work matters for alignment ⟵ D208; the authors are explicit that finding a "deception feature" does not mean the model is deceptive — reproduce that caution carefully rather than sensationalizing; the completeness problem (you only find features your dictionary is big enough to hold) is the main open issue; present the SAE-skeptical results too, because this is a live research area.

**D312 · Locating and Editing Factual Associations in GPT (ROME)** — Meng, Bau, Andonian & Belinkov, *NeurIPS* 2022 (arXiv:2202.05262) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Finding where a fact lives, then changing it · `7:00` Causal tracing: corrupt the subject tokens, restore one hidden state at a time, and measure recovery — this is the method, spend time on it · `18:00` The result: mid-layer MLPs at the last subject token carry most of the causal effect ⟵ D129 · `26:00` MLPs as linear associative memory: the key–value framing of the FFN · `33:00` The rank-one update, derived, with a constraint that preserves other facts · `39:00` Generalization vs specificity, and the ripple-effect critique (edits that don't propagate to implications)
▸ **Nail these:** causal tracing is an intervention-based localization method and is far stronger evidence than attention-weight inspection — this methodological point is the episode's real content; the FFN-as-key-value-memory view (Geva et al. 2021) explains why the FFN holds ~2/3 of the parameters ⟵ D129; edits are brittle — the model may "know" the new fact directly but not its consequences, which MQuAKE and the ripple-effect papers document; model editing as an alignment tool is promising and currently unreliable.

**D313 · Probing Classifiers: What Does BERT Learn?** — Tenney, Das & Pavlick *ACL* 2019 (arXiv:1905.05950); Hewitt & Liang *EMNLP* 2019 (arXiv:1909.03368) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Asking a representation what it knows · `5:00` Edge probing: train a small classifier on frozen representations for POS, parsing, NER, coreference · `13:00` The pipeline result: BERT's layers recover the classical NLP pipeline roughly in order · `21:00` The control task: a probe with random labels, and selectivity as the metric that fixes the field's biggest methodological hole · `29:00` Why a high-accuracy probe may be reporting its own capacity, not the representation's content · `33:00` Amnesic probing and causal alternatives
▸ **Nail these:** control tasks are the fix and every probing paper published before them is suspect — teach the selectivity metric properly; a linear probe is a weaker and therefore more informative instrument than an MLP probe; probing shows *decodability*, never *use*, and the gap between those two is where causal methods ⟵ D312 earn their keep; the layer-ordering result is real but softer than the headline suggests.

**D314 · Attention is not Explanation / Attention is not not Explanation** — Jain & Wallace *NAACL* 2019 (arXiv:1902.10186); Wiegreffe & Pinter *EMNLP* 2019 (arXiv:1908.04626) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` A two-paper argument, and how to read a disagreement · `5:00` Jain & Wallace: attention weights correlate poorly with gradient-based importance, and adversarial attention distributions produce identical predictions · `15:00` Wiegreffe & Pinter's response: the adversarial distributions are found per-instance, and a model *trained* to use different attention performs worse · `24:00` The real disagreement is about what "explanation" means — plausible vs faithful, and exclusive vs non-exclusive · `30:00` What to conclude when publishing attention heatmaps · `33:00` Why this generalizes to every visualization in Month 11
▸ **Nail these:** the substantive lesson is definitional, and drawing the plausible/faithful and exclusive/non-exclusive distinctions on screen is the whole episode; a distribution existing that gives the same output is a much weaker claim than the model being indifferent to attention; attention heatmaps remain useful as hypotheses and unusable as proof; this is a great model of how scientific disagreement should be conducted, and worth saying so.

**D315 · The Lottery Ticket Hypothesis** — Frankle & Carbin, *ICLR* 2019 (arXiv:1803.03635) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` A subnetwork that trains as well as the whole thing · `6:00` Iterative magnitude pruning with *rewinding to the original initialization* — the rewind is the entire finding · `16:00` The hypothesis stated precisely: a sparse subnetwork exists that, trained in isolation from the same init, matches the dense network · `24:00` 10–20% of weights, matching accuracy, sometimes faster · `30:00` Why random reinitialization of the same mask fails — the init and mask are coupled · `36:00` Rewinding to iteration k rather than 0 for large networks (Frankle et al. 2020), and the stability analysis · `41:00` What this does and does not imply about why overparameterization helps
▸ **Nail these:** the control experiment (same mask, new random init) is what makes this a result rather than a pruning technique — lead with it; finding the ticket requires training the dense network first, so this is not yet a training-cost saving and the paper says so; late rewinding is needed at ImageNet scale, which qualifies the original claim; connect to Day 316's overparameterization puzzle and to Day 332's pruning literature.

**D316 · Deep Double Descent** — Nakkiran, Kaplan, Bansal, Yang, Barak & Sutskever, *ICLR* 2020 (arXiv:1912.02292) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` The curve that breaks the textbook ⟵ D18, D17 · `7:00` Model-wise double descent: error rises to a peak at the interpolation threshold, then falls again · `17:00` Epoch-wise double descent: the same shape over training time, at fixed model size · `26:00` Sample-wise non-monotonicity — more data making things *worse* in a regime, which is the most disturbing result · `33:00` Effective model complexity as their unifying quantity · `38:00` Label noise amplifying the peak; the implicit-bias explanation and its current status
▸ **Nail these:** the interpolation threshold (where the model can just barely fit the training data) is where the peak sits, and the classical U-curve is only the left half of the picture — draw the full curve; epoch-wise double descent means early stopping can land you exactly on the peak, which is a real practical hazard; more-data-hurts is genuine and holds in a narrow regime; this closes the loop opened on Day 17 and Day 49, so structure it as a payoff episode.

**D317 · Grokking: Generalization Beyond Overfitting on Small Algorithmic Datasets** — Power, Burda, Edwards, Babuschkin & Misra, 2022 (arXiv:2201.02177) · 40 min · L4
`0:00` Perfect training accuracy for 10,000 steps, then sudden generalization · `6:00` The setup: modular arithmetic and other small algorithmic tasks, tiny transformers · `14:00` The delay scaling with dataset size; weight decay dramatically shortening it · `22:00` The mechanistic explanation (Nanda et al. 2023): the model learns a discrete Fourier/trig algorithm for modular addition, and the circuit can be read out · `32:00` Memorization circuits then cleanup — the three-phase story · `37:00` What grokking does and does not tell us about real models
▸ **Nail these:** Nanda's progress measures showing the Fourier circuit forming *before* the test accuracy jump is the key result — generalization was continuous underneath a discontinuous metric, which is exactly Day 206's mirage argument in a different setting; weight decay is what drives the cleanup phase; the tasks are tiny and algorithmic, so resist overclaiming about frontier models; this is the best available example of interpretability answering a training-dynamics question.

**D318 · Neural Tangent Kernel** — Jacot, Gabriel & Hongler, *NeurIPS* 2018 (arXiv:1806.07572) · 50 min · L4
`0:00` A regime where deep learning is exactly a kernel method ⟵ D15, D30 · `8:00` The infinite-width limit: at initialization the network's function is a Gaussian process ⟵ D30 · `18:00` The tangent kernel Θ(x, x') = ⟨∇_θ f(x), ∇_θ f(x')⟩, and the theorem that it is constant during training in the infinite-width limit · `30:00` Gradient descent therefore becomes kernel regression, with a closed-form solution · `38:00` The lazy-training critique (Chizat & Bach): NTK networks barely move their parameters and *do not learn features* · `44:00` Why finite networks beat their NTK, and what that gap means
▸ **Nail these:** the kernel being *constant* is the whole theorem and it's what makes the analysis tractable; the fatal limitation is that feature learning is exactly the thing the NTK regime excludes, so it cannot explain transfer learning or representation quality; empirically NTK predictions underperform real networks by a consistent margin, and that margin is the interesting object; the µP / tensor-programs line (Yang & Hu) is the modern descendant and it produced hyperparameter transfer, which is practically useful.

**D319 · On Large-Batch Training and Flat Minima** — Keskar, Mudigere, Nocedal, Smelyanskiy & Tang, *ICLR* 2017 (arXiv:1609.04836) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` The generalization gap that appears when you scale your batch size · `6:00` Sharp vs flat minima, defined via the ε-sharpness metric · `15:00` The parametric plot between a small-batch and a large-batch solution — the figure everyone reproduces · `23:00` The gradient-noise argument for why small batches find flatter basins ⟵ D34 · `29:00` Dinh et al. 2017: sharpness is not reparameterization-invariant, so the definition is broken · `35:00` What survived — Goyal et al.'s linear scaling + warmup, LARS/LAMB, and SAM
▸ **Nail these:** present the original claim, the rebuttal, and the practical resolution in that order, because this is a case where the intuition outlived its own formalization; ReLU networks admit rescalings that change sharpness without changing the function, which is why the metric is not well-defined; the practical fix (scale the LR linearly with batch size, add warmup) works and is why nobody worries about this now; SAM explicitly optimizes for flatness and does improve generalization, so the intuition wasn't wrong, just under-specified.

**D320 · The Information Bottleneck and Deep Learning** — Tishby & Zaslavsky 2015 (arXiv:1503.02406); Shwartz-Ziv & Tishby 2017; Saxe et al. *ICLR* 2018 · 45 min · L4
`0:00` An ambitious theory, and a careful refutation · `6:00` The IB principle: compress X while preserving information about Y, on the information plane ⟵ D2 · `16:00` The two-phase claim: a fitting phase then a long compression phase, with compression driving generalization · `26:00` Saxe et al.'s rebuttal: the compression phase depends on saturating nonlinearities, and disappears with ReLU · `34:00` Mutual information is infinite or ill-defined for deterministic networks — the estimation problem underneath everything · `40:00` What remains useful from IB regardless of the phase claim
▸ **Nail these:** the binning estimator's choice determines the result, and that is the technical heart of the disagreement — different discretizations give different information planes; IB survives as an *objective* (used in β-VAE ⟵ D242 and variational IB) even though the descriptive claim about SGD did not replicate; this is a second worked example, after Day 314 and Day 319, of how to hold a contested theory — and by now viewers should recognize the pattern.

**D321 · Intriguing Properties of Neural Networks** — Szegedy, Zaremba, Sutskever, Bruna, Erhan, Goodfellow & Fergus, *ICLR* 2014 (arXiv:1312.6199) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` An imperceptible change that flips the label · `6:00` The box-constrained L-BFGS attack; the original perturbation images side by side · `15:00` Transferability: the same perturbation fools a different model trained on different data — the finding that made this a security problem · `24:00` The second half of the paper: random directions in activation space are as semantically meaningful as basis directions, so individual neurons aren't special ⟵ D309 · `31:00` "Blind spots" and the low-probability-pocket framing · `35:00` The counterintuitive fact that these are not rare
▸ **Nail these:** transferability is what turns a curiosity into a threat model — black-box attacks become possible without ever touching the target; the neuron-basis result is genuinely important and almost always forgotten in favor of the adversarial half, so cover both; the original explanation (pockets of low probability) turned out to be wrong, and Day 322 replaces it.

**D322 · Explaining and Harnessing Adversarial Examples (FGSM)** — Goodfellow, Shlens & Szegedy, *ICLR* 2015 (arXiv:1412.6572) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` The linear explanation, which reversed the field's intuition ⟵ D321 · `5:00` In high dimensions, many tiny aligned perturbations sum to a large activation change — do the arithmetic with n and ε · `14:00` FGSM: x + ε·sign(∇_x L), one gradient step · `21:00` Why the linear view predicts transferability, and the linear-model demonstration · `27:00` Adversarial training as a regularizer; the fast-gradient objective · `31:00` Why "more nonlinearity" is the wrong defense
▸ **Nail these:** the argument that networks are *too linear*, not too nonlinear, is the counterintuitive core and it takes two lines of algebra with an n·ε term to make obvious; FGSM is one step, cheap, and therefore the standard attack for adversarial training but a weak attack for evaluation; the RBF-network experiment supports the linearity hypothesis and is worth a minute; label leaking and gradient masking are the traps that made a decade of defenses look better than they were.

**D323 · Towards Deep Learning Models Resistant to Adversarial Attacks (PGD)** — Madry, Makelov, Schmidt, Tsipras & Vladu, *ICLR* 2018 (arXiv:1706.06083) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` The paper that made adversarial robustness a well-posed problem · `7:00` The saddle-point formulation: min over θ of max over δ — one equation that defines the field · `16:00` PGD as the inner maximization: iterated FGSM with random starts and projection ⟵ D322 · `25:00` The empirical claim that PGD finds a near-universal local maximum — their loss-landscape histograms · `32:00` Adversarial training against PGD; the capacity requirement (small models simply cannot be robust) · `38:00` The robustness/accuracy tradeoff, and the enormous training cost · `42:00` Obfuscated gradients (Athalye et al. 2018) and why PGD-AT survived when most defenses didn't
▸ **Nail these:** writing the min-max objective properly is the episode — it turns a grab-bag of attacks and defenses into an optimization problem with a defined solution; random restarts matter and single-start PGD overstates robustness; the capacity finding is important and underappreciated: robustness costs model size; Athalye's paper broke 7 of 9 ICLR 2018 defenses in one go, and that history is the reason to be skeptical of any new defense that hasn't faced adaptive attacks.

**D324 · Certified Adversarial Robustness via Randomized Smoothing** — Cohen, Rosenfeld & Kolter, *ICML* 2019 (arXiv:1902.02918) · 40 min · L4
`0:00` A robustness *guarantee*, not an empirical score ⟵ D323 · `6:00` The smoothed classifier: return the most likely class under Gaussian noise around the input · `15:00` The certification theorem: a tight L2 radius from the class probabilities, via the Neyman–Pearson lemma · `26:00` Monte Carlo estimation with a Clopper–Pearson bound, and the abstain option · `33:00` Training the base classifier on noisy inputs; σ as the radius/accuracy dial · `37:00` The limits: L2 only, expensive inference, radii that shrink with dimension
▸ **Nail these:** the difference between "no attack I tried worked" and "no attack can work within radius r" is the whole point of this episode, and it's the most important distinction in the robustness literature; certification is probabilistic (with confidence 1−α) and requires thousands of forward passes per prediction; larger σ certifies larger radii but destroys clean accuracy, and there's no free lunch; scaling certified radii to useful sizes remains open.

**D325 · Shortcut Learning and Distribution Shift** — Geirhos et al. *Nature MI* 2020 (arXiv:2004.07780); Koh et al. (WILDS) *ICML* 2021 (arXiv:2012.07421) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Models that pass the test and fail the world · `6:00` Shortcut learning defined; the Clever Hans framing and a taxonomy of examples · `16:00` Texture bias in ImageNet CNNs (Geirhos et al. 2019) — the cat-shape/elephant-texture image, which is the best single slide in this month · `24:00` WILDS: ten real distribution shifts across medicine, satellites, code, and wildlife · `32:00` The finding that ERM baselines beat most specialized robustness methods on real shifts · `36:00` i.i.d. test sets as the root methodological problem ⟵ D111
▸ **Nail these:** shortcuts are not bugs — they are solutions to the objective you actually wrote, and reframing it that way is the point; the texture-vs-shape result predicts a large fraction of CNN robustness failures; WILDS' result that fancy methods don't beat plain ERM under real shift is uncomfortable, well-supported, and exactly the kind of finding this month exists to surface; connect to ImageNetV2 ⟵ D111 and to benchmark design ⟶ D361.

**D326 · On Calibration of Modern Neural Networks** — Guo, Pleiss, Sun & Weinberger, *ICML* 2017 (arXiv:1706.04599) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Your 99%-confident model is wrong 10% of the time ⟵ D23 · `5:00` Calibration defined; reliability diagrams; Expected Calibration Error and its binning sensitivity · `14:00` The finding: modern networks are far worse calibrated than 1990s networks, despite being more accurate · `21:00` The causes they isolate — depth, width, weight decay, and batch norm ⟵ D47 · `28:00` Temperature scaling: one parameter fit on a validation set, and it beats everything more complicated · `32:00` Where calibration matters: medicine, autonomy, selective prediction, and LLM confidence
▸ **Nail these:** temperature scaling preserves accuracy exactly because it's a monotone transform of the logits — that's why it's free and why it should be your default; ECE depends on the binning scheme and can be gamed, so report reliability diagrams too; label smoothing ⟵ D66 and mixup ⟵ D112 improve calibration as a side effect; for LLMs, verbalized confidence and token probability are different things and both are poorly calibrated after RLHF ⟵ D184.

**D327 · BadNets and Data Poisoning** — Gu, Dolan-Gavitt & Garg 2017 (arXiv:1708.06733); Carlini & Terzis (poisoning multimodal) 2021 · 35 min · L3
`0:00` A model that behaves perfectly until it sees the trigger ⟵ D208 · `5:00` The threat model: an outsourced training pipeline or a poisoned public dataset · `13:00` A yellow sticker on a stop sign, and the stop-sign-to-speed-limit result · `20:00` Why standard validation cannot detect it — clean accuracy is unchanged by construction · `26:00` Transfer of backdoors through fine-tuning · `31:00` Poisoning web-scale datasets: the fact that a small, affordable fraction of a public crawl can be controlled
▸ **Nail these:** backdoors are invisible to every metric you normally look at, which makes this a supply-chain problem rather than an ML problem — that reframing is the takeaway; the split-view and frontrunning poisoning results (Carlini et al. 2023) showed real, purchasable attacks on real datasets; connect directly to Sleeper Agents ⟵ D208, which studies whether such behavior can be trained *out*; keep the episode descriptive and don't provide a recipe.

**D328 · Membership Inference and Training Data Extraction** — Shokri, Stronati, Song & Shmatikov *IEEE S&P* 2017 (arXiv:1610.05820); Carlini et al. *USENIX Security* 2021 (arXiv:2012.07805) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` What a model remembers about the people in its training set · `6:00` Membership inference: shadow models, and the attack that decides whether a record was in the training data · `16:00` Overfitting as the driver, and why the attack works even on well-generalizing models · `24:00` Extracting verbatim training data from GPT-2: generate, then rank by a membership signal · `32:00` Memorization scaling with model size, data duplication, and context length (Carlini et al. 2023) · `37:00` Deduplication as the most effective practical mitigation
▸ **Nail these:** deduplication of training data reduces memorization sharply and is the cheapest real defense — this is the actionable finding; memorization is not all-or-nothing, and eidetic memorization of rare sequences is the specific risk; the attacks succeed on models that are not obviously overfitting, which breaks the intuition that generalization implies privacy; this is the empirical grounding for the legal and policy arguments viewers will have heard about.

**D329 · Deep Learning with Differential Privacy (DP-SGD)** — Abadi, Chu, Goodfellow, McMahan, Mironov, Talwar & Zhang, *CCS* 2016 (arXiv:1607.00133) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` A mathematical guarantee about what a model can leak ⟵ D328 · `7:00` (ε, δ)-differential privacy defined over neighbouring datasets — state it precisely · `16:00` DP-SGD: clip per-example gradients to norm C, add Gaussian noise, then step · `26:00` The moments accountant: why naive composition wildly overestimates the privacy loss, and what tighter accounting buys · `34:00` Privacy amplification by subsampling · `39:00` The costs: accuracy loss, slow per-example gradients, and the batch-norm incompatibility ⟵ D47
▸ **Nail these:** per-example gradient clipping is the expensive part and it's what breaks any layer that mixes examples, which is exactly why BN is banned and group norm is used; ε is a *worst-case* bound and single-digit ε at useful accuracy is still hard for large models; the accountant is the technical contribution and it's what made DP deep learning practical at all; pretraining on public data then DP-fine-tuning is the current pragmatic recipe.

**D330 · Communication-Efficient Learning of Deep Networks from Decentralized Data (Federated Learning)** — McMahan, Moore, Ramage, Hampson & Agüera y Arcas, *AISTATS* 2017 (arXiv:1602.05629) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Training on data that never leaves the device · `6:00` FedAvg: local SGD for E epochs, then average the weights · `15:00` Why weight averaging works at all despite the non-convexity — the shared-initialization condition, and the ablation that shows it matters · `23:00` Non-IID and unbalanced client data as the defining difficulty, with their pathological-split experiments · `30:00` Communication rounds as the currency; 10–100× reduction over naive distributed SGD · `35:00` What FL does *not* give you — gradients still leak, hence secure aggregation and DP ⟵ D329
▸ **Nail these:** more local computation between communications is the lever, and E and B are the knobs — show the rounds-to-target-accuracy table; averaging models trained from *different* inits produces garbage, and their experiment demonstrating this is the conceptual heart; federated learning is not private by itself, and conflating decentralization with privacy is the most common misunderstanding — deep-leakage-from-gradients attacks are the proof.

---

# MONTH 12 — Compression, Systems, Agents, Evaluation, and the Long View
### Days 331–371 · Level 2–4 · 41 days · The part nobody teaches

Four blocks. First, making models small and fast enough to serve. Second, the computer-science papers underneath all of it — the distributed-systems classics and the frameworks. Third, agents and code. Fourth, evaluation, governance, and a capstone that turns 370 episodes of watching into one piece of work.

---

## Block A — Compression and Serving (Days 331–341)

**D331 · Distilling the Knowledge in a Neural Network** — Hinton, Vinyals & Dean, 2015 (arXiv:1503.02531) · 40 min · L2
