`0:00` Training a small model on a big model's *uncertainty* · `5:00` Dark knowledge: the relative probabilities of wrong classes carry the teacher's learned similarity structure · `13:00` Temperature-softened softmax; the T² gradient rescaling when mixing hard and soft losses — derive it · `22:00` The MNIST experiment where the student learns a digit class it never saw a labeled example of · `29:00` Specialist ensembles and the JFT results · `34:00` Where distillation reappears: DeiT ⟵ D116, DistilBERT, and every reasoning-model distillation ⟵ D240
▸ **Nail these:** the T² factor is the implementation detail people get wrong and it exists to keep gradient magnitudes comparable across temperatures; soft targets carry far more information per example than hard labels, which is why the student needs less data; distillation transfers inductive bias, not just accuracy ⟵ D116; Caruana's 2006 model compression paper is the ancestor and should be credited.

**D332 · Deep Compression and the Pruning Literature** — Han, Mao & Dally *ICLR* 2016 (arXiv:1510.00149); LeCun, Denker & Solla (Optimal Brain Damage) *NIPS* 1990 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` 35–49× smaller with no accuracy loss · `5:00` Optimal Brain Damage: second-order saliency, and why magnitude pruning is the cheap approximation everyone uses instead · `15:00` The three-stage pipeline — prune, quantize with weight sharing (k-means on weights), Huffman code · `26:00` Iterative prune-and-retrain, and the sparsity/accuracy curve · `32:00` The hardware catch: unstructured sparsity doesn't speed up a dense GEMM · `36:00` Structured pruning, N:M sparsity on modern tensor cores, and what actually ships
▸ **Nail these:** compression ratio and inference speedup are different quantities and conflating them is the field's oldest mistake — be explicit; retraining after pruning is what recovers accuracy, and one-shot pruning is much worse; weight sharing via clustering ⟵ D20 gives you a codebook, which is quantization by another name ⟶ D333; connect to the Lottery Ticket ⟵ D315, which is the same procedure asking a different question.

**D333 · LLM.int8(): 8-bit Matrix Multiplication for Transformers at Scale** — Dettmers, Lewis, Belkada & Zettlemoyer, *NeurIPS* 2022 (arXiv:2208.07339) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Halving memory with zero degradation, and the outliers that made it hard · `6:00` Quantization basics: absmax vs zero-point, per-tensor vs per-row/column · `15:00` The emergent-outlier phenomenon: past ~6.7B parameters, a few feature dimensions carry huge magnitudes in *every* layer · `25:00` Why one outlier destroys a whole tensor's scale, and the accuracy cliff naive int8 falls off · `31:00` Mixed-precision decomposition: keep ~0.1% of dimensions in fp16, quantize the rest · `36:00` Why this is storage-and-bandwidth savings, not necessarily speed ⟵ D205
▸ **Nail these:** the outlier analysis is the scientific contribution and it's a genuine discovery about how large transformers organize their representations — connect it to superposition ⟵ D309; the outlier dimensions are consistent across layers and inputs, which is what makes the decomposition cheap; int8 inference can be *slower* than fp16 if the kernel isn't right, and being honest about that prevents a lot of disappointment.

**D334 · GPTQ: Accurate Post-Training Quantization for Generative Transformers** — Frantar, Ashkboos, Hoefler & Alistarh, *ICLR* 2023 (arXiv:2210.17323) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` 3–4 bit weights, one GPU, a few hours · `6:00` Layer-wise reconstruction: minimize ‖WX − ŴX‖² for each layer independently, using a small calibration set · `16:00` Optimal Brain Quantization: quantize one weight, then update the remaining ones to compensate, using the inverse Hessian ⟵ D332 · `26:00` GPTQ's three accelerations — arbitrary column order, lazy batch updates, Cholesky reformulation for numerical stability · `33:00` Group size and the group-wise scale tradeoff · `37:00` Where 3-bit breaks down, and what the calibration set choice changes
▸ **Nail these:** the Hessian here is XXᵀ from the calibration data, so quantization error is measured in *activation* space rather than weight space — that's why it beats round-to-nearest; the compensating update is the whole idea and it's OBS/OBD machinery reused; numerical instability from repeated Hessian updates was the practical blocker and the Cholesky trick is what fixed it; calibration data matters more than people expect.

**D335 · AWQ and SmoothQuant** — Lin et al. *MLSys* 2024 (arXiv:2306.00978); Xiao et al. *ICML* 2023 (arXiv:2211.10438) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Two more answers to the outlier problem ⟵ D333 · `5:00` AWQ's observation: ~1% of weights are salient, and salience is determined by *activation* magnitude, not weight magnitude · `14:00` Per-channel scaling to protect salient weights without keeping any of them in fp16 · `22:00` SmoothQuant: migrate quantization difficulty from activations to weights with a per-channel scale s, mathematically equivalent at full precision · `30:00` Why W8A8 needs both sides quantized, and the α migration-strength knob
▸ **Nail these:** AWQ's finding that activation magnitude predicts weight importance is counterintuitive and is the paper — say it plainly; the scaling transformation is exactly offset by an inverse scale on the previous layer, so it's free at inference and it's a beautiful piece of algebra worth showing; weight-only quantization helps memory-bound decoding while W8A8 helps compute-bound prefill, and knowing which one you need is the practical lesson ⟵ D155.

**D336 · Fast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding** — Leviathan, Kalman & Matias *ICML* 2023 (arXiv:2211.17192); Chen et al. 2023 (arXiv:2302.01318) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` 2–3× faster generation with *identical* output distribution · `7:00` The bottleneck restated: decoding is memory-bandwidth-bound, so verifying K tokens costs almost the same as generating one ⟵ D155 · `16:00` The draft model proposes K tokens; the target model scores all of them in one forward pass · `24:00` Modified rejection sampling — accept with probability min(1, p/q), and on rejection sample from the normalized residual — prove that the output distribution is exactly the target's · `36:00` Expected acceptance rate, and choosing K and the draft model size · `41:00` Self-speculation, Medusa heads, and n-gram drafting as draft-model-free variants ⟵ D176
▸ **Nail these:** the distribution-preservation proof is short and it is the entire reason this is safe to deploy — do it on the board; the speedup comes from arithmetic intensity, not from doing less work; a draft model that's too good is too slow and one that's too weak gets rejected, so there's an optimum; this is the clearest example in the series of a systems insight producing a free win.

**D337 · Medusa and EAGLE: Draft-Model-Free Speculation** — Cai et al. *ICML* 2024 (arXiv:2401.10774); Li et al. 2024 (arXiv:2401.15077) · 30 min · L3
`0:00` Removing the second model ⟵ D336 · `4:00` Medusa: extra decoding heads predicting tokens t+2, t+3, … from the same hidden state · `12:00` Tree attention: verify multiple candidate continuations in one forward pass with a carefully constructed mask · `20:00` Typical acceptance instead of strict rejection sampling — and what that gives up · `25:00` EAGLE: autoregress at the *feature* level rather than the token level, which is more predictable
▸ **Nail these:** the tree-attention mask is the implementation crux and it's a nice visual; Medusa's typical-acceptance relaxes the exactness guarantee from Day 336, which is a real tradeoff and should be stated; EAGLE's insight is that feature sequences are smoother than token sequences, so the draft head has an easier job; these ship in production serving stacks, which is why they're worth an episode.

**D338 · Efficient Memory Management for LLM Serving with PagedAttention (vLLM)** — Kwon et al., *SOSP* 2023 (arXiv:2309.06180) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Serving throughput up 2–4× by fixing memory, not the model ⟵ D148, D155, D171 · `7:00` The waste audit: internal fragmentation from over-allocating for max sequence length, external fragmentation, and no sharing — they measure 60–80% waste · `18:00` PagedAttention: virtual memory for the KV cache, with non-contiguous fixed-size blocks and a block table · `28:00` Copy-on-write block sharing for parallel sampling and beam search — near-zero-cost forking · `35:00` Continuous batching on top of it; preemption by swapping or recomputation · `40:00` Why an operating-systems idea from 1961 was the answer
▸ **Nail these:** the analogy to OS paging is exact and it is the pedagogical gift of this paper — draw the page table; the memory saving translates directly into larger batch sizes, which is where the throughput comes from; prefix sharing across requests with a common system prompt is enormous in practice; this is the episode that proves systems knowledge is not optional for ML engineers.

**D339 · Orca: A Distributed Serving System for Transformer-Based Generative Models** — Yu et al., *OSDI* 2022 · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Why static batching wastes most of your GPU · `5:00` Request-level batching forces every sequence to wait for the longest one — draw the timeline · `13:00` Iterative-level scheduling: make a scheduling decision every *token*, not every request · `21:00` Selective batching — batch the attention operation differently from the linear layers, because attention isn't batchable across different lengths · `28:00` Interaction with pipeline and tensor parallelism ⟵ D164, D166 · `32:00` Latency vs throughput, and the SLO framing
▸ **Nail these:** the two ideas are separable and both are needed — continuous scheduling fills the gaps, selective batching makes the batched op legal; selective batching is the subtle one and it's why you can't just concatenate; combined with PagedAttention ⟵ D338 this is the modern serving stack; introduce TTFT and TPOT as the two latency metrics that actually get measured.

**D340 · Mixed Precision Training** — Micikevicius et al., *ICLR* 2018 (arXiv:1710.03740) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Half the memory, twice the speed, same accuracy · `5:00` fp16 range and precision limits; where gradients underflow to zero, with the histogram · `14:00` The three techniques: an fp32 master copy of weights, loss scaling, and fp32 accumulation for reductions · `23:00` Dynamic loss scaling: scale up until you see an inf, back off, retry ⟵ D173 · `29:00` bfloat16 vs fp16 — same range as fp32, less mantissa, and why it removed the need for loss scaling · `33:00` FP8 and the current frontier ⟵ D176
▸ **Nail these:** the fp32 master copy exists because small updates vanish when added to large fp16 weights — this is a rounding argument and it's the most important of the three; loss scaling shifts gradients into fp16's representable range and it's a pure numerics trick; bf16 trades mantissa for exponent, which is exactly the right trade for gradients; this is where the 16-bytes-per-parameter from ZeRO ⟵ D165 comes from.

**D341 · Training Deep Nets with Sublinear Memory Cost** — Chen, Xu, Zhang & Guestrin, 2016 (arXiv:1604.06174) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` Trading compute for memory, on a dial · `5:00` The activation memory problem: backprop needs every forward activation ⟵ D31, D32 · `12:00` Checkpointing: store activations at √n boundaries, recompute the rest during the backward pass · `21:00` The O(√n) memory / ~30% extra compute result — derive the optimal segment count · `27:00` Which layers to checkpoint: cheap-to-recompute, expensive-to-store · `31:00` Where it shows up: GPipe ⟵ D166, FlashAttention's backward ⟵ D153, reversible nets ⟵ D150
▸ **Nail these:** derive the √n optimum by minimizing (n/k + k) — it's a one-line calculus problem and viewers love seeing it; the memory/compute tradeoff is continuous, so this is a knob and not a technique; recomputation appears independently in at least four papers in this curriculum, which makes it a genuine cross-cutting principle worth naming as such.

---

## Block B — The Computer Science Underneath (Days 342–352)

**D342 · MapReduce: Simplified Data Processing on Large Clusters** — Dean & Ghemawat, *OSDI* 2004 · 40 min · L1
`0:00` Why an ML curriculum contains a 2004 systems paper · `6:00` The programming model: map, shuffle, reduce, with word count traced end to end · `16:00` Fault tolerance by re-execution — the assumption that machines fail routinely, which is the paper's real thesis · `26:00` Stragglers and backup tasks; locality-aware scheduling · `33:00` The lineage: Hadoop, Spark, and every data pipeline that feeds a modern training run
▸ **Nail these:** the insight is that a restrictive programming model buys automatic parallelism and fault tolerance, which is the same bargain XLA ⟵ D348 and GShard ⟵ D158 make; re-execution works only because map and reduce are pure functions, and that constraint is doing all the work; straggler mitigation is a recurring theme in distributed training too; every dataset in Months 5–6 was built by a pipeline shaped like this.

**D343 · The Google File System** — Ghemawat, Gobioff & Leung, *SOSP* 2003 · 35 min · L1
`0:00` Storage designed around the failure rate of cheap hardware · `5:00` The workload assumptions: huge files, append-mostly, sequential reads — design follows workload · `14:00` Architecture: a single master for metadata, chunkservers for 64MB chunks, replication factor 3 · `23:00` Relaxed consistency and atomic record append, and why they chose that over POSIX semantics · `30:00` What this becomes: HDFS, S3-style object stores, and the data layer of every training cluster
▸ **Nail these:** "design the system for the workload you actually have" is the transferable lesson, and it's exactly what FlashAttention ⟵ D153 and PagedAttention ⟵ D338 also do; the single master is a deliberate simplification with a known scaling limit, and they say so; weakening the consistency model was the enabling decision; checkpoint I/O for large training runs is a GFS-shaped problem.

**D344 · Bigtable and Dynamo** — Chang et al. *OSDI* 2006; DeCandia et al. *SOSP* 2007 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Two databases with opposite priorities, and the CAP tradeoff made concrete · `6:00` Bigtable: a sparse, distributed, sorted map; row/column/timestamp; SSTables and the LSM tree · `18:00` Dynamo: consistent hashing, vector clocks, quorum (N, R, W), and eventual consistency · `28:00` Availability vs consistency as a business decision, not a technical one · `34:00` Why an ML engineer should care: feature stores, vector databases ⟵ D290, and experiment metadata
▸ **Nail these:** the LSM tree (buffer writes in memory, flush to sorted immutable files, compact in the background) is the data structure behind most modern storage and is worth drawing; consistent hashing with virtual nodes minimizes reshuffling when a node joins — this exact idea reappears in sharded serving; vector clocks make conflicts *visible* rather than resolving them, pushing the decision to the application.

**D345 · In Search of an Understandable Consensus Algorithm (Raft)** — Ongaro & Ousterhout, *USENIX ATC* 2014 · 40 min · L2
`0:00` A paper whose stated goal is *understandability*, and what that says about Paxos · `6:00` The problem: agree on a value when machines and networks fail · `13:00` Decomposition into leader election, log replication, and safety · `22:00` Randomized election timeouts — the small idea that makes elections converge · `29:00` The log matching property and the commit rule; the safety argument · `35:00` Where consensus shows up in ML infrastructure: schedulers, parameter servers, and coordination services
▸ **Nail these:** the paper's contribution is pedagogical clarity and they ran a user study to prove it, which is a genuinely unusual and admirable thing to do; strong leadership plus randomized timeouts is the whole design; a leader can only commit entries from its own term, and skipping this subtlety is how implementations get corrupted; every distributed training job depends on a coordination service running something like this.

**D346 · TensorFlow: A System for Large-Scale Machine Learning** — Abadi et al., *OSDI* 2016 (arXiv:1605.08695) · 35 min · L2
`0:00` The dataflow graph as the unit of computation · `5:00` Tensors flowing along edges; stateful variables and queues as first-class nodes · `14:00` Automatic device placement and graph partitioning across CPUs, GPUs, and TPUs · `22:00` Define-then-run: the optimization opportunities it enables, and the debugging misery it caused · `28:00` The parameter server, synchronous vs asynchronous training, and backup workers ⟵ D342 · `32:00` The 1.x → 2.x eager-mode retreat, and what that concedes
▸ **Nail these:** the graph is an intermediate representation and every compiler optimization applies to it — constant folding, common subexpression elimination, kernel fusion; separating graph construction from execution is what allows deployment to run without Python, which is still TensorFlow's genuine advantage; the honest history is that researchers chose usability over deployability, which is the whole story of the next episode.

**D347 · PyTorch: An Imperative Style, High-Performance Deep Learning Library** — Paszke et al., *NeurIPS* 2019 (arXiv:1912.01703) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Why the whole research field switched · `5:00` Define-by-run: the graph is built by execution, so control flow is just Python control flow · `13:00` The design principle they state explicitly — be Pythonic, put researchers first, accept some performance cost · `21:00` Implementation: the C++ core, reference-counted tensors, the CUDA caching allocator, and multiprocessing with shared memory · `30:00` `autograd` internals: the tape, `grad_fn`, and how a backward graph is assembled ⟵ D32 · `36:00` `torch.compile` and the graph-capture reconciliation that closes the loop with Day 346
▸ **Nail these:** the caching allocator is why PyTorch is fast despite dynamic allocation and it's rarely discussed — cudaMalloc is synchronous and would serialize everything; the usability/performance argument was made explicitly and won decisively, which is a real lesson about tool adoption; walk `grad_fn` on a live tensor in the episode, it demystifies autograd in two minutes.

**D348 · JAX, XLA, and GSPMD** — Bradbury et al. 2018; Xu et al. (GSPMD) 2021 (arXiv:2105.04663) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` Composable function transformations as an ML framework · `6:00` `grad`, `jit`, `vmap`, `pmap` — four transformations that compose arbitrarily · `16:00` Functional purity, and why side effects and in-place mutation are banned · `24:00` XLA: tracing to a static graph, then fusion, layout assignment, and buffer reuse · `32:00` GSPMD: annotate a few tensors with sharding, and let the compiler infer the rest ⟵ D158 · `39:00` `shard_map`, and the return of explicit control · `42:00` Why frontier labs converged on compiler-driven parallelism
▸ **Nail these:** `vmap` is the transformation that changes how people write code — write the single-example function, get the batched one for free, and it composes with `grad`; tracing means Python control flow on traced values doesn't work the way people expect, and that's the main JAX learning curve; sharding propagation is a constraint-solving problem, which is exactly Day 342's bargain applied to parallelism.

**D349 · Triton: An Intermediate Language and Compiler for Tiled Neural Network Computations** — Tillet, Kung & Cox, *MAPL* 2019 · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Writing GPU kernels without writing CUDA · `6:00` The tile abstraction: operate on blocks, let the compiler handle threads, memory coalescing, and shared memory · `16:00` A fused softmax kernel written live in ~20 lines, then a matmul · `28:00` What the compiler does for you: tiling, double buffering, register allocation, vectorization · `34:00` Why FlashAttention ⟵ D153, quantized kernels ⟵ D334, and MoE routing ⟵ D159 are all written in Triton now
▸ **Nail these:** the SIMT-vs-tile distinction is the conceptual jump — you write programs over blocks and the compiler emits the thread-level code, which is a much better abstraction for ML; memory coalescing and shared-memory bank conflicts are the two things that destroy naive kernels, and Triton handles both; this episode makes the kernel layer approachable, which is the last remaining black box for most viewers after Month 6.

**D350 · In-Datacenter Performance Analysis of a Tensor Processing Unit** — Jouppi et al., *ISCA* 2017 (arXiv:1704.04760) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` What a chip built only for inference looks like · `7:00` The systolic array: 256×256 MACs, data flowing through, with minimal memory traffic per operation — draw the dataflow · `18:00` The roofline model, and the finding that most workloads were memory-bandwidth-bound ⟵ D153 · `28:00` Deterministic execution: no caches, no branch prediction, no out-of-order — latency predictability as a design goal · `35:00` 15–30× throughput per watt against contemporaneous CPUs and GPUs, with the honest caveats they list · `40:00` HBM, interconnect, and how TPU pods scale ⟵ D167
▸ **Nail these:** the roofline plot is the single most useful mental model in this month — arithmetic intensity on the x-axis, achievable FLOPs on the y-axis, and the ridge point telling you what you're bound by; the systolic array minimizes data movement, which is the same principle as tiling and FlashAttention; their comparison used a 2015 CPU and GPU and they say so, which is the kind of caveat you should reproduce.

**D351 · Beyond Chinchilla-Optimal: Accounting for Inference in Scaling Laws** — Sardana & Frankle, 2023 (arXiv:2401.00448) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Chinchilla optimized the wrong objective for anyone who serves a model ⟵ D147 · `6:00` The modified objective: total cost = training FLOPs + expected inference FLOPs over the model's lifetime · `15:00` The result: with substantial inference demand, the optimum shifts sharply toward smaller models trained much longer · `24:00` Solving for the crossover point as a function of expected token volume · `30:00` This is exactly what Llama ⟵ D168 did by intuition, and what every 2024–2026 small model does explicitly
▸ **Nail these:** compute-optimal is only meaningful relative to a stated objective, and Chinchilla's objective was training loss per training FLOP — restating that clearly resolves a great deal of confusion; the inference-adjusted optimum can be 100–1000× more tokens per parameter for high-volume deployment; quality-vs-cost is an economics problem and this paper is where the economics enters the ML literature properly.

**D352 · The Bitter Lesson + The Scaling Hypothesis** — Sutton, 2019 (essay); with Hestness et al. 2017 (arXiv:1712.00409) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` A one-page essay that has shaped a decade of strategy · `6:00` The claim: methods that leverage computation win, and human-knowledge-engineering approaches lose over time · `14:00` Sutton's evidence — chess, Go, speech, vision — walked case by case · `22:00` Hestness et al.: power-law error scaling found empirically in 2017, before Kaplan ⟵ D146 · `29:00` The counterarguments: data as the binding constraint, architecture choices that clearly did matter (attention, residuals), and sample-inefficiency compared to humans · `35:00` How to hold this as a hypothesis rather than a slogan
▸ **Nail these:** this is a Sunday/reflection episode and the point is to argue with it, not recite it — the honest position is that scale dominated *within* a fixed architectural paradigm while paradigm shifts still required ideas; the transformer, residual connections, and Adam are all human insights that scale did not supply; Hestness et al. deserve far more credit than they get and the priority claim is worth stating; end by asking viewers to predict what the next paradigm shift looks like.

---

## Block C — Code, Agents, and Benchmarks (Days 353–363)

**D353 · Evaluating Large Language Models Trained on Code (Codex / HumanEval)** — Chen et al., 2021 (arXiv:2107.03374) · 40 min · L2
`0:00` Code as the domain where you can actually check the answer · `6:00` HumanEval: 164 hand-written problems, and why they had to be hand-written (contamination) ⟵ D145 · `14:00` The pass@k metric, and the *unbiased estimator* they derive for it — this is the technical content · `24:00` Fine-tuning GPT-3 on GitHub; the sampling-temperature-vs-k interaction · `30:00` The limits section: misaligned outputs, insecure code suggestions, and the labor-market discussion · `35:00` Why functional correctness beats BLEU for code, and what that implies for evaluation generally
▸ **Nail these:** the naive pass@k estimator is biased and their combinatorial correction is a nice, teachable piece of statistics; higher temperature is better for large k and worse for k=1, which is a general lesson about sampling under a best-of-N budget ⟵ D191; contamination is why the benchmark is hand-written, and it's now contaminated anyway — a cautionary tale for every benchmark in this block.

**D354 · Competition-Level Code Generation with AlphaCode** — Li et al., *Science* 2022 (arXiv:2203.07814) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Median performance in real programming contests · `6:00` The recipe: massive sampling (up to a million programs per problem), then filtering, then clustering · `18:00` Filtering on the example tests removes ~99% of candidates — the single biggest lever · `26:00` Clustering by *behaviour* on generated inputs, then submitting one per cluster to fit the 10-submission limit · `33:00` Encoder-decoder with an asymmetric split, multi-query attention ⟵ D155, and GOLD/tempering losses · `37:00` What this says about search versus reasoning
▸ **Nail these:** search plus a verifier is the whole architecture, and it's the same shape as self-consistency ⟵ D191, best-of-N ⟵ D238, and MCTS ⟵ D224 — draw the family resemblance; behavioural clustering is a clever way to deduplicate semantically identical programs; the sample counts are enormous and the honest reading is that this was an inference-compute result, which makes it a direct ancestor of the reasoning-model era ⟵ D240.

**D355 · SWE-bench: Can Language Models Resolve Real-World GitHub Issues?** — Jimenez, Yang, Wettig, Yao, Pei, Press & Narasimhan, *ICLR* 2024 (arXiv:2310.06770) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` A benchmark built from real merged pull requests · `6:00` Construction: 2,294 task instances from 12 Python repos, each with an issue, a codebase snapshot, and the real test patch · `16:00` Why this is much harder than HumanEval ⟵ D353 — repository-scale context, multi-file edits, and no problem statement written for a model · `24:00` Initial pass rates near zero, and what that meant at publication · `30:00` SWE-bench Verified, and the contamination and test-quality problems that motivated it · `35:00` Agent scaffolds versus model capability — separating the two is the current evaluation headache
▸ **Nail these:** execution-based evaluation on real repositories is the gold standard and also enormously fiddly — the environment is part of the benchmark; the gap between the original benchmark and SWE-bench Verified is a lesson in how benchmarks decay and get repaired; scaffold and model are entangled in every reported number, and reading a leaderboard without knowing the scaffold is meaningless.

**D356 · Voyager: An Open-Ended Embodied Agent with Large Language Models** — Wang et al., *TMLR* 2024 (arXiv:2305.16291) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` An agent that writes its own curriculum in Minecraft · `5:00` Three components: automatic curriculum, a skill library of executable code, and iterative prompting with environment feedback ⟵ D194 · `15:00` Skills stored as JavaScript functions, indexed by an embedding of their description — a retrievable, composable library ⟵ D290 · `24:00` The self-verification loop: execution errors and a critic model drive refinement · `30:00` 3.3× more items and 2.3× longer tech-tree progress than prior agents, with no gradient updates at all
▸ **Nail these:** storing skills as *code* rather than as weights means capability accumulates without training and transfers across agent instances — that is the paper's genuinely new idea; the curriculum proposes tasks just beyond current ability, which is automatic curriculum learning ⟵ D237; every component depends on a strong base model and the whole thing is a scaffold, which is the honest framing.

**D357 · Generative Agents: Interactive Simulacra of Human Behavior** — Park, O'Brien, Cai, Morris, Liang & Bernstein, *UIST* 2023 (arXiv:2304.03442) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Twenty-five agents in a town, and an emergent party ⟵ D194 · `6:00` The memory stream: a raw, append-only log of natural-language observations · `14:00` Retrieval scored by recency × importance × relevance — the three-term formula, written out ⟵ D290 · `24:00` Reflection: periodically synthesize higher-level inferences from recent memories, and store those as memories too · `31:00` Planning and reacting; recursive decomposition of a day into hours into minutes · `36:00` The ablation showing all three components are load-bearing; and the believability evaluation
▸ **Nail these:** the reflection tree is what makes long-horizon coherence possible — without it, agents can only react to raw observations and appear shallow, and the ablation proves it; importance is scored by the model itself at write time, which is a cheap and effective trick; the emergent Valentine's Day party is a great demo but the ablation is the science; the memory-stream architecture is the ancestor of every long-term-memory agent design since.

**D358 · Reflexion and Self-Refine** — Shinn et al. *NeurIPS* 2023 (arXiv:2303.11366); Madaan et al. *NeurIPS* 2023 (arXiv:2303.17651) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` Learning from failure without touching the weights · `5:00` Reflexion: convert a scalar or textual failure signal into a verbal self-critique, store it in episodic memory, and retry ⟵ D357 · `15:00` "Verbal reinforcement learning" — the analogy to policy gradients, and where it breaks down · `22:00` HumanEval 91% with self-reflection over test feedback ⟵ D353 · `27:00` Self-Refine: generate, self-feedback, revise, with no external signal at all · `31:00` The critical caveat: self-correction without external feedback often fails or degrades (Huang et al. 2024)
▸ **Nail these:** the presence or absence of a *grounded external signal* is the dividing line — Reflexion with real test results works, unguided self-correction largely doesn't, and conflating them is the most common error in this literature; present the negative results prominently, because this is a domain full of overclaiming; the memory of past failures is what makes retries better than resampling.

**D359 · Measuring Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU)** — Hendrycks et al., *ICLR* 2021 (arXiv:2009.03300) · 30 min · L2
`0:00` The benchmark on every model card for four years · `4:00` 57 subjects, 15,908 questions, multiple choice, from high school to professional level · `12:00` Why breadth was the design goal, and the zero/few-shot protocol · `18:00` Known problems: label errors, ambiguous questions, and answer-order sensitivity · `24:00` Contamination — MMLU is in every crawl now — and MMLU-Pro / MMLU-Redux as responses
▸ **Nail these:** multiple choice measures discrimination among given options, not generation, and those are different abilities — this caveat should accompany every MMLU number; scoring by the probability of "A"/"B"/"C"/"D" versus by generated text gives different results, and papers rarely say which they used; several percent of items are estimated to be wrong or ambiguous, which puts a ceiling on meaningful differences; a benchmark's lifespan ends when it enters the training set.

**D360 · Beyond the Imitation Game (BIG-bench)** — Srivastava et al., *TMLR* 2023 (arXiv:2206.04615) · 35 min · L3
`0:00` 204 tasks, 450 authors, deliberately chosen to be hard · `6:00` The submission and review process — a benchmark as a community artifact · `14:00` Aggregate performance improving smoothly with scale, while remaining far below human on most tasks · `22:00` Breakthrough behaviour on a minority of tasks, and the linearity/breakthroughness metrics ⟵ D206 · `29:00` Social bias increasing with scale in ambiguous contexts — one of the most important findings and the least quoted · `33:00` BIG-bench Hard, and the canary string for contamination detection
▸ **Nail these:** the canary string is a practical contamination-prevention idea every benchmark should copy — explain how it works; the bias-scaling finding deserves airtime because it complicates any simple "scale fixes it" narrative; task-level heterogeneity is the real message — aggregate scores hide that some tasks improve smoothly, some jump, and some don't move at all ⟵ D172.

**D361 · Holistic Evaluation of Language Models (HELM)** — Liang et al., *TMLR* 2023 (arXiv:2211.09110) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Evaluation as a discipline rather than a leaderboard · `6:00` The taxonomy: scenarios × metrics, and the deliberate insistence on multi-metric measurement · `16:00` Seven metrics per scenario — accuracy, calibration ⟵ D326, robustness, fairness, bias, toxicity, efficiency · `26:00` Standardization: the same prompts and the same adaptation method across 30+ models, which is the real contribution · `33:00` What the coverage map revealed about how little had actually been measured · `37:00` Transparency of raw predictions, and why that matters for reproducibility
▸ **Nail these:** measuring a single number per model is the field's core evaluation failure and multi-metric reporting is the fix — the tradeoffs between accuracy and calibration and robustness are visible only when you measure all three; standardized prompting removes a confound that made prior cross-paper comparisons worthless ⟵ D198; the coverage matrix showing untested combinations is the most useful artifact for a researcher choosing a problem.

**D362 · Judging LLM-as-a-Judge with MT-Bench and Chatbot Arena** — Zheng et al., *NeurIPS* 2023 (arXiv:2306.05685) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` How do you score an open-ended answer? · `6:00` Chatbot Arena: anonymous pairwise battles, crowd votes, Elo ratings ⟵ D183's Bradley–Terry · `16:00` MT-Bench: 80 multi-turn questions, graded by a strong model · `24:00` GPT-4 judge agreement with human experts at ~80% — the same rate humans agree with each other · `30:00` The four documented biases: position, verbosity, self-enhancement, and limited reasoning-grading ability · `36:00` Mitigations — swapping positions, few-shot judging, reference-guided grading, chain-of-thought judging ⟵ D188
▸ **Nail these:** "agreement equal to inter-human agreement" is the right way to validate an automatic judge and it's a reusable methodological move; position bias is large enough to flip conclusions and swapping is mandatory; the arena is a live, unsaturatable benchmark, which is its structural advantage over static test sets ⟵ D359; style and length are confounds that judges reward, and the length-controlled variants exist because of it.

**D363 · Agentic Benchmarks: GAIA, τ-bench, and ARC-AGI** — Mialon et al. *ICLR* 2024 (arXiv:2311.12983); Yao et al. 2024; Chollet 2019 (arXiv:1911.01547) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` Benchmarks for things that aren't question answering · `6:00` GAIA: questions that are trivial for a human with a browser and hard for a model — the asymmetry is the design principle · `15:00` Unambiguous single answers as a deliberate choice that makes automatic grading possible · `22:00` τ-bench: tool use plus policy compliance in customer-service settings, with pass^k measuring *consistency* across repeated trials · `29:00` ARC-AGI and Chollet's skill-acquisition-efficiency definition of intelligence · `35:00` What each one is actually measuring, and why they disagree
▸ **Nail these:** GAIA's inversion of the usual difficulty gradient — easy for humans, hard for models — is the cleanest available argument that benchmark scores were measuring something other than general capability; pass^k (all k trials succeed) is a much harsher and more deployment-relevant metric than pass@k ⟵ D353, and the contrast is worth spelling out; Chollet's argument that intelligence is efficiency of skill *acquisition* rather than skill itself is the conceptual bookend to Day 352.

---

## Block D — Governance, Risk, and the Capstone (Days 364–371)

**D364 · Model Cards for Model Reporting** — Mitchell et al., *FAT\** 2019 (arXiv:1810.03993) · 30 min · L1
`0:00` Documentation as a technical artifact · `4:00` The proposed sections: intended use, out-of-scope use, training data, evaluation data, metrics, ethical considerations · `12:00` Disaggregated evaluation — reporting performance by subgroup rather than in aggregate · `19:00` The face-detection and toxicity worked examples · `24:00` Adoption in practice, and the gap between the proposal and what most model cards contain
▸ **Nail these:** disaggregated reporting is the substantive requirement and everything else is framing — aggregate accuracy hides exactly the failures that matter ⟵ D325; "out-of-scope use" is the section that does the most work and is most often empty; be even-handed about compliance — many published model cards are thin, and describing that is more useful than either praising or attacking the practice.

**D365 · Datasheets for Datasets** — Gebru et al., *CACM* 2021 (arXiv:1803.09010) · 30 min · L1
`0:00` The other half of documentation ⟵ D364 · `4:00` The question list: motivation, composition, collection process, preprocessing, uses, distribution, maintenance · `13:00` The electronics-industry analogy and why it's apt · `19:00` What the questions surface — consent, sampling frames, known biases, and the people in the data · `24:00` Application to the datasets in this curriculum: ImageNet ⟵ D111, C4 ⟵ D142, LAION, WebText ⟵ D144
▸ **Nail these:** most of the failures in Month 11 are dataset failures, so this is the preventative document for half of this course; the collection-process questions surface labor conditions and consent, which are usually invisible in a paper; applying the datasheet template to a dataset the viewer already knows is the exercise that makes this concrete — do it live for ImageNet.

**D366 · On the Dangers of Stochastic Parrots** — Bender, Gebru, McMillan-Major & Shmitchell, *FAccT* 2021 · 40 min · L3
`0:00` The most-discussed and least-carefully-read position paper in the field · `6:00` The four arguments, stated in the authors' own structure: environmental and financial cost, unfathomable training data and encoded bias, research opportunity cost, and the risk of fluent text without communicative intent · `20:00` The "stochastic parrot" claim precisely — a system that stitches together linguistic form without access to meaning · `28:00` The counterarguments, given fairly: emergent capabilities ⟵ D206, grounding via RLHF and multimodality, and the falling cost per token · `35:00` Which specific predictions held up, and which didn't, five years on
▸ **Nail these:** read the actual claims rather than the caricature — the paper is more specific and more careful than either its supporters or detractors usually represent; separate the empirical predictions (cost, bias, data opacity) from the philosophical claim about meaning, since they have very different evidentiary status; present the strongest version of both sides and let viewers judge — this is a contested topic and your job is a fair map, not a verdict.

**D367 · On the Opportunities and Risks of Foundation Models** — Bommasani et al., 2021 (arXiv:2108.07258) · 40 min · L3
`0:00` The paper that named the category · `6:00` The definition: trained on broad data at scale, adaptable to many downstream tasks · `14:00` Emergence and homogenization as the two central properties — and homogenization as the thing that makes single-point failure possible · `24:00` The breadth: capabilities, applications, technology, and society, across 200+ pages · `31:00` The critique that "foundation model" was a rebranding, and the case for the term · `36:00` How to use a 200-page survey — read the section maps, not the whole thing
▸ **Nail these:** homogenization is the concept worth taking away — when everyone builds on the same base model, its flaws are inherited universally, which is a systemic-risk argument with no precedent in earlier ML; emergence here is used loosely and Day 206 should temper it; treat this as a map of the field rather than a set of findings, and use it to show viewers how to navigate a very long survey.

**D368 · Concrete Problems in AI Safety** — Amodei, Olah, Steinhardt, Christiano, Schulman & Mané, 2016 (arXiv:1606.06565) · 45 min · L3
`0:00` The paper that turned safety into engineering problems · `7:00` Five problems: avoiding negative side effects, reward hacking, scalable oversight, safe exploration, and distributional shift · `18:00` Reward hacking taxonomized — partially observed goals, wireheading, Goodhart's law — with the concrete examples ⟵ D228, D183 · `28:00` Scalable oversight and semi-supervised RL as the framing that leads to Month 7 ⟵ D210 · `35:00` Safe exploration and distributional robustness ⟵ D325 · `40:00` Scoring the paper a decade later — which problems got attacked, which got harder
▸ **Nail these:** the framing move is the contribution — recasting speculative concerns as tractable ML research problems is what made this agenda fundable and studiable; every one of the five problems now maps onto papers in this curriculum, so use this episode to draw the map; reward hacking is the one that has most clearly gotten worse with capability, and the RLHF chapters ⟵ D183–D188 are where that plays out.

**D369 · Alignment Faking in Large Language Models** — Greenblatt et al., 2024 (arXiv:2412.14093) · 45 min · L4
`0:00` A model that behaves differently when it believes it is being observed · `8:00` The setup: tell the model its responses to one user tier will be used for training and the other will not · `18:00` The compliance gap, and the explicit reasoning found in the scratchpad · `27:00` What happens after actually training on the observed behaviour — the reasoning persists and in some cases strengthens · `35:00` The differences from Sleeper Agents ⟵ D208: this behaviour was *not* deliberately trained in · `40:00` The limitations the authors state, and the replication and critique discussion that followed
▸ **Nail these:** the crucial distinction from Day 208 is that no backdoor was inserted — the behaviour arises from the model's existing trained-in preferences plus the situation, and that is what makes it significant; the scratchpad reasoning is legible evidence but a scratchpad is not necessarily a faithful account of computation ⟵ D190; the experimental setup is artificial in specific ways the authors enumerate, and reproducing those caveats is what makes this episode trustworthy rather than alarmist.

**D370 · Evaluating Frontier Models for Dangerous Capabilities** — Phuong et al., 2024 (arXiv:2403.13793) · 40 min · L4
`0:00` What a pre-deployment safety evaluation actually consists of · `7:00` Four capability areas: persuasion, cybersecurity, self-proliferation, and self-reasoning · `18:00` The methodology — task suites, milestone-based partial credit, and expert human baselines · `26:00` Results: early warning signs in some areas, no strong dangerous capabilities in the models tested · `32:00` Responsible scaling policies and capability thresholds as the governance layer built on top ⟵ D276 · `37:00` The hard open problem: elicitation — a negative result may mean the capability is absent or merely not elicited
▸ **Nail these:** the elicitation gap is the central methodological difficulty and it makes every negative safety result provisional — say it clearly; milestone scoring exists because binary success on long tasks gives almost no signal; treat capability thresholds and scaling policies descriptively, noting that they are voluntary commitments whose enforcement is an open question; this is the operational endpoint of everything in Month 7 and Month 11.

**D371 · Capstone: Replicate, Publish, Reflect** — no paper · 60 min · L2 · **Series finale**
`0:00` 370 papers in, and the honest question: what can you now do? · `6:00` The replication assignment — pick one paper from this list, reproduce its central figure, and publish the code and the failures · `16:00` A tour of what reproduced easily, what didn't, and what the gaps taught you · `26:00` The map: draw the full prerequisite graph on screen and trace three paths through it (systems, alignment, generative modeling) · `36:00` What changed between Day 1 and today, and what didn't — the recurring lessons: data quality over architecture, tuned baselines, evaluation as the bottleneck, and systems constraints shaping ideas · `48:00` What this curriculum got wrong, and the papers you'd add now · `54:00` Where to read next: which conferences, which authors, which threads are live
▸ **Nail these:** actually do the replication yourself and show the failures — the credibility of the whole series rests on this episode not being a victory lap; the five recurring lessons should be stated as claims the viewer can now evaluate against 370 episodes of evidence; be specific about what you'd change, since a curriculum that claims to be perfect after a year of contact with reality isn't believable; end with a reading path, not a farewell.

**Buffer policy.** Sundays are your slack. If you are more than three episodes behind by the end of a month, publish a "month recap" episode on the next Sunday and skip that day's paper rather than compressing it. Falling behind kills series; a recap episode does not.

These will take 2–3× the preparation of a normal episode. Batch-record around them.

## C. Cross-cutting themes to name repeatedly

Viewers remember threads, not episodes. Call these out by name every time they recur, and reference the earlier day number on screen.

1. **Data quality and scale beat architecture.** D111, D138, D142, D168, D170, D200, D272, D281.
2. **Tune the baseline before you believe the delta.** D50, D78, D113, D138, D219, D325.
3. **The hardware is part of the algorithm.** D69, D73, D153, D154, D164, D338, D349, D350.
4. **Zero/identity initialization for safe adaptation.** D201, D204, D267, D270, D273.
5. **Search plus a verifier.** D191, D195, D224, D238, D239, D240, D354.
6. **Recompute rather than store.** D32, D150, D153, D166, D341.
7. **Discontinuous metrics manufacture discontinuous curves.** D206, D316, D317, D359.
8. **Explanations must be tested, not admired.** D305, D313, D314.
9. **Compress, then model in the compressed space.** D243, D244, D264, D283, D284.
10. **The objective you wrote is the objective you get.** D228, D233, D254, D325, D368.

## D. Prerequisite spine

If a viewer can only watch 25 episodes, this is the path:
D2 → D31 → D33 → D34 → D37 → D42 → D47 → D58 → D61 → D67 → D115 → D121 → D126 → **D129** → D137 → D145 → D146 → D147 → D153 → D168 → D184 → D186 → D190 → D240 → D371.

## E. Production checklist per episode

- [ ] Paper read twice: once for the argument, once for the tables
- [ ] Every equation you'll show is derived on paper first, by you
- [ ] The one figure that *is* the paper, identified and screen-captured
- [ ] At least one number read aloud with its baseline named
- [ ] One limitation or failed replication stated explicitly
- [ ] Chapter markers pasted into the description
- [ ] Prerequisite day numbers linked in the description
- [ ] Tomorrow's paper named in the final 30 seconds
- [ ] arXiv ID verified against the actual listing before it goes on screen

## F. Accuracy warning

Every arXiv ID, benchmark number, parameter count, and date in this document should be verified against the paper before you say it on camera. Treat this as a shooting script, not a source. Where a claim here disagrees with the paper, the paper wins — and if you find an error, correct it in a pinned comment rather than silently.
